## Translator’s Note

Throughout this book, Rossi uses **“Arab”** in a much broader historical sense than the word normally carries today. He applies it not only to Arabic-speaking peoples and societies, but to a far wider cultural world stretching across Egypt, Mesopotamia, the Levant, and neighbouring regions, including periods long before the rise of Islam. This translation preserves that usage because it is central to Rossi’s argument. Unless the context clearly points to the narrower modern meaning, **“Arab” should therefore be understood in Rossi’s own expansive sense.**

# PREFACE

*A narrow view of history has taught us to look for its origins close to home, on the barren Greek peninsula and the poor banks of the Tiber. Europeans readily trace the beginnings of their culture back no farther than Athens and Rome. That view is mistaken. It grew out of religious and political bias. For more than a thousand years, historians of the Roman Catholic Church had virtually exclusive control over the surviving records of antiquity, and there is little doubt that they shaped their interpretation to glorify the European West.*

*Yet the history of both East and West unfolded under the blazing light of Asia, **mater gentium** — mother of nations — and beneath the skies of the Nile. Egypt and Babylonia gathered together the powerful currents from which the great Arab civilization emerged. Since the dawn of history, that civilization has carried its art of living across the lands between the Indus and the Tagus, the Blue Nile and the Baltic. Athens and Rome were only reflections of it. By restoring Asia and the Arab world to their proper place, and by honestly recognizing their role in creating the culture we now call our own, we hope to recover — beyond the familiar gateways of Athens and Rome — the deeper ties that bind Europe to a vastly larger world, a world within which the shape of Europe’s future may become easier to see. Europe is neither the centre of the world nor the mirror of absolute Good. A daughter of the Afro-Asian East, it is only one region in an ocean of space and time, carried along by forces far larger than itself — just as the Caesars once were.*

*But prejudice remains. Dogmatic teaching still distorts our judgment, and the images lodged in our minds have taken the place of evidence. We imagine that the history taught in our schools is the same history taught in Cairo, Tehran, Kabul, or Calcutta. It is not. Yet on the strength of this illusion, we have claimed the right to rule over non-Western nations. Once we learn to give the worlds around us their true weight, perhaps we will discover not only genuine kinship, but also both the reach and the limits of our own world. The spell of words, pride in ourselves, the urge to place ourselves above others: once we say “the West,” we think we have said everything. As though the West were anything more than the sunset side of the East…*

*As for the Arab world — and it truly is a world — we have shrunk its borders to a few acres of barren desert where scraps of old myths drift in the air. We have belittled it, caricatured it, almost buried it altogether. Yet now it is returning to the living world. It is time to recognize that if our West is pleasant, prosperous, beautiful, and still orderly, it owes much of that to the great Arab empires that created the conditions for such good fortune. We are like Omar Khayyam’s poppy, drawing its crimson from the blood of a buried emperor.*

*Paris, May 1976.*

# I

## FROM THE PYRAMIDS TO THE MEDICI CHAPEL

In Florence’s Medici Chapel, Michelangelo turned the four moments of time into four monumental figures: Dawn and Dusk, Day and Night — Isis and Osiris, Apollo and Persephone. The whole work is an attempt to transform our fear of time into the beauty of space, and to draw the mind toward something eternal. The chapel creates a space for thinking about human destiny within the movement of the universe: about death overcome, resurrection promised and already present, and an unchanging perfection embodied in marble.

The stone face of Giuliano de’ Medici seems lost forever in troubled reflection on the contradictions of this world. Art, philosophy, and religion become one. The strict geometry of three dimensions is used to suggest a fourth: the invisible world revealed through the visible.

Anyone who enters the chapel alone can feel it. In the silence and perfect geometry of the tomb and its statues, there is the suggestion of something beyond appearances, something that cannot be touched or violated. The same visitor might feel much the same before a temple at Memphis or the great colonnade of Baalbek beneath the open sky and desert. The pyramids, the colossal figures of Nineveh, the Dome of the Rock, and the Medici Chapel all belong to the same mental landscape. They deny that the fleeting moment has any final importance. They present art as a movement of the whole earth toward the sky.

For the Medici Chapel — one of the jewels of Western art — to become possible, many civilizations first had to endure, mingle, and build on one another: Egypt and Chaldea, Palestine and Anatolia, Yemen and the Black Sea, Greece and finally Rome. Together they formed the great cultural forces of what I will call the Arab world. It was this world that gave the Western mind its Eastern sense of proportion. Without it, the West could never have found its own form or achieved its characteristic harmony.

When I speak of an “Arab world” in this very broad sense, I know that I am challenging a deeply rooted idea: the picture of the Arab as essentially a desert people who suddenly appeared on the stage of history at some uncertain date.

“The beginnings of Arab history,” the *Encyclopaedia of Islam* solemnly tells us, “are still very obscure; we know neither where the Arabs came from nor what their earliest way of life was.”

One thing, however, seems certain to the author of the article: they were, and remain, “Semites.” And with that, the great explanation is supposed to have been given. In reality, the explanation tells us nothing. So little, in fact, that the same *Encyclopaedia of Islam* did not even dare to include “Semites” as a heading in its table of contents.

It hardly needs saying that the word *Semite* appears neither in Greek vocabulary nor in Latin. That alone should give us pause. The term is nowhere to be found before the end of the eighteenth century. It was coined by the German scholar A. L. Schlözer in a work published in 1781 under the title *Repertory of Biblical and Oriental Literature* — as though biblical literature were somehow not Oriental. The very fact that Schlözer felt the need to draw such a boundary should make us suspicious.

We are nevertheless told, with great confidence, that the division of humanity into East and West is one of the keys to history, and that this geographical division corresponds to a second division, both ethnic and linguistic: Indo-Europeans, sometimes called Aryans, on one side, and Semites on the other. Generation after generation of respectable scholars has bowed before this invention of German philologists.

Future historians may well be astonished that such credulity and conformity could triumph in an age like ours, which prides itself on being sceptical, rational, and rebellious. The surviving documents simply do not allow us to prove the existence of distinct “Semitic” and “Aryan” peoples, still less to draw firm boundaries around them or assign each group its own fixed characteristics. The same is true of the theory that East and West can be defined and separated through Indo-European and Semitic languages. Given what we actually know, we have no right to treat such ideas as established facts.

The words *Semite* and *Aryan*, by themselves, mean nothing. For them to have real historical meaning, peoples would first have had to claim these identities for themselves and recognize themselves as Aryan or Semitic. Yet no people, culture, or society in antiquity ever defined its destiny in those terms.

That needs to be said plainly.

Our world, however, has become so theoretical that we are comfortable living inside categories invented for us by intellectuals. The apparent universality of their theories, the solidarity — not to say complicity — that binds them together, and all the academic authority with which they surround themselves give their words enormous power over public opinion. As Erasmus wrote, “man is so made that fictions impress him more than truth.”

But the truth has nothing to do with this naive, or perhaps suspicious, division between “Aryans” and “Semites.” Of the two terms, the first is simply an invention, while the second is a modern word derived from Shem, son of Noah — a thoroughly mythical figure. Indeed, if we wanted to remain faithful to biblical tradition, we ought to speak of “Japhetites,” not Aryans, because Japheth, one of Noah’s three sons, was supposed to have fathered the Greeks, the Anatolians, and our European ancestors. How casually our scientific establishment ventured into such uncertain territory.

Speaking elegantly is not enough. One must also speak accurately. Some scholars became so eager to create words that, in the end, they became creatures of the words they had invented. Not everyone joined the chorus. Here and there, dissenting voices were heard. Established theories were sometimes attacked sharply for their extravagant claims. But universities are institutions that protect the faithful and can be merciless toward dissenters. Criticism fell silent when it was not silenced outright.

Many professors and commentators, afraid of offending the masters under whom they had studied, chose to fall into line. They found themselves teaching ideas they no longer believed and preserving an illusion that was no longer their own — unwilling falsifiers, in effect.

The Arabs themselves also bear some responsibility. Impressed by what they imagined to be the infallibility of Western scholarship, they accepted definitions of themselves handed down by outsiders. They swallowed the reckless judgments of our Orientalists without asking how Europeans themselves would have reacted if schools of “Occidentalists” in Cairo or Baghdad had begun issuing equally confident theories about Europe.

We have Orientalists.

Cairo has no Occidentalists.

That absence is significant, and the imbalance is hardly innocent.

In the tenth century, reflecting on the unfathomable secrets of history, the Egyptian saint Niffari wrote:

“The surface of the sea is a brightness that blinds us with its reflections; the depths of the sea are an impenetrable darkness. Between them swim great fish, and they are to be feared.”

The errors of scholarship can be more dangerous than the errors of ignorance. God knows how many bad causes have been sheltered beneath Noah’s cloak by the supposed distinction between Semites and Indo-Europeans. And the distinction is especially arbitrary because one of the enduring features of Eastern history is precisely the absence of firm ethnic or religious boundaries. Alongside that fact stands another: East and West are not opposing worlds. They are the two poles of the same truth, the same culture, the same history.

The Greeks were Easterners. The Romans claimed descent from Aeneas. Languages, sounds, and writing systems are so deeply entangled that attempts to separate them cleanly quickly become impossible. Our mistake was to begin the exercise from assumptions derived, more or less openly, from pseudo-Christian teaching and from a partial knowledge of ancient literature.

We took the immense accumulation of culture around the Mediterranean since the fourth or fifth millennium BCE and compressed it into three or four narrow episodes. We turned a dizzying past into a few crude diagrams tailored to our own tastes — and, by extension, to our own prejudices. Before admitting that many events of antiquity have simply become difficult for us to understand, and that problems of meaning and language remain almost impossible to overcome, we rushed to firm conclusions without having acquired anything resembling certainty.

There is another, even more dangerous habit. We entrusted the study of vast ancient societies to a small circle of scholars shut away in monasteries, universities, and laboratories. Yet those societies were fundamentally collective, communal, anonymous, and religious. Their way of life had very little in common with the rare and highly specialized individuals studying them.

A recent book by J.-P. Adam, head of the Bureau of Ancient Architecture in Paris, published by Laffont under the title *Archaeology Confronts Fraud*, denounces the misleading and purely hypothetical character of some supposed knowledge about the past. The author is astonished at the extent to which common sense has been ignored. Yet even he has not dared to follow his own reasoning all the way to its conclusion.

How can one avoid smiling when Ernest Renan writes in his *History of the Semitic Languages* that, “by a special grace,” Aryans and Semites never passed through a “savage state” but somehow emerged fully formed at the highest level of culture? Like Minerva springing fully armed from the head of Jupiter. How seriously can we take statements such as: “The Assyrians were certainly Semites; as for the Chaldeans, it is impossible to know who they were or where they came from,” immediately followed by the claim that “Chaldeans and Assyrians rapidly formed a common civilization, known as Assyro-Babylonian”?

Where, then, within this shared civilization does the Semitic part end and the non-Semitic part begin?

Let us continue.

“For some, the Hittites were Semites; for others, Mongols; for still others, Mongol-Semites.”

As for the Egyptians, some writers make them Ethiopians, others half-Semites mixed with Hamites or “white Africans.” They “must have invaded the Nile valley at so remote a date that the imagination cannot calculate it.” Some have even wondered whether they came from Oceania — without troubling to say from which ocean. The Egyptians themselves had a rather simpler idea. In the inscriptions at Karnak they called themselves descendants of the *Shesou-Hor*, the “followers of Horus” — in other words, descendants of Egyptians.

At least that is an honest tautology.

Then we are told: “It is now established that central Arabia was the birthplace of the Semites. The Semites who remained in Arabia became the ancestors of the Arab people. Those who settled along the lower Euphrates and later spread throughout Western Asia became the Assyrians and the Israelites.” This explanation manages to confuse Chaldeans, Assyrians, and Israelites while also assuming that Western Asia was empty before they arrived.

If it was not empty, who lived there?

On that question, silence.

According to Flavius Josephus, so often quoted by our biblical scholars, the Syrians, Egyptians, Lebanese, and Ethiopian Jews are not Semites.

The Persians, however, are.

Our scholars then tell us that the Persians were Aryans who came from Asia and invaded the Iranian plateau and northern India around 3000 BCE. Unfortunately, another theory tells us that the Semites also came from Asia. In any case, the inscriptions found in Media and Persia — the supposedly sacred homeland of Aryan civilization — are in cuneiform, Egyptian, or Aramaic, three languages classified as Semitic.

The contradiction is obvious.

But in what passes for scientific scholarship, one contradiction more or less apparently makes little difference. Continuing our investigation into the Semites, we consulted recent works published under the authority of the Sorbonne, the Collège de France, and the École Biblique in Jerusalem.

We found many fascinating studies. But they were littered with phrases such as:

“There is a fairly strong chance that…”

“Everything happened as though…”

“Among the available hypotheses, we have chosen the one that seemed most plausible and most consistent with *the idea we had formed*…”

“The text may have been copied from a Hebrew original… which has not been found.”

Is there not something strange about calling a witness who is absent?

Elsewhere one comes across a note by a Heidelberg scholar confidently identifying a mistake supposedly made by a copyist four thousand years ago.

Can this really be taken seriously?

Examples of uncertain hypotheses and unsupported conclusions could be multiplied without end. The striking thing about these experts is that they agree on almost nothing — except, strangely enough, on the use of the word *Semite*, while disagreeing completely about what the word actually means.

In short, we are dealing with ignorance.

Scientific ignorance, certainly, but ignorance all the same. Everything would become much simpler if, instead of speaking of Semites — fictional heroes created by an imaginary genealogy — we spoke of Arabs: a real people whose social, cultural, and linguistic continuity has helped shape the Mediterranean for thousands of years. The monuments themselves show that the civilization we have inherited first took form and reached greatness in the enormous region bounded by the Nile and the Indus, the Caucasus and the Bab el-Mandeb Strait.

Four great peoples built long-lasting empires there: the Egyptians, the Syro-Canaanites, the Greco-Hittites, and the Babylonians. One written and spoken language eventually spread across this enormous region: Aramaic, together with its western companion, Greek, which was closely related to it. Aramaic, in turn, developed naturally and without rupture into Arabic. Arabic therefore became the heir to the Egyptian, Canaanite, Hittite, and Babylonian pasts.

That is the true scale of Arab culture.

It was the mother and inspiration of Hellenism, which it shaped in spirit and in law. Arab and Greek culture worked together to produce what we call civilization — a civilization that, seen in this light, is neither Eastern nor Western, neither Semitic nor Aryan, but one and indivisible in both its spiritual and material forms. The three original texts of our monotheistic tradition stand as powerful witnesses to this truth: one, the Qur’an, is written in Arabic; the other two, the Old and New Testaments, in Greek.

We need to pause here and clarify the role assigned to Hebrew, because a mixture of illusion and endless etymological sleight of hand has led many people to treat the Hebrews and their “culture” as the ultimate ancestors of Eastern history, and therefore of our own. To begin with, outside biblical texts, history is completely silent about the Hebrews.

Neither archaeology, inscriptions, nor sculpture reveal any trace of a specifically Hebrew civilization. Among the thousands of cuneiform and Egyptian texts that fill the libraries of Egypt, Ras Shamra, and Nineveh, and in Aramaic narratives as well, the word *Hebrew* does not appear. The famous biblical kings David and Solomon appear in no chronicle. Nor is there any record of the epic events and battles supposedly connected with a Hebrew Exodus. Excavations carried out in Palestine since 1890 reveal no break in civilization.

The absence is complete.

History cannot be written when the facts are unknown, and events cannot be reconstructed when no documents exist. In 1973, under the patronage of the Israeli authorities, a handsome edition of the works of Flavius Josephus was published. It was illustrated with Babylonian, Sumerian, Egyptian, and Hittite material — in other words, in the terminology used here, Arab material. There was nothing Hebrew about it, not even the text itself, which, as everyone knows, comes to us in Greek. Josephus wrote in Greek and spoke Aramaic, as did the other Palestinians of his time.

The Gospels likewise know nothing of “Hebrews,” and neither does the Qur’an, which speaks only of Jews, Israelites, or the Children of Israel. Whenever the word *Hebrew* appears in Arabic, Greek, or Latin literature, moreover, it refers to a religion rather than a nation. There is, of course, an *Epistle to the Hebrews*. But commentators themselves have questioned the title, first because the words “to the Hebrews” appear to have been added in the margin, and second because nobody could agree on what *Hebrew* actually meant.

At present, we cannot define the Hebrews securely in terms of time, geography, society, or religion. Flavius Josephus offers little help. His *Antiquities of the Jews* is full of contradictions and stories: he places Chaldea outside Mesopotamia, turns Abraham into a king of Syria, and remarks that “the Samaritans are Hebrews but not Jews.” One thing is certain: Hebrew was not the original language of Judaism. Its living spoken language was first Aramaic and later Arabic, and Judaism shared the wider cultural history of Egyptian-Babylonian religion, Orphism, Christianity, and Islam.

When Jesus cried from the cross, “Allah, Allah, limadha sabaqtani,” he cried out in Arabic. Any Arab today would understand the words as: “My God, my God, why do you go before me?” or “Why do you leave me behind?” There is nothing Hebrew in this, despite what some scholars have written. To satisfy ourselves, we looked through the words described as “Hebrew” by Christian commentators and found that most of them are simply Arabic words.

If biblical scholarship would finally stop making matters needlessly complicated and reread the Bible in the light of Arabic language and culture, an entire artificial scholastic system would collapse. In its place we would gain a new and living vision of the Testaments. It is extraordinary that Revelation — intended for universal preaching and prayer, and meant to be understood by ordinary people — should have become imprisoned in Hebrew, a sacred writing system supposedly created for a tiny priestly sect.

The Jews of the East, by contrast, never stopped expressing themselves in Arabic and produced some of the most distinguished figures in Arabic literature, thought, and science. We have already said that the earliest form in which the Jewish Bible comes down to us is Greek: the text produced in Alexandria in the third century BCE under Ptolemy III, during the same period in which the Homeric, epic, and esoteric works transmitted by tradition were being collected and organized.

Egypt, then, is the mother of the Old Testament.

The same Arab-Hellenic spirit presided over the epics of David and Achilles. Any attentive reader can see it. As one might expect, the undeniable originality of the Greek text known as the Septuagint — the source from which Jewish tradition was transmitted — creates a problem for Hebraists, who insist on treating it as a translation or adaptation of an earlier Hebrew version.

Nothing has ever confirmed this hypothesis.

It was only from the third century CE onward that Jewish tradition, until then expressed in an Arab-Aramaic form, began to be written down in Hebrew, with Syriac being used for the purpose much as it is still taught today. A few Hebrew fragments do appear on the Nash Papyrus, dating from the first century BCE, but their interpretation remained disputed, and the signs may instead be Neo-Phoenician.

The Hebrew text of the Jewish Bible was fixed only much later, between the ninth and tenth centuries CE, by scholars of the Tiberias school known as the Masoretes, who drew on four sources: the Greek Septuagint, Saint Jerome’s Latin Vulgate, the Aramaic Targums, and Syriac material.

A great deal of excitement arose some years ago around the discovery of the Dead Sea Scrolls at Khirbet Qumran. At the time, Zionist political activity was at its height in Palestine, at the United Nations, and in public opinion, and there was an obvious interest in finding biblical justification for the military enterprise. The timing of the discovery therefore struck some as suspicious.

Scholars consulted about the documents remained very cautious and in any case dated their composition to the second or third century CE. Their writing, moreover, appeared on close inspection to be full of Phoenician and Aramaic signs. Suspicion had since increased, and the Dead Sea Scrolls were being treated with growing scepticism. They could do nothing to change the judgment of serious scholars about the role of Hebrew in Eastern history.

The Roman Church itself created a liturgical and evangelical Latin for its internal life, deliberately giving it an archaic sound to express the distance between ordinary human society and the language of the divine. There is no denying that the harmonies of the Psalms in Church Latin or Hebrew can touch the heart and awaken a sense of the world beyond. But nobody would seriously use such languages as reliable evidence for linguistic history, still less as the beginning or end point of an entire culture. The beauty of liturgical languages lies precisely in their unreality.

Their value is aesthetic, not historical.

Modern Hebrew is itself an invention shaped by circumstance. Eliezer Ben-Yehuda published, between 1910 and 1922, a dictionary commissioned by the World Zionist movement and intended to provide something like an Esperanto for Jews from around the world who were being encouraged to migrate to Palestine.

Modern Hebrew was therefore a political instrument.

Why, then, did Western scholarship make Hebrew the guiding thread of its investigations into the East when it had at its disposal a living and reliable Arabic language — the faithful expression of a continuity thousands of years old and an obvious key to the mysteries of the ancient world? One explanation might be found in the bias of the Roman Catholic Church and its scholars, whose monasteries became strongholds of militant biblical interpretation.

Since the fourth and fifth centuries, when the Eastern and Western churches clashed violently over Christological questions involving Nestorians, Monophysites, Arians, and other “heretics,” Rome had been in open or hidden conflict with Arab modes of thought. The Crusades later launched by the papacy against the East were directed not only against Islam but against religious and philosophical systems shaped directly by Arab thought, whether these flourished in the East itself or had been adopted by communities in Spain, Russia, or southern France.

Ancient chronicles even called Aquitanians, Basques, Andalusians, and Castilians “Arabs” when they had been only lightly touched by Trinitarian Christianity and remained open to both Judaism and Islam. In this respect it is significant that Eastern Christianity, despite its wealth of faith and evangelical tradition, never looked to Hebrew for its justification, even though it would have been ideally placed to do so. That form of Christianity remained attached to its ancestral language: Arabic.

It was Latin Christianity, and Latin Christianity alone, that turned toward a remote and eccentric branch of the tradition, making early use of Hebrew as a weapon in its struggle against Eastern metaphysics. Just as it is absurd, in the name of some supposed Semitism, to separate the Arabs from the Egyptian-Canaanite-Babylonian cultural world to which they belong, it is equally superstitious to assign Hebrew a unique and separate place.

Hebrew is simply a late liturgical branch of Arabic: an intellectual language that remained unknown to ordinary people for a very long time and was therefore largely sterile, because much of it had been deliberately constructed. Even today, Hebrew belongs to a small closed world of scholars. In the popular and historical sense, it has no independent existence. The prestige once given to it by its defenders is already fading and modern scholarship is beginning to question the value of this liturgical emblem wrapped in the robes of Roman and apostolic propaganda.

The time has come to replace the phrase “Hebrew reference” with “biblical reference,” because the two are far from identical. The biblical tradition has infinitely more in common with Arabic — or Aramaic, which here amounts to much the same thing. Replace Noah with his Arabic name Nuh, Job with Ayyub, Jonah with Yunus ibn Matta, Shem with Sam ibn Nuh, Abraham with Ibrahim, David with Dawud, Aaron with Harun, Solomon with Sulayman, Goliath with Jalut, Jesus with Isa, and Mary with Maryam.

Then we recover the original freshness of the Testament and feel its living presence again. We begin to understand how, across thousands of years, it preserved a voice whose tone and spirit can still be heard among the millions of people who inhabit the East today and who are its unquestionable heirs.

It then becomes obvious that Judaism, Christianity, and Islam — together with Zoroastrian religion, solar religions, Orphism, mystery religions and religions of salvation, and the Greek and Roman cults — arose like brothers and sisters from the same Eastern cosmos that once stretched from the Nile to the Indus and was carried by a common language: Aramaic, still living within modern Arabic. When Jesus declares, “Before Abraham was, I am,” the words imply that his message did not arise from Judaism but belonged to a much older spiritual universe.

Between the Nile, the Caucasus, Yemen, and the Indus, mystical currents crossed and recrossed one another until they sometimes became so tightly woven that any attempt to draw geographical borders between them is pointless. The cultural, religious, and poetic world of the early East was deeply universal in character, and remained so for a long time. As early as the second millennium BCE, Ishtar was worshipped at Thebes, Babylon, Carchemish, and Susa before appearing among the Greeks as Aphrodite and among the Romans as Venus.

Likewise, in the third century CE, Emperor Alexander Severus — raised at Antioch and educated by the Egyptian Origen — worshipped in the same private shrine a trinity consisting of Abraham, Orpheus, and Jesus.

Ancient society did not analyze religion.

It lived religion, just as it lived nature, the sky, and the earth. It did not constantly ask the difficult questions with which our rationalism entertains itself. Whatever the doctrinal differences between the religions of Baal, Yahweh, Isis, Orpheus, and Jesus, all of them shared the same basic understanding of humanity. They looked down on human life from the height of the heavens. Their concern was not personal happiness but humanity’s harmonious relationship with the elements, with origins, with death, and with the universe.

And because the history of the universe cannot be measured in days but in light-years, religious ancient societies thought politically and historically across immense stretches of space and time. An individual human life mattered only when it became part of a reality that was at once cosmic, epic, and therefore mythical.

For God can only be epic.

He is not there to keep an account of our moods or the fluctuations of our hearts. History as we understand it — day by day, event after event — would have seemed a pitiful little affair to the ancient person. Ancient people saw and lived their destiny as a drama unfolding on many levels at once, in which entire peoples, heavenly cities, and fabulous powers confronted one another.

At that height, differences disappear.

All religions meet in heaven.

Heaven is one; therefore religion is one.

Theological quarrels came only later.

For a long time, temples of Baal or Mithras, churches in Axum or Armenia, shrines of Osiris, chapels in Mesopotamia, and mosques or synagogues in Yemen and Syria expressed essentially the same cast of mind. So true is this that across the centuries the same sanctuaries could pass successively from the worship of Ishtar to Orpheus, from the Eternal God of the Jews to Jesus and then to Islam. Religious and philosophical fanaticism is much more characteristic of the modern world.

Greek offers valuable evidence of this spiritual continuity across space and time, but Arabic offers even more. From its Nile and Mesopotamian origins to the present day, Arabic has carried every form of religious thought that has shaped our societies, together with their meditations, philosophies, aesthetics, and both public and hidden sciences. The priest of Baal spoke Arabic. So did the devotee of Isis and Moses the Egyptian. Christ, naturally, spoke Arabic when addressing Caiaphas or the people of Palestine. Muhammad spoke Arabic as well. The straight line of our culture has never been broken.

For a philologist it is child’s play to uncover the essential elements of Arabic at the roots of Egyptian, Canaanite, Anatolian, and Assyro-Babylonian languages. Sometimes an entire word has travelled intact through the centuries and compresses thousands of years of history into a single astonishing survival.

A few examples make the point.

In cuneiform and Aramaic texts, Mesopotamia is called *Senaar*; modern Arabic calls it *Shenaar*. The sun god Shamash corresponds to modern Arabic *shams*, meaning the sun or the East. *Baal* means “master” in Arabic, while *rab* — a Mesopotamian word from which *rabbi* derives — means “father.” *Rab el beit* is the “master of the house.” The suffix *malek*, found in so many biblical names, means “owner.”

The Babylonian god of lightning is Baraq; twentieth-century Arabic has *barqua*. The god of fortune is Djad, while modern Arabic *djada* means prosperity. Tammuz gave his name to the Arabic month corresponding to July. The many Mesopotamian and biblical words containing roots such as *Chalem* or *Chalom* recall the Arabic *salam*. The Syro-Palestinian god of the underworld is Mout; the same word means death in Arabic. *Hag* means a ritual festival in Mesopotamian usage; in Arabic, *Hajj* is the great pilgrimage.

And *Sabet*, Arabic for Saturday, or the Sabbath, derives directly from the Babylonian *Sabattu*, the festival of the full moon.

The examples could go on forever.

Even Greek drew a significant part of its vocabulary and structure from the Mesopotamian and Aramaic world. If the Greek *Sibyl* (*Sibulla*) means a sacred person who delivers oracles, Arabic *as-sabil* means “the road that leads to God” and can also refer to a meeting or contemplative place in a town, usually marked by a fountain. The Greeks themselves never concealed their Asian ancestry. They regarded themselves as pupils of the Egyptians and Babylonians. Their pantheon was Arab; their accounts of the origins of the universe and the gods came directly from Anatolia or Canaan.

Was Hesiod’s own father not Aeolian in origin?

Herodotus expresses surprise at the distinction between Europe and Asia because he and his countrymen saw them as parts of one and the same culture. Greece, in this view, was born from Asia. Through Phoenician settlement and contact, it inherited the fruit of some four thousand years of Egyptian and Babylonian development.

Its flowering came late.

A thousand years before Homer, while the Greeks still lived in obscurity, the subjects of Thutmose enjoyed highly developed art and refined comforts in the Nile valley. When Greece passed its Asian inheritance westward into Sicily and Italy, it also carried with it the different religions of the Arab world — including Christianity, since the New Testament reached the western Mediterranean in Greek. Why, then, should we, the heirs of Hellenism, continue to define ourselves only through a supposed Judeo-Christian philosophy? Judaism and Christianity are merely parts of the larger Hellenic inheritance.

We are children of Asia — children of the Nile-Mesopotamian Arab world.

We claim the whole inheritance.

We should therefore distrust ready-made labels.

*Judaism* and *Christianity* are terms that conceal a reality far more complicated and far broader than the narrow boxes into which we have placed them. There are many troubling things about the Jews in the histories of Flavius Josephus, in Greek and Latin literature, and even in the Gospels, where Saint John calls the enemies of Christ “the Jews.” How are we to explain Jesus openly departing from the law of Moses and even celebrating his own Passover on a date of his own choosing, since the Jewish Passover coincided with his death?

Another troubling question follows.

Why do the Apostles — themselves supposedly “Jews” — who wrote the Gospels from around 70 or 80 CE onward never mention an event said to have shaken Jewish society to its foundations: the destruction of the Temple by Titus in the year 70?

Why, for his part, does Flavius Josephus mention Jesus of Nazareth only incidentally?

And there is another serious question.

The first great centres of Christianity were neither Jerusalem nor Nazareth but the major cities of the Arab world of the time: Philadelphia, Ptolemais, Sardis, Pergamon, Damascus, Smyrna, Ephesus, and Laodicea. Why was the *Apocalypse* — a letter of Revelation, from the Greek *apokaluptein*, “to uncover” — addressed at the end of the first century to seven churches in Asia, located in the very same places where the great cults of Isis, Baal, and Orpheus had flourished? The *Apocalypse* contains a curse directed against “those who say they are Jews and are not, but belong to the synagogue of Satan.”

Equally strange is Saint Paul’s use of “Israel” for the whole community of believers, followed by the cryptic declaration: “Not all who descend from Israel belong to Israel.” The Gospels also say that there were “Greeks who went up to worship at Jerusalem.”

What, in such passages, does *Greek* mean?

What does *Jew* mean?

Nothing about this terminology is simple.

We should therefore seriously reconsider the habit of claiming an exclusively Judeo-Christian inheritance. Instead of constantly invoking it, we should speak of a Greco-Asian tradition.

What better way could there be to escape the false categories of Aryanism and Semitism?

Judaism, Orphism, Christianity, Manichaeism, Islam, and the countless forms of gnosis were seen by ancient peoples as parts of an indivisible whole. Gods and angels could pass easily from one tradition to another because the people of antiquity were, above all, believers. Perhaps it is precisely because we no longer believe as they did that we cling so fiercely to a handful of theological distinctions and reduce religion to debates over doctrine. With our eyes fixed relentlessly on the same ghosts, we fail to notice the movement of the sun.

We should admit that our horizon has narrowed to ourselves and to a few inherited ideas that flatter our opinion of our own importance. Carefully and laboriously, we keep improving the image of ourselves that pleases us. Peoples with ancient cultures can live within their history almost unconsciously, integrated into it in body and spirit. We Europeans, by contrast, suffer from the sleeplessness of a perpetual search.

Egyptian, Canaanite, Babylonian, and Anatolian civilizations have all been studied separately through isolated monographs. An “Arab world,” equally separate from everything around it, was then invented as another category. Analysis went further. Within each civilization, scholars identified provincial subgroups, which were then divided and subdivided into smaller religious, ethnic, family, and other units. The art of dissection was carried so far that, beneath our deadly microscope, civilizations eventually shattered into fragments. As analysis advanced, our appetite for synthesis declined.

Yet without synthesis, there can be no history.

Our critics seem unable to imagine studying the histories of East and West not through one isolated country or one particular episode, but through a cultural and social unity whose coherence is demonstrated by the surviving evidence as a whole. Military and political borders drawn for the convenience of professors or archaeologists do not necessarily run through the hearts of human beings. When we take this broader view and say that the East is defined by an Arab culture within an Arab cultural world, we are inventing nothing.

We are simply gathering together and reconnecting geographical and cultural elements that an excessive passion for analysis has kept apart. That excessive analysis is the first reason that we have lost contact with the real world. The second is the university education that, since the Renaissance, has overwhelmingly favoured Athens and Rome. These two cities became retrospective utopias in which Europeans from the fifteenth century onward imagined they had discovered the highest expression of their own ideals.

From that point, the Arabs gradually disappeared from European culture and retreated into ever more distant sands, only to be rediscovered in the twentieth century by specialists in camels, tribes, vendettas, and Bedouins. It was also from the Renaissance onward that Europeans, faced with the undeniable artistic, industrial, and scientific sophistication of the age of the Caliphs, began explaining the Arabs merely as translators and adapters of the Greeks.

The legend has proved remarkably durable.

Even today, some Arabs appeal to it when defending the achievements of Arab civilization.

One well-meaning writer tells us:

“If Avicenna had not translated Aristotle, Saint Thomas Aquinas might never have existed.”

The truth is the reverse:

“If Greece had not first been formed by Arab culture, Aristotle certainly would never have existed.”

Only Athens and Rome shone in the Renaissance sky. They were the alpha and omega of the cultivated European. “I want to read Homer’s *Iliad* in three days,” Ronsard promised himself eagerly. In his magnificent *School of Athens*, Raphael set out to depict the intellectual architecture of perfect humanity. The Catholic popes of Rome clothed themselves in the military and legal majesty of the Caesars. Yet beneath this apparent intellectual reunion with antiquity began an age of cultural narrowing.

Athens and Rome, both relatively modest powers, came from the fifteenth century onward to occupy almost the entire European stage. At the same time, the memory of the immense Arab civilization that had stretched from the Nile to the Indus across several thousand years faded from our tradition. In the eighteenth and nineteenth centuries, theorists of democracy and free thought took possession of the schools and largely shaped the writing of textbooks.

Athens became the idol of republican education.

The Rome of Brutus became the emblem of civic virtue. Turned into caricatures as the teachers of humanity, the two capitals were placed above the entrances to the political temples and creeds of liberal society. Ernest Renan’s *Prayer on the Acropolis* remains a masterpiece of the genre — and a fine example of literary foolishness. Beside the pyramids and the temples of Karnak or Luxor, the Parthenon is, after all, a very small thing. So too was the Athenian republic. Between the victory at Salamis and its surrender to the Spartan Lysander, its brilliance lasted barely seventy years.

And what are some 1,150 years of Roman power beside the five or six thousand years of Egyptian and Babylonian civilization, rising from the depths of prehistory and continuing their unbroken rule in the East even today?

Indeed, Rome itself owed its longevity to the East.

From the reign of Augustus onward, the Roman Empire was absorbed into a wider cultural, religious, and political system joining Egypt, Anatolia, Mesopotamia, and Syria. The Empire took on the mark of Arab civilization from the moment Agrippa commissioned a Syrian architect to build the Pantheon in Rome at the dawn of the Christian era.

With the Flavians began the Asian dynasties.

Rome survived through Byzantium.

Justinian prepared the way for the Caliphs.

And Athens?

By then it was a forgotten provincial town.

Imagination and political or aesthetic prejudice may have magnified it, but history itself restored it to its proper scale. Neither Christianity nor Islam travelled through the capital of Pericles. Their roads led through Damascus, Medina, and Jerusalem. The Middle Ages, fascinated by Egypt and the Holy Land, barely remembered Athens. One of the great works of the period, the *Golden Legend*, which gathered together the popular traditions of Europe in the thirteenth century, celebrates Palestine, Syria, Egypt, Byzantium, and Anatolia.

Athens receives barely a few words.

The *Golden Legend*, largely forgotten today, was an enormous storehouse of knowledge and learning. Together with Dante’s work, which it helped inspire, it became one of the richest sources of artistic, poetic, and liturgical themes Christianity ever possessed. So of the two capitals that dominate our classical education, one — Athens — faded into the outskirts of history. A meteor in the intellectual sky, it contributed nothing lasting to the peoples of East or West. The other — Rome — melted into the crucible of Arab culture and became, through Byzantium and the Church, both its heir and its witness.

We should draw the necessary conclusions and begin putting our inherited beliefs back in order. It is also high time to stop confusing the history of a few poor tribes of the Arabian Peninsula with the history of the Arabs as a whole. The history of France is not the history of Lozère or a few impoverished Alpine valleys. In the same way, Arab history and culture cannot be reduced to the grazing grounds of three or four wandering families whose miraculous destiny specialists describe to us in lyrical and confused terms.

There are no miracles in history.

If Arab civilization spread almost overnight from the Pyrenees to the Malay Archipelago, it cannot have been the private possession of a handful of lizard-eaters suddenly raised to glory. If Islam spread across whole continents, if Arabic achieved a reach that no other language had yet matched, and if it became the language of Judaism, Christianity, Islam, gnosis, mystery traditions, and magic, then behind it must have stood a civilization whose authority extended far beyond some hill in the Hijaz.

The Greeks submitted to that authority and then the Romans and Etruscans, followed later by the Visigothic kingdoms of the West and the princes of India. For the same reason, it is as difficult to believe in a straightforward Greek victory in the Persian Wars as in a straightforward conquest of Asia by Alexander. Nor is it any easier to accept that Spain could have been invaded militarily by Arabs from the Hijaz alone. In every case, the imbalance of forces was so great that we must look beyond textbook explanations for the deeper causes of what was not really a conflict at all, but a collaboration.

For that search, common sense will be enough.

It is generally more honest than scholarship.

If we reject the idea of a “Semitic people and Semitic language” as fanciful and scientifically worthless; if we are thinking in order to see clearly rather than to comfort ourselves with familiar assumptions; and if we are determined not to build history out of dreams, then we must define Arab identity first and foremost as a culture — indeed as the culture of the East — and use that perspective to reconsider everything we were taught at school under the heading *The East and Greece*. We can do that only by abandoning the incoherent and fragmented picture of the East inherited from our teachers.

We must begin by separating what belongs to Islam from what existed long before it. Islamic government centralized power, but it did not create the nations it ruled. The territorial and social unity that Alexander had already inherited centuries before the Caliphs was itself the product of an older history.

Nor did Islam create Arab civilization.

That civilization had already been carried to its highest levels by pharaonic and Babylonian ancestors. Islam emerged from the desert, but it did not return to the desert. It addressed the enormous populations of the great maritime and river cities in a language and a mental world they already understood. The religion revealed to the Prophet made sense to them. It was neither a bizarre innovation nor a revolution. It fulfilled earlier traditions and scriptures. It did not deny or reject them; it absorbed them.

The spiritual traditions of the East were many in appearance but one in essence. Islam gathered them into a single act of faith in the oneness of God.

The Qur’an does not add; it brings together.

The Qur’an does not debate; it concludes.

It does not divide; it unites.

Strictly speaking, Islam was not a new religion.

It proclaimed submission to the eternal God: present in past and future alike, one, unchanging, uncreated, filling the universe. Such an idea was not born from an echo heard in some lonely corner of the desert. It was the distilled result of centuries of reflection across the Nile valley, Canaan, and Mesopotamia. Within it we find Chaldean cosmology, Egyptian and Christian resurrection, and the apocalyptic hope that had been voiced by followers of gnosis before it was heard from Saint John. Islam therefore did not take the peoples of the East by surprise.

It illuminated something they already knew.

That is a very different thing.

No sword or persecution was needed to convert them.

The natural direction of their ancestral beliefs was already leading them toward Islam. Jews and Christians, but also Magi, Greeks, and worshippers of many different gods, recognized in it a message that was not foreign to them. Just as Islam did not need to conquer public belief by force, the Arabs did not need to conquer the East and the Mediterranean militarily. They had been at home there since the distant past. What they did conquer culturally was the European West and, by extension, the American West, introducing their religions, philosophy, and aesthetics.

It was an immense undertaking in which Hellenism and its Etruscan offshoot served as allies and intermediaries, helping shape the societies of Italy. For a long time, this cultural initiation of the West was credited to Hellenism alone. Nobody stopped to consider what a miracle that explanation required, or to notice that Greece itself was only a balcony and an extension of the vast Arab structure of the East — something the Greeks themselves understood perfectly well. In this respect, we became more Greek than the Greeks.

We risk understanding very little about Pericles or Aeschylus if we ignore their Asian kinship. And we misunderstand Islam and the Arabs if we tear them away from the geography and spiritual world that gave birth to them.

# II

## FIVE SEAS, FIVE RIVERS, FIVE EMPIRES

> “Time, the moving image of unchanging eternity.”
>
> PLATO.

How, then, were these peoples — misleadingly called “Semites,” though in reality Arab — organized politically across the region? We must avoid the old mistake of breaking their geographical world into separate pieces and studying each piece on its own, as though they were not limbs of the same body, animated by the same life. Instead, we will follow their shared movement through time.

The East has always been imperial ground. Its history unfolded to the steady rhythm of five empires: Egyptian, Babylonian, Roman, Byzantine, and the Caliphate. Each empire, we shall see, settled over the one before it without fundamentally changing its social or cultural structures. From the mythical first pharaoh, Menes, who was said to have ruled in the fifth millennium BCE, through Alexander and down to the fall of the last caliph, the East displays a remarkable continuity: continuity of language, thought, and economic life.

The changes brought by the centuries are so slight that they show how little the slow patience of history resembles the impatience of individual lives. The Egyptian of today is as ancient as a contemporary of Rameses; the Yemeni who lived beside Bilqis, the Queen of Sheba, is no less modern than a merchant in the port of Aden. Everything happens as though time really were only the moving image of unchanging eternity.

The four great Eastern empires did not confine themselves to the lands between Libya and the Indian Ocean, or between the Black Sea and Somalia. Very early on, their influence spread to mainland Greece and the Greek islands, to Sicily and western Italy, and eventually as far as the Strait of Gibraltar. Palestine and the Aegean were the keys to this whole system. Its five great bodies of water were the Mediterranean, the Black Sea, the Red Sea, the Persian Gulf, and the Indian Ocean. Its great channels of movement were the Tigris, the Euphrates, the Nile, the Rhône, and the Danube.

Over the centuries, our civilization took shape within a triangle stretching between the Bosporus, the Nile, and Susa, the Elamite capital. Its founders were the Egyptian, Canaanite, Anatolian, and Assyro-Babylonian peoples, all belonging to the same Arab family. Where they originally came from matters little. We will probably never know, and even if we did, it would add very little to our conclusions. The civilizations of Crimea and the Caspian region should also be included in this wider world.

Look at a map of the East. Which great cities appear and disappear as this drama unfolds — a drama whose beginnings reach back, as far as our knowledge allows, to the fifth millennium BCE?

Four names shine first: Memphis, Tyre, Babylon, and Susa. Six more follow them: Alexandria, Carthage, Rome, Antioch, Byzantium, and Seleucia. Finally one last name shines almost alone: Baghdad. It is as though Baghdad were the culmination and flowering of a culture that had once drawn Athens, Carthage, Syracuse, and Cumae into its orbit, while its deepest centres remained Egypt and Mesopotamia.

Beyond these famous cities, much of the map of the East lies in darkness. We barely notice Tayma in the northern Hijaz or Marib in Yemen, although both show that even in very ancient times there was already a cultural community linking the peoples of the Arabian desert with the wealthy river kingdoms — linking what our scholars, reluctant to use the word “Arab,” prefer to call the “southern Semites” and the “northern Semites.” Nor do we notice Akhetaten, the city of Akhenaten’s religious revolution; the River Meles, on whose bank stood the tomb of Orpheus; Bethlehem; or Assos in Thrace, where Aristotle was educated.

A city or region can be overlooked by archaeologists and scholars without being absent from history. Two cities in particular, modest though their fame may be, occupy a central place in the history of the East because they reveal its larger movement and its main lines of force: Gaza on the Palestinian coast and Carchemish in the upper Euphrates valley.

They were crossroads of trade and culture, and therefore natural battlefields. Both stood on the two great international routes of the age.

The first route was anchored by the Egyptian capitals, Memphis or Thebes, with the Gulf of Aqaba as an offshoot. Following the Nile and the Red Sea, it carried goods from Sudan and West Africa. It passed through Yemen and the trading depots of the Hijaz and ran northward, roughly along the meridians, toward Nineveh. From there it followed the Euphrates down to Babylon. Another branch crossed overland toward the Black Sea.

The pharaohs constantly watched over the security of this upper-Euphrates route, whose key point was Carchemish. Repeated military expeditions eventually established a long-lasting Egyptian protectorate over Palestine and northern Syria.

But there was a second route, this one Asian, centred on Babylon. Running east to west, it carried goods from India and the islands of Southeast Asia. It enriched Oman along the way, crossed the Strait of Hormuz, and followed the Persian Gulf. It then climbed through Cissia, Matiene, Armenia, Cilicia, Cappadocia, and Phrygia before reaching Sardis, the great capital of Asia Minor, and from there Pergamum, Smyrna, Ephesus, and Miletus, where Greek shipowners and merchants flourished.

This route too passed through Carchemish. There the Egyptian and Babylonian empires — African and Asian — met one another. When their strength was balanced, they held each other in check; when one weakened, they fought. Naharaina, the upper Euphrates basin, with Carchemish at its heart, became one of the most fiercely contested strategic crossroads of antiquity.

The peoples of Anatolia, who naturally controlled this disputed junction, were bound to try to build an empire of their own there. That empire was the great Hittite or Mitannian power, which at different times fought or cooperated with Egypt and Assyro-Babylonia, and whose history was every bit as brilliant, if not as long-lived, as that of its two rivals.

For at least three thousand years — and probably much longer — two vast, continuous streams of exchange connected three continents: Asia, Africa, and Europe. Through Babylonia and Asia Minor, the Greek and Aegean world received ideas and goods from Chaldea and India. Through the Nile and Palestine, the Greek peninsula received products from Africa, Ethiopia, and Yemen. In the other direction, Greek and other travellers and merchants carried their languages, cultures, and goods to places as distant as Angola and Ceylon.

Ever since Paul Valéry flattered modern Europe with his reflections on its place in the world, our self-importance has only grown. Because we can move faster, we assume the world has somehow become smaller. We imagine the ancient world as static, provincial, and ignorant of neighbouring peoples.

The opposite is closer to the truth. The multiplication of national borders, the hardening of customs, discrimination, and all the ties and obligations of everyday modern life have made us more homebound and more cautious than people once were. Faster and easier travel has not necessarily improved matters. If anything, it can leave us less prepared to take a real interest in the country we visit. The conditions of modern transport and tourism often isolate us more than they bring us close to others. We travel inside a cage designed for us, and only step out of it once we are home again. Can that still be called travel?

In antiquity, entire tribes, peoples, and families set out to settle elsewhere, sometimes for a short period and sometimes for good. The chronicles of Egypt, Babylonia, Phoenicia, Anatolia, and Greece are full of migrations, colonies, departures, and arrivals. People moved constantly.

Are we really expected to believe that the inhabitants of the Hijaz, Hadramaut, Sana’a, Mecca, and Medina remained poor, passive spectators while this extraordinary movement unfolded around them from north to south and east to west — with goods, precious metals, weapons, ships, ideas, cults, and gods circulating everywhere? They were swept up in the same movement as everyone else. They knew what was happening in Sardis, Susa, Nineveh, Karnak, and Athens. They drew on the same cultural sources and shared in a common destiny, shaped — as destinies always are — in the great capitals of the age.

This is why a history built from still pictures of one country after another can never capture the character of any of them. A country’s character lies in its life, its conduct, and the movement it carries or is carried by. To see history as anything less than a moving picture is to stop its blood and dry up its flow.

I have deliberately reduced this first picture of the East to three great imperial faces — Egypt, the Hittite-Mitannian world, and Babylon — not simply for the sake of convenience. The kingdoms of the Medes and Persians lay farther from the Mediterranean and were soon drawn into the empires closer to its shores, sometimes through conquest.

The long-term movement of the Near East was always toward the sea. Just as Susa eventually gave way to Babylon, Egypt’s capital would move toward Alexandria; the power of Lydia would come to rest at Byzantium; and the Syrian principality centred on Seleucia would shift toward Palmyra and Damascus. The Medes and Persians turned west early, toward their relatives and neighbours.

As early as the third millennium BCE, pottery, rock art, and sculpture reveal clear similarities between the peoples of the Indus Valley — at Harappa, Amri, and Rupar — and those of Sumer and Susa. The terraced structures of Pasargadae, Persepolis, and Masjed Soleiman follow Babylonian forms. The colossal guardians standing before the royal palaces of southern Persia imitate Hittite or Assyrian art. The glazed-brick decoration of Achaemenid monuments at Susa, with its lions, griffins, and winged creatures, recalls the Ishtar Gate and its imagery at Babylon.

That alone is enough to cast serious doubt on the famous dividing line some scholars draw between “Aryan” Persians and “Arab-Semitic” peoples. Nor is the claim now promoted by the authorities in Tehran — that Iran has, from time immemorial, been radically different from Iraq in ethnicity, mentality, and tradition — historically convincing. Such language may serve a diplomatic purpose. It does little to explain history.

From the first known Median king, the great Cyaxares, to the last Persian king, Darius Codomannus, defeated by Alexander, the history of the Iranian world is dominated by its relations with Babylonia, Egypt, and Greece. The Persian Wars were only one episode in that much longer story. Around 500 BCE, did not Darius have the Egyptian law code drawn up in Aramaic so that it could be applied throughout the empire?

The Persian armies, like those before them, took the roads to Gaza and Carchemish. Alexander would do the same, and after him Byzantine emperors and caliphs would follow in his footsteps. History is like the stars: it moves along enduring paths.

When the future “Colonel Lawrence” joined the excavations at Carchemish in February 1911, after an agreement between the Ottoman and British governments, he was already looking for traces of the Arabs. He had become convinced that the word concealed a reality very different from the poor and narrow picture he had been taught at Oxford.

The finds at Carchemish, where Egyptian-Mediterranean influences met Indo-Babylonian currents, closely resemble objects found at Gaza, Tayma in the Hijaz, Marib, and Bahrain. That alone should make us wary of dividing these remains into several separate cultures, still less of trying to assign fixed geographical identities to peoples whose very names are uncertain.

To measure the full extent of Egyptian influence in the ancient world would be almost impossible. No one could seriously attempt it with the documentation we have, especially when entire centuries or vast regions — Macedonia, Crimea, the Anatolian plateau, North Africa, East Africa — sometimes disappear almost completely from the record. We can barely imagine the economic and intellectual influence continuously exercised by great centres such as Memphis, Thebes, and Amarna. We simply lack enough points of reference.

What we do know is that Egypt’s policy toward Asia, later made famous by Thutmose III and Rameses II, had already begun by the middle of the third millennium BCE, around 2600. The wall paintings in the mortuary temple of Queen Hatshepsut of the Eighteenth Dynasty show expeditions into the Indian Ocean. Cretan and Cypriot civilization drew heavily on Egyptian and Canaanite influences. Phoenicia reached the height of its prosperity between the seventeenth and fifteenth centuries BCE, when the fleet of Sidon operated under pharaonic authority.

Historical Greece, at least as it appears to us through its literature, begins with the pharaoh Psamtik, who in the middle of the seventh century BCE opened Egyptian ports to Greek sailors. Solon, the first of Europe’s great lawgivers, who established the Athenian constitution around 590 BCE, had studied in Egypt.

Perhaps the most striking evidence of Egypt’s truly international power is the inscription on the Karnak stela erected by Thutmose III to celebrate the victories he won, year after year, along the road through Gaza, Megiddo, Kadesh, Carchemish, and the Euphrates between roughly 1480 and 1475 BCE. From Ethiopia to Cilicia, his protectorate was then unchallenged.

God declares to him on the stela: “By decree I assign to you the land in all its breadth… I have come and granted you the power to crush the land of the West, so that Keftiu and Cyprus stand in terror of you… I have come and granted you the power to crush those on their frontiers, so that the lands of Mitanni tremble in fear… I have come and granted you the power to crush those who dwell in the islands… I grant you the power to crush the Tjemehu, the Herusha, the Nomads, and the Nubians as far as the land of Punt…”

Some scholars have thought they could identify among the peoples named on this stela Greeks, Libyans, and inhabitants of the western Mediterranean. If so, we cannot entirely exclude the possibility that, in the middle of the second millennium BCE, Egyptian power extended over peoples whom Homer would later call Achaeans.

There is another curious detail. The mythical founder and king of Argos was said to be Danaus, a “king of Egypt.” Among his descendants was Agamemnon, the tragic hero of the Trojan War. The Greeks of Herodotus’s day also made Thutmose III the centre of one of their strangest legends. The two colossal statues guarding the pharaoh’s temple near Gournah were identified by them as Memnon, son of Dawn and brother of Priam, king of Troy, who himself ruled over the Ethiopians.

They also believed that the Persian city of Susa was sacred to Memnon. Tradition placed his birth variously in Syria, Anatolia, or Upper Egypt. A characteristically Arab hero in the sense used throughout this book, he was said to have been buried on the Asian shore of the Dardanelles. Every year, legend said, flocks of birds — the immortal souls of his companions — gathered there to mourn him, divided into two hostile groups that ended by fighting each other to the death.

That a single legend could take root across the whole civilized world — in Susa, Thebes, Greece, and the Hellespont — shows both the cultural unity of the period and the unquestioned reach of pharaonic Egypt.

But by the time the pharaohs of the Nineteenth Dynasty, Seti I and Rameses II, once again led their armies along the road to Gaza and Kadesh, the balance of power had changed. North of Carchemish, the empire of the Khatti — the Hittite-Mitannian world — had become strong enough to offer serious resistance.

Rameses II, though victorious, still had to compromise. He concluded an alliance with the Hittite ruler Hattusili, sealed by marriage to the ruler’s eldest daughter, who for the occasion became queen under the ritual name Ourimaounofirouri, “She Who Beholds the Sun in His Beauty.”

The second victory at Kadesh was celebrated in the great epic poem traditionally associated with Pentaur. From it we learn that among the Hittites fighting against Egypt were “the chiefs of Arwad, Lycia, Ilion, the Dardanians, Carchemish…” Ilion! Troy and the Dardanians are already before us. Here, before our eyes, are unmistakable signs of a Trojan War, complete with the Arab background that our classical historians have passed over in silence.

In any case, Egypt remained master of Sinai and the Arabian Peninsula, of the Jordan territories, and of the Palestinian coast from Gaza to the sources of the Orontes. It kept this position despite several invasions.

After the death of Rameses II, his thirteenth son Merneptah came to the throne. A Libyan king named Meryey, who ruled a territory stretching from the Faiyum to the shores of the Syrtes, attacked Egypt and occupied part of the Delta. Curiously, he brought with him Achaeans, Sicilians, Lycians, and other western peoples bearing the same names as those who had appeared at Kadesh. This might suggest that geography did not necessarily determine a people’s name and identity, unless a large Asian colonial population had already existed for a long time in the western Mediterranean and included the Achaeans.

No one has yet managed to give a precise identity to the different contingents of what is called Meryey’s Libyan army. Defeated, it scattered in flight.

The victory stela carved at Thebes around 1229 BCE in honour of Merneptah strangely presents the Libyan defeat as though it were also an Asian one. Its text reads: “Since the Libyans have been crushed, no one among the Nomads raises his head; Khatti is at peace; Canaan is captive in all its wickedness; the man of Ashkelon is carried away; Gezer is taken; Israilu is laid waste and has no seed; Kharu has become a widow of Egypt.”

Some commentators have eagerly seized on the word “Israilu” as irrefutable proof of the existence of a kingdom of Israel. In fact, the meaning of the word escapes any secure etymology, especially because it is what grammarians call a *hapax legomenon*: a word found only once in the surviving corpus of inscriptions.

From the second millennium BCE down to the Ptolemaic period, the land and population of Palestine are repeatedly designated by terms such as Omri or Amurru. It is therefore impossible, today, to assign a precise ethnic or geographical meaning to “Israilu.”

This first invasion was followed by a second and larger movement, which looks less like an invasion than a migration from north to south. It brought together peoples of many origins, named in the sources as Tjeker, Denyen, Peleset, and others; Egyptologists commonly identify the Peleset with the Philistines of the Bible. The Hittite Empire seems to have broken apart in their path.

But were these really foreign invasions, or were some of these upheavals local revolts? It is pure supposition to see in them the settlement of Indo-European tribes in “Semitic” lands. Nothing allows us to make the Philistines ethnically different from the peoples of Egypt or Mitanni. Were they even so foreign? Is the phonetic and semantic distance between “Phoenicians” and “Philistines” really so great? Or are we once again willing victims of our biblical prejudices?

Rameses III launched a successful offensive against these supposed Philistines and “Sea Peoples.” Around 1190 BCE, Egyptian troops once again reached Naharaina near Carchemish. The prisoner lists carved at Medinet Habu contain many of the same names found in the age of Thutmose III and Rameses II: people from Carchemish, Arwad, Mitanni, the Mannu, the Khatti, and others whom the pharaoh delivered to the wrath of Amun.

Egyptian power would show a similar continuity through the dynasties that followed, including the Hellenistic dynasty of the Ptolemies. Administrative capitals might move from Thebes to Tanis, Bubastis, or Mendes, but the great sacred centres of Memphis and Thebes remained important. Pharaohs of Libyan or Ethiopian origin continued to maintain and extend Egyptian influence up the Nile and toward Syria.

Palestine in particular never possessed a sovereignty of its own. To this day we have not discovered a single fragment or allusion that would allow us to speak of a Hebrew capital or Hebrew kings. Nowhere is the name of David or Solomon inscribed; nowhere do we find the great Jewish conquests celebrated in the Old Testament. The pharaonic chancery, usually so eager to record the region’s major political and military events in detail, is completely silent on them.

How, then, can we see the Old Testament as anything more than a poem, like the epic of Troy, built from local quarrels and village struggles between one branch of a tribe and another, one god and another, one landowner and another — each promoted for the occasion to the rank of “king”? History gives David and Solomon little more reality than Achilles or Odysseus, and the poor island of Ithaca mattered no more to the great powers of the age than Jerusalem or Shechem.

It is strange that biblical documents written in Greek some thirteen to fifteen centuries after the events they are supposed to describe are still used today as unquestioned references by the most serious scholars. Chronicles, Kings, and Samuel continue to be cited as though they were photographs of actual people and places. Only when the Old Testament ceases to feed our historical scholarship will our interpretation of the East be freed from the grip of these prejudices.

From the Twenty-First and Twenty-Second Dynasties onward, around 900 BCE, Egyptologists often speak at once of decline, imperial expansion, political crisis, and above all a growing influx of immigrants from every direction, as though Egypt were being submerged beneath an international tide.

They point in particular to large numbers of Meshwesh, often shortened to Ma: mercenaries, workers, and officials, “people from everywhere” who supposedly replaced a native, purely Egyptian aristocratic ruling class. That idea is completely foreign to the tolerance of an East that is international by its very nature. There were never any “pure Egyptians,” any more than there were “pure Babylonians.” Even in the supposedly purest periods of Egyptian history, people were constantly moving in and out, with immigrants as numerous as emigrants.

The very ideas of ethnic or religious “purity” were born in Europe with the Enlightenment, with a supposed rationalism that claimed to be universal and ecumenical while paradoxically digging deadly boundaries between different races and religions. Scholastic rigidity is responsible for a profound distortion in our historical thinking.

Take, for example, the standard description of an Assyrian “conquest” of Egypt around 670 BCE, when Taharqa of the Twenty-Fifth Dynasty ruled at Tanis. Esarhaddon, king of Assyria, was angered by revolts that Egypt encouraged against him in Palestine and Mesopotamia — which shows that Egypt had hardly lost its power. He therefore intervened in the Nile valley.

We are told that he crossed the desert, avoiding the populous and well-defended Delta, hurried to seize Memphis, failed before Thebes, and then quickly returned home. Was this a conquest, or simply a military raid? His son Ashurbanipal had much the same experience. He entered Egypt in 666 and soon left again; he returned in 664 with no lasting success. A century later Nebuchadnezzar would not even reach the Nile. Cambyses of Persia did establish a protectorate over Egypt in 525, but it was largely a formality, since the pharaoh Nectanebo was still ruling in 360. Under such conditions, can we really speak of a “conquest”?

We are as well informed about Mesopotamia as we are about Egypt, thanks partly to the great cuneiform libraries discovered at Nineveh and elsewhere, and partly to the chronicles of the Babylonian priest Berossus. At the request of the Seleucid ruler Antiochus I, Berossus wrote a royal history in the third century BCE. Much of it has been lost, and what survives is as bare as the Egyptian chronicles of the priest Manetho, written at roughly the same time for Ptolemy.

Without getting lost in the legendary mist surrounding Semiramis and Ninus, one thing stands out: the histories of Egypt and Mesopotamia seem almost symmetrical. Thebes and Babylon act as two poles of a single, coherent world. Both civilizations grew on great rivers across broad alluvial plains. In both cases, northern and southern regions eventually united into a single political order. Egypt had Upper and Lower Egypt, symbolized in the pharaoh’s double crown. Mesopotamia had a northern Assyria and a southern Chaldea, also called Babylonia.

Notice, in passing, the obvious sound resemblance between *Syria* and *Assyria*: the second may simply be the first with a definite article attached. Nineveh was the capital of Assyria; Babylon the capital of Chaldea. Each dominated in turn, but both ruled populations governed by broadly similar laws, customs, and culture. The main difference was that lowland Babylonia retained a more strongly priestly and sacred character than the Assyrian highlands.

This remains true in Iraq today: Najaf and Karbala, south of the ruins of ancient Babylon, contain sacred cemeteries where Shi'a Muslims from every continent still bring their dead for burial.

It is tempting, then, to picture the ancient world as two great half-circles meeting along roughly the fortieth meridian: the Egyptian Empire to the west and the Assyro-Babylonian Empire to the east, with the short-lived Hittite Empire linking them. Their territories were broadly comparable in size, as were their populations. On either side lay the same immense weight of antiquity.

If Egypt, as we believe, awoke sometime in the fifth millennium BCE, excavations at Uruk take us securely into the fourth. From there the first Chaldean empire emerges, followed in the traditional chronology by the legendary Sargon in the twenty-sixth century and Hammurabi in the nineteenth.

But it is with the first Assyrian Empire, established at Nineveh not long after Thutmose III’s expedition to Carchemish, that relations with Egypt and Mesopotamia’s western policy become clearer. Until then, the Mesopotamian kingdoms had been preoccupied with the powerful rulers of Elam on their eastern frontier.

The Euphrates inevitably gave Mesopotamian policy its direction and continuity. The overriding need was to reach the sea — to connect with Greek and Phoenician shipping and develop commercial and financial links between the Persian Gulf and the Mediterranean. That meant securing free passage through the Carchemish basin and, at the same time, winning the friendly neutrality of the Phoenicians and the peoples of the Orontes valley.

As long as Egypt guaranteed the safety of those communications, the Mesopotamian rulers maintained a profitable alliance with it, joined by the Hittites. But when revolts and migrations disturbed the peace at these crossroads, Assyrian armies took the road to Carchemish, Kadesh, and Sidon, just as the armies of Thutmose III and Rameses II had done before them, around the beginning of the first millennium BCE.

Geography, politics, and war all pushed in the same direction. The pattern would change very little over the centuries. Egypt and Mesopotamia were complementary powers when they cooperated, rival powers when they did not, but both were essential to the political and economic balance of the Near East.

The Assyrian expeditions coincided with the rise of the second Assyrian Empire, which lasted from about 1020 to 625 BCE, nearly four centuries. Step by step, still seeking access to Mediterranean outlets and the cooperation of Aegean and Phoenician fleets then under pharaonic influence, Assyrian armies eventually penetrated into Egypt itself in three waves, in 671, 666, and 664. These campaigns never quite became permanent conquests, but they were powerful military warnings.

To the east, Susa fell to Ashurbanipal in 660. The Assyrian Empire was now at its height. Nimrud, the magnificent palace of Khorsabad, and Sennacherib’s palace at Nineveh became symbols of its power. At Nineveh stood the famous cuneiform library, containing no fewer than thirty thousand clay tablets — a treasure of humanity, much of which is now in the British Museum.

The names of Sargon II, Sardanapalus — remembered as one of history’s great conquerors — and Esarhaddon mark this brilliant period. It is worth noting that in Hittite inscriptions of the period, King Sennacherib is officially called “Master of the Assyrians and the Arabs.”

The second Chaldean Empire, which inherited the power of Nineveh from 625 to 533 BCE, revived the splendour of the earlier Chaldean state, the empire of Hammurabi that had disappeared around 1360. Its greatest name was Nebuchadnezzar, who in turn defeated Egypt, the Canaanite principalities, and Tyre.
