Under him, Babylon became one of the wonders of the world, worthy of the immense fame later generations would give it. It was also worthy of welcoming its next master: Cyrus, who took the city in 533 and made it one of his capitals.

Cyrus, founder of the Achaemenid dynasty and the Persian Empire, had no intention of destroying the Babylonian Empire. Quite the opposite: he meant to strengthen it and inherit all its economic and political rights. Persia had until then stood somewhat outside the main trade route linking India and the Mediterranean. By taking Babylon, Cyrus gained direct control of that route and became a natural partner of the Egyptians, Canaanites, Anatolians, and Greeks, both on the Aegean islands and on the mainland.

He inherited the interests of Sargon, Ashurbanipal, and Nebuchadnezzar, but now backed them with the greater manpower, cavalry, and resources of the Indo-Iranian world. The Achaemenids would accomplish what neither the pharaohs nor the Assyro-Babylonians had managed: bringing Egyptian, Anatolian, Persian, and Mesopotamian power together within a single empire.

Babylon and Memphis fell almost one after the other: Babylon in 533, Memphis in 525 under Cambyses, Cyrus’s son. Sardis, capital of the Anatolian empire that had become the kingdom of Lydia under Gyges, Ardys, Sadyattes, Alyattes, and Croesus, had already been taken in 546.

Yet this imperial sovereignty was largely administrative and nominal. It did not overturn local life or customs. Regional kingdoms remained in place, required only to acknowledge imperial authority. Egypt, for example, kept its pharaohs. They simply ceased to act as “emperors of East and West” and instead became subordinate rulers under Babylon and Persepolis.

The ease with which Cyrus and his family extended royal authority over such enormous territories proves, once again, how similar the region’s religious, legal, and social structures already were. Ordinary people did not necessarily experience a new dynasty as an intolerable foreign intrusion. Whether orders arrived from Memphis, Tyre, or Persepolis, they were issued in the same general administrative world, in familiar languages, under familiar gods, and by officials drawn from the same interconnected provinces.

No corner of the land stood outside administration, and above all none stood outside a culture that had permeated the whole region for thousands of years. The rulers of these empires relied on that shared culture even more than on their soldiers to establish authority from the Mediterranean to the Indus and from Armenia to the Ethiopian coast. The essential thing was not to disturb the cultural inheritance. Politics might agitate royal chanceries, but the life of the population continued.

The accession of the Achaemenids to the throne of Babylon therefore did nothing to change the face of Eastern society. And by Eastern society we mean Greek society as well, since it had long since been absorbed into the Egyptian-Mesopotamian cultural world.

So we arrive at 533 BCE. The old lands from the Nile to the Euphrates are united under one ruler. Two centuries later, beginning with Alexander’s landing in the Troad in 334, these same lands will pass under Greek rule.

The time has therefore come to explain why that transition could occur so smoothly, and why Greek culture, language, politics, and administration had long been deeply Asian — “Semitic,” if we must use that absurd fashionable neologism. The reason is that Arab civilization had shaped Hellenism from the earliest antiquity.

Compared with the vast and almost prehistoric civilizations of Memphis or Uruk, Greek civilization is extremely young. In the form most familiar to us, it barely appears before the seventh century BCE. The oldest Greek inscriptions on stone or pottery are no earlier than this period. The inscription on one of the colossi at Abu Simbel, for example, dates from the age of Psamtik II in the sixth century.

We possess no Greek inscription from the supposed age of Homer, while Egypt and Babylonia had been writing for many centuries. The Prisse Papyrus in the Bibliothèque Nationale, often described as one of the oldest surviving books in the world, was written around 2000 BCE under Egypt’s Twelfth Dynasty; and as early as the Sixth Dynasty there was already an official title meaning “governor of the house of books.”

Greek civilization, of course, did not suddenly come into existence in the seventh century with the first inscriptions written in what we recognize as classical forms. It had long been present in Asia Minor and along the Egyptian-Palestinian coast, woven into an Aramean cultural setting.

By the eleventh century BCE, during the upheavals associated with the Philistines and the collapse of Hittite power, we can clearly see a strategic and cultural alliance between Aegean Greeks and the peoples of Palestine. In the Trojan War, Greeks and Canaanites fight together against the Hittite centre of power.

The same coalition appears again in the seventh century, now joined by the Lydians, in struggles against Cimmerian invaders arriving from Thrace and the Black Sea. The prosperity of the Greek cities of Aeolis and Ionia — Smyrna, Ephesus, Miletus, Halicarnassus — depended both on cooperation with Egypt and Babylon and on the protection of the Anatolian kings.

In Pericles’ day, visitors to Delphi could still see the great treasures of solid gold dedicated by Gyges and Croesus, the wealthy rulers of Lydia whose magnificence haunted Greek literature for centuries.

Greeks were also numerous in Egypt. They mingled with the immigrant Meshwesh of the tenth century and were already mentioned in the annals of Thutmose III in the fifteenth century. By the middle of the seventh century BCE, Pharaoh Psamtik formally recognized them as friends and opened Egypt to them. They would continue to fight in Egypt’s defence until the arrival of Alexander.

Allies of Egypt and Lydia, the Greeks were also allied with the Etruscans. According to Herodotus, the Etruscans came from the Lydian region. Under Atys, son of Manes, they were said to have left their homeland, passed through Smyrna and the Aegean islands, and eventually reached Italy.

The Romans, for their part, claimed descent from the Trojan Aeneas. Taken together, these traditions force us to conclude that the Italian peninsula owed its civilization to Asia Minor.

The founding of Carthage by Greco-Canaanite — that is, Palestinian — colonists in 833 BCE completed the Arab character of the western Mediterranean. Its imprint survives to this day, not only in North Africa but in Sicily and even Venice.

Etruscan writing has not yet been deciphered, but the language it records has a structure identical to Greek, as the seventh-century inscription discovered on Lemnos shows. The Romans themselves did not seem to share our ignorance of the Etruscans. Emperor Claudius was a serious student of Etruscan civilization and wrote twenty-five books on the *Tusci* under the title *Tyrrhenika*. Those works have been completely lost.

According to research associated with the Etruscan Academy of Cortona, founded in 1726, Chaldea and Etruria may have had close ties. Like Cybele, the great mother goddess, Asia gave its blood, culture, wealth, and thought to its children in the West.

The great Greek cities themselves took pride in Asian ancestors. Athens named Erechtheus as a founder figure: he was said to have come from Egypt, introduced the cultivation of wheat to Attica, and saved the country from famine. The very name *Attica* is Arabic, meaning either “ancient” or “old,” or, when used as a woman’s name, “full of wisdom.”

The aristocratic democracy of the Alcmaeonids, made famous by Pericles, was established with Lydian money. Thebes was founded by the Canaanite Cadmus, from Sidon, where his father Agenor was king; Agenor’s mother was named Libya.

Pelops, son of Tantalus, gave his name to the Peloponnese. Born in Anatolia, he was said to have settled in Greece with a group of Phrygian companions whose tombs were still shown in Laconia in historical times. A tradition still alive in the age of Flavius Josephus even made the Spartans relatives of the Arameans.

Among the kings of Argos appears a certain Abas — an unmistakably Arab name — who was said to descend from Aegyptus and Danaus. Danaus himself, one of the strangest figures in Greek legend, was king of Libya before fleeing after an oracle warned him to leave.

The list could go on almost without end. Greek tradition repeatedly traced the founders and namesakes of its cities and peoples back to the lands between the Nile and the Euphrates. Hardly any Greek, Sicilian, or Italian city failed to claim the glory of some Asian ancestor.

We therefore have to move beyond the narrow truths usually taught to us and cross the conventional borders of Athens and Sparta if we want to reach the sources of what we call Western culture. History cannot easily be confined to one place. It is scattered, almost like a vapor, across enormous territories.

Even so, some centres were more active than others, especially where geography allowed their influence to spread more widely and last longer. Palestine and the Aegean, standing at the strategic and cultural intersection of the great empires, matter especially to us.

A language developed there whose grammatical patterns helped shape the languages of Europe. Religions met and blended there that helped form our mental world. Art emerged there whose models travelled to the farthest West. A philosophy took shape there, with metaphysical and legal foundations without which we would not be what we are.

And those centres, which sent both their light and their shadows directly toward us, were themselves continuously fed — with wealth and energy, moral and political currents, science and ideas — by the peoples and immense Arab spaces whose social organization, culture, and quiet, deep self-confidence have survived into the present despite the many divisions imposed on them by historians.

Let us begin with writing. Here we need to be careful. What could a phrase such as “the original edition of Homer’s works” possibly mean? Nothing at all. We must also remember that ancient culture was overwhelmingly oral. Writing remained largely in the hands of three groups: government officials and administrators, priests, and professional scribes.

Written correspondence was, in effect, a code. It was used less for communication within a country than for carrying messages across long distances. Writing therefore served international communication more readily than everyday exchange between people in the same place. That helps explain why ancient writing systems acquired such a wide geographical reach, even when spoken languages remained intensely local.

The literary Greek of Homer, Thucydides, and the Evangelists existed alongside a great number of Greek dialects that were rarely written down in their spoken form: Macedonian, Illyrian, Arcadian, Cretan, Pamphylian, and many others.

The Greeks themselves said that their alphabet came directly from the Phoenicians. Herodotus calls its letters *Phoinikēia grammata*, “Phoenician letters,” and attributes their introduction to the Canaanite Cadmus, who left Sidon and went to found Thebes in Greece.

But we must go farther back, to the two great writing traditions that spread across the East from the third millennium BCE onward: Egyptian and Babylonian. Egyptian writing is preserved in countless monumental inscriptions and papyri. Babylonian writing, in cuneiform, survives above all in the enormous library assembled at Nineveh, but also in inscriptions from Hittite palaces, Canaanite cities such as Byblos and Ugarit — modern Ras Shamra — and even Egyptian sites such as Tell el-Amarna.

Egyptian hieroglyphic writing was known in Babylonia, just as cuneiform was used in Egypt, and both systems reached as far as Elam. Archaeologists repeatedly find the two traditions side by side. They were so deeply intertwined that they eventually produced a common written language, using simplified Egyptian signs, from which Aramaic emerged.

Their survival was much longer than people often assume. Cuneiform continued at least into the Arsacid period. The Hellenistic Seleucids actively encouraged its use and restored the old libraries. It even survived in bilingual texts close to the beginning of the Common Era. At Seleucia on the Tigris, inscriptions dating from around 150 BCE have been found in cuneiform Akkadian accompanied by phonetic transcriptions in Greek capital letters.

Egyptian writing, in its hieratic and demotic forms, was likewise still well understood in the age of Caesar and Augustus.

Aramaic separated itself from these older languages only gradually. Although its recognizable written form seems to have developed in Palestine around the fifteenth century BCE, cuneiform tablets from Sardis, Kabul, Armenia, and the Arabian Peninsula already carried marginal notes in Aramaic before that date. By the fifteenth and sixteenth centuries BCE, under Egypt’s Eighteenth Dynasty, Aramaic was already so widespread that it sometimes becomes impossible to distinguish clearly between what is Hittite, Egyptian, and Aramaic.

At ancient Ugarit — called Goublou or Gebal in ancient Palestinian, and Jubayl in modern Arabic — which boasted of being the oldest city in the world, objects of Sumerian and Egyptian origin were found bearing the name of Menkaure, the pharaoh of the Pyramid. Around the fifteenth century BCE, a cuneiform system appeared there using only about thirty signs instead of the four or five hundred required by older cuneiform. It already foreshadowed the modern alphabet.

Then, again in Palestine, an alphabet of roughly twenty non-cuneiform letters developed in imitation of simplified Egyptian writing. It became the forerunner of the Greek alphabet, the Latin alphabet, and ultimately our European forms of writing. At roughly the same time, Crete developed a script influenced by Egypt.

The Palestinian language and alphabet, drawing on Egyptian and Babylonian roots and forms, thus gave rise to both Greek and Aramaic. The future of Aramaic was extraordinary. By the seventh century BCE it had become the *koine*, the common language, of the vast Asian region between the Nile and the Indus — an Arab space by definition.

The Mediterranean and Asian East soon became broadly bilingual. Greek functioned as a second language among educated circles, while Aramaic remained the first and more stable language. Aramaic never stopped being spoken. It gradually developed into modern Arabic, much as Latin developed into French or Italian.

Greek and Aramaic, closely bound together, therefore carried forward a culture reaching deep into antiquity. Among its later heirs were the two Testaments and Islam.

This bilingualism became so deeply rooted that Greek remained an administrative language for a long time even under the Muslim caliphs. Under the Abbasids, the Barmakid family of statesmen made it their mission to spread Greek works by having them translated into Arabic. Ja'far ibn Yahya, the famous minister of Harun al-Rashid, took a particular interest in the work. Under the caliph al-Ma'mun, Sahl ibn Rabban al-Tabari likewise won recognition for his adaptations from Greek.

Even Ernest Renan, in his now dated study of the so-called “Semitic” languages, noticed the large number of Greek words found in Canaanite languages and the corresponding movement of words in the other direction.

Alexander and his successors strengthened this bilingual culture until it became one of the foundations of intellectual and spiritual life in the East. Alexander had the sacred books of the Persians sought out and translated into Greek and Aramaic. The Ptolemies likewise ordered religious traditions associated with Judaism, Gnosticism, Mazdaism, and other schools to be collected and transcribed in both languages.

Alexandria became the capital of this Greek-Aramaic bilingual world and, with it, of the encyclopaedic ambition of antiquity. Pliny the Elder, in Book 30 of the *Natural History*, even claimed that the library of Alexandria contained three million verses of Zoroaster transcribed into Aramaic.

If, as classical Greek philosophers believed, Zoroaster’s teaching reached back to the sixth millennium BCE, it is hard not to feel dizzy at the sheer amount of labour accumulated by Alexandria’s translators and linguists.

The Seleucids were doing much the same. While ordering the restoration of the temple of Nabu at Borsippa and the Esagila, the temple of Marduk at Babylon, Antiochus commissioned Berossus, priest of Baal, to write a history of Mesopotamia in Greek.

All this could only happen if Greek — in its structure, habits of thought, and vocabulary — was fully understood and absorbed by the peoples of the East. But that required a second condition in return: Greek itself had to absorb the character of the East. The two processes met and reinforced one another.

How could it have been otherwise? How can one people adopt another people’s alphabet unless they already share deep affinities of thought and culture?

In truth, the land called Phoenicia — nothing more than a province of Canaan, later Palestine — was a powerful Greek-Arab cultural centre long before Athens, insofar as terms such as “Greek” and “Arab” can even be cleanly separated within a world where the two were so closely intertwined.

Greek is as much an Arab language as Arabic is a Greek one, with one important difference: Greek was primarily a language through which an older inheritance was recast, while the main cultural, scientific, and religious substance came from the Arabs.

We should therefore not reverse the roles and turn the Greeks, who were heirs, into the fathers of their own spiritual ancestors.

Faced with the approaching collapse of our cultural framework, which conventional formulas will no longer be enough to preserve, we must remember that salvation can come only from renewing our faith in our own values. But that faith first requires truth and honesty.

With apologies to the scholars, the archives of philology are packed with theories that are ingenious rather than well founded. The study of Greek language and Greek history must be taken back to its roots.

The map of Greece should not stop at the eastern shore of the Peloponnese. It must be extended much farther, to include Palestine, Phrygia, Egypt, and Syria. Above all, scholars must stop being afraid to speak of Arabs.

What Hellenist has never wished for a deeper knowledge of the history and languages of the Arab East when trying to understand Homer, Plato, Aeschylus, or Pindar? Europe is sometimes described as merely a cape of Asia. It would be even more accurate to call Greece a district of Asia Minor.

More respectful of the truth than many scholars, major newspapers often place Greece within their Near Eastern desks and assign Greek affairs to journalists who also cover the Arab world. That is wiser than the partial and compartmentalized picture offered by school textbooks.

It would therefore be better to speak of an Aegean civilization than a narrowly Greek one. The influence of the Canaanites of Tyre and Sidon in the Aegean was not merely linguistic. It extends into religion, myth, philosophy, science, and art.

The “islands of the sea” absorbed, through Palestine, the combined culture of the Nile and Mesopotamia. Through Smyrna, Miletus, and Sardis came Anatolian influences, themselves fed by the Indo-Persian interior.

No one can seriously claim to untangle every strand of so complete a synthesis. We can only try, from a distance and imperfectly, to identify its currents “as light enters water without dividing it,” to borrow Dante’s image.

The Greeks called the constellation Ursa Minor *Phoinikē*, “the Phoenician,” because sailors from Tyre and Sidon steered by it on their voyages toward the Pillars of Hercules. The Roman *Punicus* came from *Phoinikos*. The Punic Wars were therefore, in this broad sense, “Arab wars,” an early prelude to the conflicts Europe would wage in North Africa some twenty centuries later.

Let us look more closely at the word “Phoenician.” Where did the Phoenicians come from? According to traditions reported by Herodotus, they had migrated from the Persian Gulf in the third millennium BCE, first toward the Dead Sea and then toward the Mediterranean.

The tradition remained so strong that when Alexander passed through the region and wanted to punish Tyre for resisting him, he is said to have thought of “sending the Phoenicians home” — that is, back to the islands of Bahrain.

At the Twenty-Fifth International Congress of Orientalists, held in Moscow in 1960, the Danish archaeological school presented striking findings from the archipelago. The discovery of roughly one hundred thousand graves forced scholars to rethink the region, where excavations had by then been under way for about twenty years. Such cemeteries, after all, implied cities.

At Ras al-Ouala'a, not far from the modern port of Manama on the western side of the island, seven cities were uncovered one above another, the oldest dating to the third millennium BCE. Nearby, close to the village of Barbar, archaeologists found a sanctuary rich in jewellery, figurines, alabaster vessels, a pool, and sacrificial tables. The pottery from the region is exceptionally beautiful.

The Indo-Sumerian character of these objects and buildings is unmistakable, as is the resemblance between some of the seals and figurines found there and those excavated at Byblos in Phoenicia.

Chaldean commercial archives indeed speak of the rich warehouses of the kingdom of Dilmun, the Sumerian name for Bahrain. It was to Dilmun that Gilgamesh travelled in his search for immortality. And according to the Sumerian flood story, it was at Dilmun that Ziusudra, the sole survivor of the Deluge, came ashore.

Our Noah, then, would have grounded his ark not on Mount Ararat but on Jebel Dukhan, the “Mountain of Smoke,” rising in the centre of Bahrain. Orphic tradition, for its part, also placed there the dwelling of the blessed: the Elysian Fields where Apollo reigns.

As a sacred land of burial, the islands of Bahrain resemble Babylonia, which later became a resting place for the dead of Shi'a Islam. Strikingly, Shi'ism also predominates in Bahrain.

Archaeological exploration of the Arabian Peninsula has only just begun. We can expect many surprises from it, revealing just how dense the web of relationships was that formed the fabric of Arab civilization from the most ancient times.

Phoenicia, then, did not suddenly appear from nowhere, fully equipped with a language, a culture, and a science that it simply projected westward. It arose at the meeting point of countless cultural streams that nourished it deeply. Once it became a source in its own right, it branched out again into countless new currents, carrying Greek communities with them.

Instead of the ambiguous and overly ethnic labels “Phoenician” and “Canaanite,” let us use the safer geographical term and speak from now on of Palestinians. Geographical unity corresponded to cultural unity.

The Babylonians called the country Amurru. Egyptian inscriptions refer to it under names such as Amu, Haru, Kharu, Retenu, and also Amurru. Our procedural obsession has led us to dissect this world into closely related peoples — Moabites, Ammonites, Amorites, Canaanites, Arameans, Syrians, and so on.

Why? Because we are determined to identify separate ethnic and religious groups there in order to carve out a distinct place for the Hebrews and preserve the historical framework of the Old Testament. It is almost as though every branch of scholarship had joined in a single effort: to confirm the historical truth of Scripture.

Yet it is impossible to sort the inhabitants of Amurru into clean ethnic compartments by language, clothing, weapons, religion, or architectural style.

To restore the single geographical name Palestine to this land is therefore not only to follow the safest historical rule, but also to reject the arbitrary divisions of a disoriented scholarship.

It was not one race or one religion that nature somehow “chose,” but the country itself: Palestine. Palestine’s geography gave it an exceptional cultural position within the Mediterranean world. The role of carrying arts and sciences outward belongs to the whole population of the land.

Land of the alphabet, Palestine — together with the islands and mainland of the Aegean — was also one of the great meeting places of religion. Neither Greece nor Judea invented anything. The Egyptian-Babylonian pantheon, passing through Palestine and Anatolia, became the pantheon of the Greeks and Romans. The Greek gods are Asian, just as Judaism, Christianity, and Islam are Arab.

Here we enter a field that has suffered some of the gravest distortions of all. They are especially difficult to correct because the assumptions built into our way of thinking have hardened into almost unshakable falsehoods.

# III

## THE SEVEN PLANETS

One misconception needs clearing up at the outset. We often set Palestinian monotheism against Greco-Roman polytheism, and the invisible, solitary God against gods made of stone or wood. But things are not so simple. Beneath the outward, popular polytheism of ancient religions there was always, and everywhere, a more or less esoteric monotheism. Egyptian and Babylonian hymns celebrate a divinity that remains one beneath its many forms, because it alone is Light, Sun, Eternity, and Peace. The divine is one, just as the sky is one.

People did, of course, approach that divinity by many routes, some more indirect than others, and they gave each route a familiar face. Christianity itself has its Apostles, male and female saints, and intercessors assigned to particular trades, illnesses, sins, and hopes. Saint Blaise watches over sore throats, Saint Longinus over diseases of the eyes, Saint Euphemia over protection from wild beasts; there are patron saints of hunters, poets, and soldiers. Christians do not worship dozens of gods simply because they have dozens of saints. At God’s feet, the saints surround him, but they do not replace him.

Strictly speaking, true worship needs no images. Religion is the affirmation that the soul exists and can be close to God, its creator. A statue, whether in Egypt, Delphi, or Olympia, is not the deity. It is not only *not* worshipped as a god; in a sense, it is itself worshipping. It represents a human being or an animal in an attitude of devotion, brought to a state of physical perfection and alertness close to the perfection God intended. The statue worships but is not worshipped. It symbolizes the creature at prayer and gives visible form to attachment to God. Motionless, it turns the posture of the worshipper into something solemn and mystical, offering the crowd an example to follow. Above all, then, the statue expresses a culture, not the deity itself.

“At one moment I am eternal; at another I am in time,” some mystics say, describing the human movement between absolute contemplation and ordinary life. Outward forms and images belong to ordinary practice; in deep contemplation, they fade away. Compare the *Hymn to the Sun* of Amenhotep IV with Francis of Assisi’s *Canticle of the Sun*, and the supposedly clear boundary between polytheism and monotheism begins to disappear before our eyes.

No religion, then, is inherently more or less monotheistic than another. What differs are the aesthetic forms through which the divine is imagined and expressed. At the heart of religion there is not an image but faith. When religion becomes identified with the image, it dies into superstition, because superstition creates nothing. The extraordinary vitality and endurance of the religions of antiquity are proof enough that they were far more than collections of images. Islam did not overturn this tradition; it brought it to completion. It seized on the heart of the Arab tradition and tried to express it in full.

What was that tradition? We need to move carefully here, because it is hard to impose order on a world that did not possess much of it. People moved with their animals and their gods. They adopted new gods, abandoned others for a season or a lifetime, exchanged them, imported them, and exported them, all while believing that the stars in heaven remained fixed and that the divine was everywhere, sovereign in every land. So what did the Egyptian-Babylonian pantheon look like at its beginnings, and what did it become in the Palestinian and Anatolian forms known to us?

The Egyptians were obsessed with the world beyond this one. Their great concern was resurrection. They wanted life beyond life itself. Open to every force of existence, however strange, Egypt welcomed gods and spirits of every kind into its pantheon. There was hardly a Greek, African, or Asian deity that did not find a sanctuary somewhere along the Nile.

The Ptolemies, heirs of Alexander, remained true to this outlook. They greeted the Jewish religion with the same respect — or the same scepticism — with which earlier generations had welcomed Indian, Mesopotamian, and Ethiopian cults. Details, deviations, wrong turns, doctrinal errors: none of these mattered much. With his eyes fixed on the sun of resurrection, the Egyptian trusted that the ocean of life would carry every person of good will toward it.

The book of wisdom we call the *Book of the Dead* was in fact called the *Book of Coming Forth by Day*, or the *Book of the Birth of Day*. It is worth noting that the Qur’anic sura traditionally read at the bedside of the dying is called “The Dawn.” The *Book of the Dead* is therefore a continuing song of life addressed to the one God who, under the names Ra, Amun, Aten, son of Nut — Night — rules and will rule forever: the Sun. Here is a revealing passage:

> “Your holiness, O Sun, has no master. You are the great traveller through space; to you, millions and hundreds of millions of years are only an instant. You set, yet you remain. Hours, days, and nights: you multiply them in equal measure and endure according to your own laws. You illuminate the earth, offering yourself with your own hands in the form of Ra. Rising star, great in your blazing splendour, you shape your own limbs and give birth to yourself, unborn, upon the horizon. O high Light of Heaven, grant that I may reach the heights of heaven for all eternity…”

Even a brief look at this passage shows how many themes later religions would make their own. This one God, eternal Father and almighty ruler, is nevertheless accompanied by a trinity: Osiris — who is none other than Amun-Ra himself at the evening hour — his wife Isis, and their falcon-headed son Horus. Osiris, the eternal sacrificial victim, is killed each evening by his enemy and brother Seth, mourned by his wife, and glorified at every dawn through his resurrection. One God in three persons, killed by his closest relative and rising from the dead: what material for Christian meditation.

The drama of Osiris spread throughout the East, and its mystical echoes would sound for centuries through the many themes of Gnosticism and of both revealed and secret religions. Around Father, Mother, and Son, gathered in a tragedy that ends in victory, stand a host of sacred figures: companions, attendants, apostles of many kinds.

Among them is the strange Apis bull, kept by the priests in the temple at Memphis. He was said to be born from a mother impregnated by lightning. When he died, all Egypt went into mourning. Identified with Osiris, he became Osiris-Apis, shortened by the Greeks to Serapis. Herodotus, without telling us that the bull is an allegorical figure, describes him as follows: “Apis is a young bull whose mother can never bear another calf. The Egyptians say that a flash of lightning descends from heaven upon her, and from that lightning she conceives the god Apis. The young bull can be recognized by certain marks. His coat is black; on his forehead he bears a white triangular mark, on his back the figure of an eagle, and beneath his tongue that of a scarab…” (translated by Gustave Le Bon, 1889).

The companions of the divine trinity include Tahout, its messenger and scribe; Astarte, the star goddess; Ptah, represented by the scarab; Thoth, by the ibis and the baboon; and Anubis, by the jackal.

But there is also an enemy: the serpent Apap, embodiment of evil, trampled beneath the feet of the righteous, forever killed and forever returning. Coiled around the earth, it overcomes every contradiction by joining opposite realities together through the simple act of bringing its head to its tail. Curled back upon itself, it becomes a symbol of alpha and omega, of the higher principle through which spirit and body are reconciled in negation. It is therefore Death, since the universe can continue to live only through the tension between thesis and antithesis.

On earth this cosmic serpent is represented by the viper of the tombs. A chapel was built for it near Karnak, where it reigned as queen of the “supreme West.” The people called it Maritsakro, “the Friend of Silence.” Its serpent body bore the head of a woman, or else ended in three serpent heads, as though to set a dark trinity against the holy trinity of Ra.

Remember these images and symbols of the Nile: the Sun, the Bull, the Serpent, the Mother made pregnant by Heaven, and also the Ram. We will meet them again. Already we can make out, as if through a veil, Zeus-Ammon, Apollo and the serpent, the Cretan Bull, Artemis Taurobolos, Mithra, and themes that would later become familiar in the Old and New Testaments.

It would be easy — and tempting — to construct a neat chain of argument explaining Christianity through Egyptian religion alone. The Gospels themselves offer an obvious opening in the journey of the Holy Family into Egypt. Then there is the ankh, the looped cross and symbol of eternal life, repeated throughout the tombs of the Valley of the Kings. There is also the Chaldean cross, adopted in Egypt as well: a cross enclosed within a circle, symbolizing victory and the perfection of the four horizons beneath the curve of heaven.

The modern explanation that the cross represents a slave’s punishment and the suffering of the humble does not match the interpretation Eastern peoples have always given it, for whom the cross represents cosmic power. But we need not follow such disputed explanations any further. It is enough to see how many broad features of Egyptian religion invite comparison by interpreters of the Bible.

Akhenaten, formerly Amenhotep IV, is often said to have launched a true religious revolution, with his capital at Amarna becoming a kind of first Vatican of monotheism, set against the polytheism of Amun. Nothing supports this picture. It owes more to the romantic imagination of certain Egyptologists. Akhenaten changed nothing. His hymn to the sun Aten is little more than a paraphrase of the *Book of the Dead*. He did not overturn Egyptian doctrine, for the simple reason that a religion already rising to the level of Eternity, the denial of the ego, and universal purity had little to subtract from or add to its own perfect serenity.

Egyptian religion had stripped human destiny of its terror and placed it on “the paths of Tranquillity.” This concern with peace, trust, and gentleness has remained part of Egypt down to the present day. When the soul of the dead defends itself before the divine court that weighs merits against sins, it says:

> “I have not treacherously harmed anyone; I have not made those close to me unhappy; I have not left anyone hungry; I have not killed; I have not made anyone weep; I have not lied; I have not taken milk from the mouth of an infant; I have not held back the waters at the time of the flood; I have not put out the flame before its time…”

And the court replies with satisfaction:

> “What he has done, men proclaim and the gods rejoice in. Through his love he has won God’s favour. He gave bread to the hungry, water to the thirsty, clothing to the naked. He gave a boat to the man who had none…” (*Book of the Dead*, ch. CXXV.)

Do these texts, so full of charity, not anticipate the Commandments and the Gospel Beatitudes by fifteen hundred years? And the meeting with Eternity, understood as a true rejoicing of the soul, finds an echo in the words of the Persian mystic poet Jalal al-Din Rumi as he lay dying: “Now you should rejoice, because today is my wedding night with Eternity.”

From one end of the East to the other, then, the quest is the same. At the threshold of death, the Arab spirit of charity asks us to lay aside all fear and prepare for the cyclical journey among the constellations with God, who, like us, dies and rises again in Osiris. Dante takes up the same invitation at the end of each of the three books of his *Divine Comedy*.

The same religion of the stars appears in Chaldea and Assyria, though coloured there by a sharper melancholy and a more painful awareness of the terrible struggle awaiting humanity in the conquest of this world and the next. We know the Mesopotamian faith and its gods in great detail from hundreds of thousands of cuneiform texts, as well as from Greek writers such as Diodorus of Sicily:

“The Chaldeans teach that the world is eternal by nature: since it never had a beginning, it will never have an end. According to their philosophy, the order and arrangement of matter are governed by divine law; nothing that happens in heaven is the result of chance; everything is accomplished through the unchanging and sovereign will of the gods.”

Later, the Greek Heraclitus would say that “there are indeed laws of chance,” while the Stoics would add that “everything is accomplished through the will of the supreme Master.”

Here, as in Egypt, there is an Eternal One, a sovereign and perfect God. He is called El or Al, an elementary root that leads easily to Allah, but also to the early Greek *Hel* and the classical Greek *Helios*, the Sun. We find the same root in the name of the ancient Iranian kingdom of Elam, where the word *Islam* already seems to show through.

Then come twelve great gods, corresponding to the divisions of the zodiac. Grouped into trinities, they correspond to the fixed stars or the Elements. Around El, the Eternal One, Anu and Ea form a sovereign ruling triad, much as Hades and Poseidon stand around Zeus, governing the realms of earth and sea.

Three figures dominate among the other gods: Marduk, son of Ea; Shamash, the Sun; and Ishtar, both evening star and morning star, mother and lover, the bisexual goddess above all others, whose name recalls the Sanskrit *Star* and the Greek *Aster*, the star. These are the great forces that drive the universe. They would return again and again in the myths that gradually flooded the whole of the West.

Babylonian cosmology is no less remarkable. It is a drama in three acts — creation of the world, the Flood, and the salvation of humanity — whose most striking themes reappear in the biblical Genesis, in the *Theogony* of the Greek poet Hesiod, whose father came from Asia Minor, and also in the Qur’an, which has been said to adopt a cosmology like that described in cuneiform tablets from the third, second, and first millennia before our era and in the chronicles of the priest Berossus.

In the beginning was Chaos, from which two pairs of gods emerged. From the second pair came Anu, god of earth and sky, and Ea, god of the ocean. Ea’s son Marduk defeated Chaos and created humanity from a mixture of blood and mud. Early humanity was then taught the sciences by a supernatural being named Oannes, who rose from the sea: a fish with a human head, amphibious by nature, skilled in sowing, crafts, architecture, and law. He established the kings who ruled before the Flood, the first of whom, Aloros, was said to have reigned for 3,600 years.

When humankind angered the god El, he opened the gates of heaven and unleashed the Flood, which swallowed humanity except for the tenth king, Utnapishtim, and his wife. Carried to a favoured region of the earth, they went on to have many descendants.

There is no need to labour the obvious resemblance between the biblical Jonah and the Sumerian Oannes, or the parallels between these stories of humanity’s beginnings and the lists of kings and patriarchs that the Old Testament seems to have drawn from Mesopotamian memory. But one coincidence is worth noticing. At the very time when the Seleucid dynasty commissioned the priest Berossus to write his fabulous *Chronicles* in Greek and restored libraries of cuneiform texts, the Ptolemies were asking their own scholars to gather the biblical traditions. A coincidence of time; a coincidence of purpose; a coincidence in the revival of ancient myths.

The twilight femininity of the Egyptian Isis has its counterpart in Mesopotamia in the extraordinary Ishtar. Like Isis, she represents inheritance and motherhood: the creative power of time, the poetry that shapes life, the eternal return. Like Isis, she is the Mourner, the one who understands Death and comes close to it. Above all, she loves humanity because in every living creature she can already see Death growing alongside life itself, feeding on the whole of its substance, even on its joys.

A goddess of death and of supreme compassion, Ishtar is also the consoler. She gives physical love without restraint, trying — without illusion — to make of it an abundance great enough to overflow Death itself.

Ishtar is therefore a goddess of ambiguity. Her roles and attributes are almost endless. She is everywhere. There is no drama in which she is not a hidden or triumphant driving force. A power of cohesion and connection, she is the goddess of desires frustrated and fulfilled. She is that portion of Death dwelling in every creature, tinting even its greatest joy with tragedy. Such is Ishtar.

Aphrodite and Venus repeat her character. As the marriage of soul and body, she contains the couple within herself; she *is* the couple. How could she know love so intimately if she did not possess both its female and its male experience? She possesses both. In this sense she is Opposition, Contradiction, and Synthesis; in this sense she stands above all male divinities. Goddess of prostitutes, she would also become the patron of the Caesars of Rome. Because she is the star that announces the morning and rises again with evening, through her darkness shines and silence speaks.

Mesopotamian religion brought Ishtar’s character and worship to their fullest expression, as this hymn shows:

> “The female star is the planet Ishtar; she is female at sunset. The male star is the planet Ishtar; she is male at sunrise. At sunrise, Shamash is both her master and her son. At sunrise she is the goddess of Agade. At sunset she is the goddess of Uruk. At sunrise her name among the stars is Ishtar. At sunset her name among the gods is Belit.”

As we can see, the duality of nature is wrapped in an ambiguous and mysterious song whose meaning is understood only by initiates. Ishtar remains a secret path. Love, in all its forms, retains something untouchable and sacred.

Herodotus makes a revealing remark on this point, stressing the continuity of Assyrian custom: “When a Babylonian man has slept with his wife,” he says, “he burns incense and sits beside her. His wife does the same. At daybreak they both wash themselves, for they are permitted to do nothing until they have washed.” He then adds: “The Arabs observe the same custom.”

One of the finest and least damaged Mesopotamian stories tells of Ishtar’s descent into the Underworld to bring back her son — and lover — Tammuz. The queen of the “land of sorrow” receives her with little enthusiasm and cannot understand such a strange visit among the dead,

> “who are like cut grass, while the living are bronze; who are like the withered plant, while the living are the tree in flower.”

Ishtar must pass through seven gates. At each threshold she leaves behind one ornament or piece of clothing, until she crosses the seventh gate naked. Once in the depths of the Underworld, the goddess is imprisoned and struck by terrible afflictions. With her joy removed from the world, the earth itself falls into mourning: crops die, and humans and animals refuse to mate.

To save the universe, Ea, god of the ocean, quickly sends a messenger to the queen of Darkness and orders her to release Ishtar. Reluctantly, she gives her minister this command:

> “Go into the dwelling of eternity; hide the tablets that hold the knowledge of the future. Give Ishtar the waters of life to drink, and take her from my sight.”

So it was done. The earth turned green again, the trees flowered, and couples came together once more. Ishtar recovered her clothes and her form. But the story does not tell us what became of her son Tammuz.

The Greeks would take up the same story in the *Hymn to Demeter*, except that the goddess now goes in search not of a son but of her daughter Persephone. More broadly, the myth of a descent into the Underworld contains both an imagery and a mystical meaning that would pass, step by step, into Mediterranean religion and art and eventually help inspire Dante.

According to Herodotus, the Persians gave Ishtar the name Mithra. If that were true, it would open new possibilities for the study of the Mysteries. In any case, we know that even before the rise of Cyrus, all the great Mesopotamian gods were present in the Persian pantheon, just as they had already found a place in the Egyptian one.

Another source of countless Greco-Roman, biblical, and Islamic themes is the legend of Gilgamesh. We know it from texts dating to the seventh, fourteenth, and eighteenth centuries before our era, discovered variously in Assyria, Anatolia, and Egypt — a sign of how widely the story travelled.

An epic in twelve songs — there are twelve signs of the zodiac — it alternates prayers, tales, battles, and religious allegories. It tells of the labours of Gilgamesh, king of Uruk, sometimes alone and sometimes accompanied by his friend and comrade-in-arms Enkidu. Since the story is already widely known, we can reduce it to five main acts.

**Act One.** Gilgamesh, a great hunter and destroyer of evil, travels with Enkidu to the Land of Cedars — Lebanon? — where he cuts off the head of a monster terrorizing the people.

**Act Two.** He kills the Bull of Heaven, another monster sent by the gods to torment humanity, tears out its heart, and offers it to the Sun. Ishtar then curses him and secures from heaven the death of Enkidu.

**Act Three.** Gilgamesh sets out in search of “the plant of eternal life” held by his ancestor Utnapishtim — the Noah of the Bible? — who has lived in the land “at the mouth of the rivers” since surviving the Flood. Gilgamesh crosses Mount Mashu, guarded by scorpions, and reaches the wonderful garden of the nymph Sabitu, who tries to make him stay:

> “The life you are looking for, you will not find. When the gods created human beings, they made them mortal… Make use, then, of the breath they have given you; think only of rejoicing day and night.”

Like Odysseus escaping Calypso, Gilgamesh rejects this seductive *carpe diem*, builds himself a raft, and heads toward the Mouth of the Rivers.

**Act Four.** After a dangerous crossing, he reaches his immortal ancestor Utnapishtim, who tries in vain to turn him away from his quest. At last Utnapishtim tells him of a garden at the bottom of the sea where “the plant of Eternity” grows. With two stones weighing down his feet, Gilgamesh descends, tears a branch from the tree of life, and rises again in triumph.

**Act Five.** The return to Uruk ends badly. While Gilgamesh bends over a spring to drink, a serpent that has been watching him seizes the branch and carries it away. Now certain that death awaits him, and jealous of the serpent that will enjoy eternal youth, Gilgamesh gives himself over to sorrow.

Little comment is needed, so obvious are the borrowings that priests, poets, and storytellers could make from this legend, which was known in Greece from an early date. Numerous Arabic terms also appear in the Assyro-Babylonian text. The name of the goddess Sabitu, for example, has a modern Arabic ring to it, while Amesh remains a common personal name in Iraq today.

The great light of Mazdaism, which had long shone in Persia since the ancient preaching of Zoroaster, could hardly fail to illuminate Mesopotamia as well. It was a highly purified and abstract religion centred entirely on the worship of a single God, Ahura Mazda, or Ormuzd, also called the Eternal One, and worshipped without temple or image. Creator of the universe, omnipresent and almighty, he might just as easily have been called Amun-Ra, El, Allah, or Jehovah.

Opposite him stood the demon Angra Mainyu, or Ahriman, and the struggle between them was endless. This dualism, which would develop into Manichaeism in the third century of our era and also recalls the eternal struggle between Ra and Seth in Egypt, left a deep mark on Mesopotamian thought. With Mazdaism came the worship of fire:

> O Fire, supreme Lord who rises in this land!  
> Hero, son of the ocean, who rises in this land!  
> Fire, through the brilliance of your flame you carry light into  
> the dwelling of Darkness…

So proclaims an Assyrian hymn. Another song, filled even more with submission than celebration, murmurs the misery of a human being bowed beneath the light of the God who alone can absolve him:

> Lord, let the anger of your heart be calmed!  
> Let this god whom I do not know be calmed!  
> Without knowing it, I have fed on disobedience, O my god.  
> Without knowing it, I have walked against my goddess.  
> Lord, many are my faults and great are my sins…  
> Let the wind carry away the wrongs I have committed.  
> Tear apart the blasphemies I have spoken like a veil.  
> My sins, O my god, which are seven times seven, absolve them.  
> Forgive my faults; guide the one who submits to you.  
> And let your heart grow calm, like a mother beside her child.
>
> (LE BON, p. 565.)

How far is this prayer from the *Book of Job* or the Psalms? More striking still is the lament of King Tabi-Utul-Eulil of the land of Nippur, preserved on the tablets of Nineveh: “My eyes are darkened and seem locked shut; I spend the night in my dung like an ox; like a sheep, I lie among my own excrement.”

And what are we to make of this other formula, thoroughly Abrahamic and Christian in tone, also found on the tablets of Nineveh: “The lamb takes the place of the man; it gives its life for him.” Tammuz, the Victim and the Risen One, is called the Fragrant One, the Anointed of the Lord.

It is therefore far beyond Judaism and Christianity, at the very sources of Arab mysticism, that we must seek the origins of the Western mind.

Waves of belief flowed into Canaanite Palestine from Babylon, Syria, and Egypt. Over the centuries they settled layer upon layer, forming the cultural ground from which settlers, missionaries, soldiers, orators, singers, heroes, bearers of names and gods, sailors, engineers, bankers, and merchants spread out in every direction. The Palestinian fleets of Tyre and Sidon sailed beyond Spain and the Pillars of Hercules as far as Cornwall, returning with tin. Amber caravans set out from the Baltic shores, followed the Rhine and Rhône, and reached the ports of Provence or Italy.

Other caravans came north from central Africa toward Palestinian trading depots in Libya and Tunisia. Along all these routes travelled the civilization of Canaan. In his book on *The Oriental Religions in Roman Paganism*, Franz Cumont notes that “from the mountains of Asturias to the mouths of the Danube, the Syrians spread the cults of Adonis, Attis, Baal, Cybele…” Switzerland itself preserves many traces of this influence. Sion, for example, has the ruins of a sanctuary of Cybele.

Gaul, Spain, Holland, and Africa therefore knew Isis, Ishtar, Yahweh, El, divine sons, and the doctrine of resurrection long before Greek or Roman expansion reached its height. The destruction of Herod’s Temple was not needed for communities devoted to Osiris, Judaism, or Marduk to appear in the West. Every day we discover striking affinities between Celtic and Palestinian religions, between the Druids and the sanctuaries of the Orontes or Judea. Was the Gallic god Belem really a different figure from Baal? The East and Greece founded schools of thought and converted whole peoples in the West.

But the presence of worshippers of Baal, Yahweh, Jesus, or Islam in Gaul, North Africa, or Italy does not mean those people came from Palestine. There are followers of Plato all over the world; does that make Athens their homeland? Confusing nationality with religion — a confusion that produced so many discriminatory theories in Europe — comes either from ignorance of the most basic facts of history or from deliberate deception. To claim that a German or French person should be sent back to Palestine because they practise a religion born there is simply absurd, since nearly all our religions developed in Palestine.

Sending every Muslim in the world back to Mecca would create problems no less impossible.

The religious entanglement of ancient Palestine is almost beyond imagination, and it was matched by an equally tangled population. Greeks, Anatolians, Africans, and Mesopotamians lived side by side, speaking Aramaic as a common language while continuing to use their own dialects and local speech. They could find their way through this world; we cannot. Time has blurred the tracks still further. Excavations at Byblos, Arwad, Amrit, and Tyre have brought to light figures of Isis, Horus, and Osiris, but also Ishtar, and above all evidence of a highly complex blended religion whose main themes can be recovered from the tablets of Ras Shamra.

Its account of the cosmos is the same as that of Assyria and Babylonia and belongs to the same world as the *Epic of Gilgamesh*. The supreme god remains El, or Al, worshipped everywhere and especially in standing stones or *betyls* — *beit-El*, “houses of El.” He has delegated his powers on earth to three divinities: Danel, Keret, and Baal. Danel rules agriculture and the people of the Rephaim. Who were the Rephaim? Giants? Angels? Servants? They are not unknown to the Old Testament. The root *rapha* also appears in Raphael and Raphabaal.

If we turn to Arabic, it could mean “to raise toward heaven,” “to lift up,” or “to exalt”; Raphael and Raphabaal would then mean roughly “worshipper of El” and “worshipper of Baal.” Keret is a demigod, son of El and Asherah. He marries the daughter of the king of the Edomites, and the wedding described in the Ras Shamra tablets is curiously reminiscent of scenes in the Old Testament concerning the Patriarchs. Baal is lord of all the forces of the earth. He is worshipped everywhere, each city taking him as its patron under such names as Baal-Hermon, Baal-Peor, Baal-Tyre, Baal-Sidon, Baalbek, and so on.

He is invoked as Adonai, “lord”; *Rab*, “father”; and *Malek*, “king, patron, owner” — all classical Arabic words, not Hebrew ones. We will not return to that point here. Baal is anything but a peaceful god. He is always fighting: against Zabel-Yam, prince of the sea, for example, whose ally is Nahar, the River. The conflict has to be judged by El himself and his angels, the *Elim*. Baal has a female counterpart who aids and loves him: his sister, the beautiful Anat, whose name comes from the Arabic word *ayn*, “spring” or “source.” Anat — also called Astarte — is a remarkable figure, a counterpart of Mesopotamian Ishtar.

In Egypt, Ramses II is sometimes described as the “nursling of Anat-Astarte,” and one of his daughters bore a name meaning “daughter of Anat.” Like her brother Baal, the goddess is fierce and warlike, and she is readily compared with Sekhmet, the Egyptian lioness-headed goddess. We will see Anat-Astarte massacre entire peoples because they betrayed the worship of her beloved brother and wash her hands in their blood. She boasts of having “destroyed the seven-headed serpent Litan,” which the Bible seems to have turned into Leviathan; of having “destroyed the dragon Tannin”; and of having “muzzled the bitch of the gods of fire.”

Weeping like Isis, she descends into the Underworld to recover her brother Baal after he is killed in an ambush. There, with the help of the sun, she fights a savage battle against Mot, the god of death, and finally cuts down her brother’s murderer with a sickle, making Baal’s resurrection possible. Anat-Astarte therefore also represents the persistence of life, the power that drives back darkness, purity and purification, the key to resurrection. Her sign is the ankh. In Baal and Anat the myths of Isis and Osiris, and of Ishtar and Tammuz, live again.

The woman through whom death is defeated, the serpent crushed, and the world healed seems to stand at the centre of Arab-Eastern religion. “Be dust beneath your mother’s feet,” says a modern Arab proverb, neatly summing up the deeply feminine character of a society that modern sociologists have paradoxically tried to caricature as a male brotherhood. The exaltation of woman would become one of Christianity’s dearest themes, and one of the ways in which it would differ radically from Judaism while answering one of the deepest and oldest calls of the East.

Here is part of Dante’s prayer, which might just as easily have been conceived by a worshipper of Isis or Ishtar:

> “Sun at your zenith, virgin mother, daughter of your son… you set us alight with burning love; for mortals you are the fountain of living hope. O Woman, you are so great, so powerful, that whoever desires grace and does not turn to you finds that desire trying to fly without wings. Your goodness not only answers those who call upon you; it goes ahead of their prayers and generously fulfils them. In you is mercy; in you tenderness; in you magnificence; in you are gathered the virtues of every creature.” (*Paradise*, Canto 33).

Among the saints, demigods, and companions who surround the Palestinian gods or move in their wake, several are especially striking: Melkart, inventor of writing and tamer of lions, who recalls Gilgamesh and whom we will meet again in the form of Heracles, himself said to have forced apart the pillars of the Strait of Gibraltar with his own arms to open the route for Palestinian ships; Yahweh, rendered in classical Arabic as *Ya Huwa*, “O He”; and Kâshir, the “supreme architect,” who was in a sense the god of foreign relations because his domains included Kptr — that is, Caphtor, the Old Testament name for Crete — and Hptr, meaning Memphis and, beyond it, all Egypt.

There would be little point in listing every cult, divinity, magical practice, and lofty meditation that the Greeks not only borrowed from Arab tradition but helped to develop alongside it on a land they shared — the Palestinian-Aegean homeland. It would be equally routine to catalogue the countless close parallels between Palestinian religions and the central themes of Judaism, plainly drawn from the Arab background. Here is what Gustave Le Bon, that old master of sturdy common sense — set aside by scholars more learned than he, perhaps, but never truly replaced — had to say:

> “The whole of biblical Genesis — the original Chaos, with the spirit of God hovering over the dark waters; the separation of the waters below from the waters above; the creation of the world, with animals appearing before man; the Flood, the Ark, the Tower of Babel, the confusion of languages, and so on — consists of stories found in exactly the same form in the oldest cuneiform texts. The name given to God by the Jews, like the name Allah by which Muslims invoke him, are both Babylonian in their root Al or El, which in Chaldea denoted the Supreme Being.” (*Les Premières Civilisations*, Flammarion, 1889, p. 555).

Our task now is more precise: to bring some order to the great mythical themes in which Arab and Greek traditions met. Since we have not yet left her behind, let us begin with Ishtar-Anat-Astarte-Aphrodite. The famous myth of Adonis comes immediately to mind. Adonis is a Palestinian hero whose cult was celebrated in Lebanon at the spring of Afqa, where the Adonis River rises. In antiquity a temple of Aphrodite of Afqa stood there. Adonis was doubly ambiguous. First, he was born from twelve incestuous nights — twelve being one of Babylonia’s sacred numbers — between the Syrian princess Misra, or Smyrna, and her own father.

Then, by decision of Zeus, he was divided between Persephone and Aphrodite, as Osiris was divided between Isis and Night, or Tammuz between Ishtar and Mot. A hero of the eternal return from the dead season into the life-giving spring, Adonis turns Aphrodite by turns into a *mater dolorosa* mourning his disappearance — Greek mythology then gives her the Babylonian name Salambo — and into a woman transformed by his return. A rich and overwhelmingly lyrical literature grew up throughout the Mediterranean around this mythical festival.

Processions mourning the death of Adonis were held in Spain, Sicily, Provence, Italy, Athens, Antioch, Jerusalem according to Ezekiel, Bethlehem according to Saint Jerome, and Alexandria. We should note in passing that Adonis is simply the name “Adonai,” the form of address used for the divinity. We should also note the hero’s strongly Arab character, whose Syrian-born mother ultimately escapes her father’s anger by taking refuge in “Arabia.”

But Aphrodite’s role extends far beyond the story of Adonis. Born from the sea and established by Palestinians in Cyprus, Cythera, and Eryx in Sicily, she is known to the Greeks under names including Cypris, Cytherea, and Erycina. Like Ishtar, she has both female and male aspects — Rome even knew a *Venus barbata*, a bearded Venus — and from a brief union with Hermes she produced the bisexual Hermaphrodite. There is more in that name than a mere play on words. Aphrodite is also a Trojan goddess. She is the mother of Aeneas, a figure not unrelated to Adonis.

Unable to save her beloved Troy from destruction, Aphrodite follows the routes of the Palestinian fleets and guides Aeneas and his companions to Thrace, Epirus, Carthage and the Palestinian Elissa — Dido — then Sicily, and from there to the Etruscans of Cumae. Their Sibyl leads him into the Underworld. After learning there what the future holds, he reaches the banks of the Tiber and founds Lavinium, mother-city of Rome. When he dies, his mother Aphrodite carries him into heaven, where the Romans honour him as Jupiter Indiges. Such, in essence, is the story told by Virgil’s *Aeneid*.

Yet Rome was not the only city to claim this Asian son of an Asian goddess as a founding ancestor. Curiously, the Aedui of Autun in Gaul also claimed descent from him. For a long period, the three cities of the Empire exempt from taxation were Autun, Rome, and Troy. The Asian lineage, then, was not merely legendary. Another curiosity: as the mother of Aeneas, Aphrodite was also the mother and divine patron — the *Venus Genetrix* — of the *gens Julia*, Julius Caesar’s family. Is it not interesting that this same Caesar was called upon by the people of Autun, his brothers in the god, to join them in conquering Gaul?

Greek mythology knows another mourning woman, herself *amori et dolori sacra*: Demeter, goddess of bread and fruit. Her story closely follows that of Ishtar. Her daughter Persephone is carried off by the Lord of the Dead while gathering flowers on the mythical plain of Nysa in Yemen, where the eternal and miraculous vine grows. After nine days of weeping Demeter reaches Eleusis near Athens, where she threatens the world with famine unless her daughter is returned. Alarmed, Zeus orders his brother Hades, almighty lord of death, to grant Demeter’s demand.

But Hades loves Persephone and has secretly made her eat a pomegranate seed so that she will remain bound to him forever. Persephone therefore returns each spring to her mother, the Earth, but spends the dead season with her husband. To commemorate both her grief and her joy, Demeter initiates the people of Eleusis into the mysteries of life and resurrection. We know nothing with certainty about the doctrine taught to the initiates or the ritual they practised. Yet throughout classical literature the Eleusinian Mysteries are repeatedly mentioned in veiled language, sometimes with the added claim that they went back to an “Asian revelation.”

Cybele too is Asian: queen of the forces of the earth, worshipped in Asia Minor, so powerful and so feared that in 204 BCE the Roman Senate ordered the “black stone” of Pessinus, which embodied the goddess, to be brought to the Palatine. Her lover Attis, driven mad by love, castrated himself. The hermaphrodite theme associated with the cults of Ishtar and Aphrodite thus marks the road from Babylon to Rome. Hera, sovereign mother of marriage and fertile earth, sister and wife of Zeus, was married in Phrygia on the summit of Mount Ida. Athena, the virgin, was born in Libya beside Lake Triton. Artemis, sister of the sun, has her most magnificent sanctuary at Ephesus, one of Asia’s most famous cities — the city where the apostle John would live after Pentecost, and the first church addressed in his *Revelation*.

As the supreme meeting of life and death, Woman in Greek religion appears in three forms — Virgin, Mother, and Lover — and with three qualities: Serenity, Sorrow, and Desire. She is the exact image of the woman of Asia. Christianity will make Jesus the son of Woman and Spirit. So will the Qur’an. This shows how deeply the idea of creative femininity is woven into the faith of Eastern humanity. The Greek trinity of Zeus, Hades, and Poseidon may in theory be entirely male, but Rome’s includes two goddesses: beside Jupiter sit Juno and Minerva.

Two unmistakably Eastern lords shine in the Greek pantheon: Apollo and Dionysus. Apollo alone could almost sum up the religions, myths, and rites born in Egypt, Palestine, and Assyria-Mesopotamia. A solar prince — at once Osiris, Ahura Mazda, and Anu — by his very name, *El-ios*, he is also a close relative of the Babylonian Eternal, El, which becomes in Arabic *Al-lah*, Allah. His number is seven, the number of the planets. Born on the seventh day of the month and greeted at birth by seven flights of swans, representing the seven days of Creation, he is called “Seventh” by Aeschylus in *Seven Against Thebes*.

In his honour Greek and Roman children would be given the name Septimus, later borne by several Roman emperors. Through his mother Leto, Apollo is a son of God. Fleeing Hera’s jealousy, Leto takes refuge on the lonely island of Delos, where for nine days and nine nights she suffers the pains of childbirth at the foot of a palm tree. And what does the Qur’an tell us? That the Virgin Mary withdrew from her family “toward the East,” went apart to a secluded place beneath a palm tree, and there gave birth to Jesus. Nowhere else do we find such a close pairing of divine birth and the desert palm.

There is another parallel, this time with the origins of Rome, whose patron was said to be the she-wolf. Soon after Apollo’s birth, Leto is taken to Asia Minor, where she becomes a wolf-goddess ruling over a pack of wolves. Among the infant Apollo’s first gifts was a mitre. His first miracle was to kill the serpent Python, which was ravaging the region of Delphi. In memory of this, Delphi — the navel of the world — became one of the Mediterranean’s great centres of divination, home to the famous Pythia, whose oracles were sought by every people. In what international language did the Pythia speak? None, we are told.

She prophesied in cries, which translators attached to the sanctuary then rendered into the visitor’s language. God of purifying killing and great healer, Apollo was also the Announcer — which, etymologically, means the Angel. In this role he occupies an important place in Orphic tradition. He rules the Pythagorean paradise of the Isles of the Blessed, where Achilles and the beautiful Helen, among others, live beatified in immortality. As god of love, he joins with the nymph Cyrene and gives her the eastern province of Libya; he joins with Hecuba, wife of Priam, and fathers a son who will found Miletus.

Finally, the Apollonian holy spirit joins with a virgin who gives birth to Pythagoras, himself a son of god and virgin — a miracle foretold by the Pythia of Delphi. If Apollo is master of music, it is because he knows the secret of numbers. More architect of sound than poet of melody, he leads the Muses with exacting knowledge.

If Demeter is goddess of bread, Dionysus is god of wine. The mystery of bread and the mystery of wine will become the keys to two mystery religions that meet first in Asia, then in Greece and Rome, and finally in Christianity. Friedrich Nietzsche’s lyrical writing gave the cult of Dionysus an aura of frenzy and set it against the harmonious calm of Apollo. From this grew an entire philosophy in which Asian madness was used to throw European clarity into sharper relief. It is a convenient old cliché, but not a convincing one: first because Apollo and Dionysus are both Asian; second because a Dionysian orgy is no more simply a debauch than a communion meal is. Any divine celebration can include ritual feasting.

Etymologically, Dionysus means “son of god,” and he is indeed the son of Zeus, who united with the Palestinian Semele — *Shemla* in Arabic — the daughter of Cadmus, king of Sidon and founder of Thebes in Boeotia. Palestinian through his mother, Dionysus is Arab through his journeys and his stays in Egypt, Syria, Phrygia, where he knew and loved Cybele, and on the Indus, where he became conqueror and lawgiver. He is said to have spent his childhood in Yemen, in the mythical grape-growing land of Nysa, hidden from the jealousy of Hera, the irritable lawful wife of the king of the gods.

For Dionysus is first the hidden god, the *deus absconditus* of Scripture; then the travelling god; and finally the god of ecstasy and of the descent into the Underworld. At Nysa he lived after Zeus transformed him into a young goat: mystic kid, mystic lamb — the two images stand close together. As an adult he travelled throughout the Asian world preaching the ineffable; scarcely a corner of the earth failed to preserve some memory of his passing. Had the lower world not already been spiritually unified by Baal or Osiris, Dionysus would surely have done it himself.

His journeys were not always peaceful. In Thrace he came up against King Lycurgus, who tried to stop him crossing the Hellespont from Asia into Europe. Dionysus defeated him, put out his eyes, and crucified him. Some authors place this dramatic episode instead in Yemen, at the Strait of Bab al-Mandab. Ecstasy, the erasure of the self, the collapse of personality into the divine abyss through dance, communion, and “sacred wine” — this is what Dionysus teaches the Maenads or Bacchants who worship him.

The attempt to free the soul through physical disciplines is common to mystical brotherhoods, and the whirling dervishes allow us to imagine what the Dionysian congregations may have been like, perhaps even as their heirs. Through ecstasy one goes to God; through ecstasy one returns alive from the realm of the dead. Ishtar descended into the Underworld to save her son; Dionysus descends to save his mother, whom Hades returns to him in exchange for a branch of myrtle. This is why the followers of Demeter at Eleusis include Dionysus in their rites.

The night procession that takes the Athenians to Eleusis each year is accompanied by the Thyiads, priestesses devoted to the son of Semele. Every two years, in November, they go in procession to wake the god near Mount Parnassus, only to commemorate his death again three months later. They are also priestesses of Apollo and dance for him. While the faithful at Eleusis share in bread and wine, at Delphi the circle of the sun and the dance of Bacchus-Dionysus carry their followers into the same cosmic joy. “* Io skotos emon phaos*” — “Darkness, my light!” — Sophocles makes Ajax cry. The phrase sums up the accord between Apollo and Dionysus.

The torches lighting the wild festivals of the Bacchants were themselves a splendid homage to Apollo — that is, to El(ios).

Greece owes its theatre to this Dionysus, son of Zeus and grandson of the Palestinian Cadmus — but it was a theatre unlike anything we know today. Introduced officially at Athens by the family of Peisistratus in the sixth century BCE, it gave formal and poetic shape to the public rite and procession that the Bacchants had long celebrated tumultuously in honour of their sovereign master. The mystery plays of medieval Europe likewise turned the Passion of Christ into living tableaux. Modern Iranian-Mesopotamian theatre also grew out of public mourning ceremonies for the Muslim hero Husayn, son of Ali, martyred at Karbala on the Euphrates. Greek theatre, then, was originally — and in essence remained despite later outward changes — an oratorio: a dramatic musical composition on a religious subject, the service of Dionysus.

Everyone knows that the word “tragedy” is built from *tragos*, “goat” or “kid,” and *ode*, “song” — a clear reference to the god’s childhood, when he lived hidden in Arabia Felix in the form of a young goat. “Tragedy” could therefore be rendered “Song of the Kid,” or “song for a kid.” Greek tragedy is first of all a song: humanity addressing the heavens while feeling itself ruled by cosmic destiny. On stage the actors hardly act; they declaim. In the orchestra the chorus sings and dances, while the chorus leader is at once conductor and partner to the actors. The music is Eastern.

In a treatise on choreography written in his youth, Sophocles affirmed the primacy of the Phrygian melody. Slowness, prayer, lyricism, epic, communion: these words come closest to defining Greek tragedy. There is little intellectual suspense in such drama, little plot or dramatic intrigue. What it demands instead is intense nervous involvement, an almost physical rapture. The purpose is to draw every person present into the universal drama, not to entertain them — especially since the audience already knows the stories by heart.

Did the audiences of Aeschylus and Sophocles sing with the chorus? Did they dance with the actors? Probably. In any case, they took part in the performance, in the liturgy. The same is true of the contemporary Arab festival: there are not actors on one side and spectators on the other; everyone is an actor. The festival is an expression of shared feeling. It is not a show, still less a show sold for a price. Its virtue is that it is freely shared; its necessity is communion. Like classical Greek theatre, the Arab festival does not represent: it celebrates. It does not distract: it commits. It does not immobilize: it sets people in motion. It is ceremony in the fullest sense, with everyone playing a part according to a precise code. It is, in short, the opposite of television: sleeping images for sleeping eyes.

The Arab festival and Dionysian theatre are such thoroughly public “concerts” that the actor is not allowed to distinguish himself through facial display or some original gesture. The actor in Aeschylus has no individual height, face, or name. Raised on cothurni, wearing a fixed mask of wood or metal and robes of hieratic breadth, he is Man or God — the sovereign representative of everyone and no one. The Greek chorus, absorbing and denying human solitude, is always present. Even when silent it is there to watch, listen, and judge. It follows the actors closely, never letting them out of its sight or attention for a moment.

It is the Eye, the Witness, the collective Conscience. The characters are at its command, and the author is condemned to modesty. Everything for the work, nothing for the artist: that seems to be the rule of this artistic discipline. In European theatre, the passions of Hamlet or Hermione are personal adventures accompanied by lyrical monologues. The laments of Antigone or Prometheus are choral: expressions of universal tragedy. They are one string made to set every other string vibrating, and the instrument is the chorus — the whole city. Victor Hugo saw in Aeschylus “a Greek Bible,” and in his Prometheus “Christian gleams.”

Better to listen to Aeschylus himself:

> “Let your god wreck and shake the universe; let him send his birds of snow and his crashing underground thunder. Nothing will prevent his fall. Nothing — no, nothing — will make me reveal the name of the one who will one day overthrow his rule.”

Aeschylus came from Eleusis and was probably initiated into the Mysteries of Dionysus and Demeter. The religious power that comes through Greek theatre is, in many ways, more Christian and more convincing than most of our secular works inspired by European Christianity. This is proof that faith is not acquired through dogma, and that the Palestinian and Greek mind of the great early periods already contained a readiness to receive true Christianity because Christianity itself emerged from that world. The idea of the sacred trinity also continued to shape Greek drama for a long time: the performance consisted of three plays, not one. Greek theatre is built around the trilogy. It carries myth beyond immediate circumstances and unfolds it in three dimensions — historical, poetic, and supernatural — corresponding to the threefold nature of existence as the ancient cities experienced it.

The Palestinian-Theban legend did not end with the wandering preaching of Dionysus. It gave rise to one of the great heroic cycles of Greek mythology, alongside the Trojan War and the Orphic cycle of the Argonauts. Its figures include Europa, Niobe, Oedipus, Antigone, Tiresias, Eteocles, and Polynices, but above all Heracles, the Saviour. Like Dionysus, Heracles is warrior, judge, invader of the Underworld, and traveller, moving from one end of the ancient world to the other along routes that often match those of Dionysus. Born at Thebes and son of Zeus, he resembles Gilgamesh in stature and in physical and moral strength.

He also resembles the legendary Melkart of the Babylonian-Palestinian tradition, also called Salman, “the saviour,” whose feminine counterpart is Salambo, another name for Astarte-Ishtar. Simply placing the Arabic words *salam* and *Salman* beside the Greek and Latin *soter* and *salve* exposes the emptiness of the arbitrary division between so-called Semitic and Indo-European languages.

Heracles therefore represents salvation, just as Thebes is the holy city. Thebes brings together three symbolic animals of Eastern religion: the Egyptian sphinx encountered by Oedipus; the dragon killed by Cadmus, the Phoenician founder of Thebes and counterpart of the Lernaean Hydra beheaded by Heracles; and finally the bull of Baal, which carried off Europa, Cadmus’s sister, to Crete and gave rise to the Minotaur, the dynasty of Minos, and the cult of the bull slain by Heracles.

Knossos, capital of Crete and of Minos, was also one of the centres of Dionysus — showing that it claimed the Palestinian lineage of Europa, the Bull, Dionysus, and Heracles. The hero’s twelve labours correspond to the twelve divisions of the Babylonian zodiac: trials the soul must overcome before reaching apotheosis. In Libya, Egypt, Phrygia, and Arabia he never stopped fighting and driving out demons, rather like Saint George in Christian tradition.
