Before descending into the Underworld to bring back Theseus and Alcestis, he prudently had himself initiated into the Eleusinian Mysteries.

His terrible death came through the unwitting act of his last wife, Deianira, daughter of Dionysus, whose name is typically Arabic. Poisoned by the robe of Nessus that she gave him in innocent ignorance, Heracles built his own funeral pyre on Mount Oeta, lay down upon it, and set it alight, dying after a dreadful agony. In an apotheosis his body was immediately carried into the heavens amid the crash of thunder. Zeus judged that through his trials, virtues, and above all his suffering he had earned heaven. The idea of redemptive suffering became attached to him, together with faith in a resurrection reserved for noble souls who had suffered martyrdom. This is why images of Heracles later appear among the frescoes of the Christian catacombs.

Closely connected with the Theban cycle is the blind prophet Tiresias, whose fate was unlike any other. Serpents shaped the course of his life; he was successively woman and man; and finally, through the grace of Dionysus, Zeus granted him the power to continue prophesying after death. Among humanity’s prophets he holds a special place as “the blind man who sees.” Odysseus travelled to the cold land of the Cimmerians to consult him and summoned the great council of the dead. Spreading far beyond the Mediterranean and entering both popular belief and philosophical esotericism, the Theban legend still surfaces here and there in East and West.

Yet few people know that beyond the Palestinian Cadmus its ancestry goes back to the marriage of Poseidon and the nymph Libya, herself descended from Zeus through her father and from the Nile through her mother, Memphis. Greece’s family tree is therefore nourished by an incalculable network of Arab roots. It is striking that interpreters of the three monotheistic religions, so ready to take biblical genealogies seriously and draw questionable conclusions from them, dismiss as legend the family lines established by Greek mythology, though they are surely worth as much as those in Samuel, Kings, or the Gospel of Matthew.

With Orpheus we leave the world of gods and enter that of high priests and esoteric theology. Orpheus is at once the Psalmist of the living and the dead, the Protector, and the great Prior of the earliest monasteries. Initiated in Egypt, he is, together with Dionysus, the founder of the Eleusinian Mysteries. Around him appear not only Dionysus but Heracles; the Cabiri — that is, the Seven Babylonian Planets; the polar star Eshmun; Medea the sorceress; the serpent; and the sacred ram whose cult was widespread in the Nile Valley.

Around the name of Orpheus grew a tradition far older than historical times, giving rise to a vast apocryphal literature: hymns, popular votive offerings, miracle formulas attached to sacred objects, relics, and amulets — not unlike the devotional items sold at Lourdes. More interesting are the cosmologies that claim to come from his revelations, and the endless *Argonautica* poems recounting his mystical journeys from the Caucasus to Campania, written by figures such as Apollodorus and Apollonius of Rhodes, themselves initiates into his cult.

We should not make ourselves ridiculous by pretending to explain Orphism. Like every doctrine of high antiquity, it is a bottomless well. Still, an interesting outline — the only real one, in fact — was offered by the Peripatetic philosopher Eudemus, whose works preserve the essentials of Orphic cosmology. Hesiod makes Heaven and Earth the original pair; the followers of Orpheus instead place Night before Day, and make Night, together with the Void represented by Ocean, the mother of all things and all life. If Apollo celebrates light, Orpheus is a kind of priest of darkness. The epics and hymns devoted to him are therefore both nocturnal and maritime.

Nocturnal is his journey into the Underworld in search of Eurydice, killed by the serpent. Through the enchantment of his lyre and his voice he charms the monsters and gods below; the tormented are given relief; Tantalus forgets his hunger and thirst, Ixion forgets the wheel of serpents on which he turns. Orpheus is on the verge of bringing his wife back to earth when, breaking his promise to Hades, he turns to look at her, so overwhelming is his longing to see the beloved face again. Eurydice vanishes, this time forever. Nocturnal, then, is the search for lost love.

Maritime is the epic of the Argonauts: the warriors who sail aboard the Argo toward the dark, cold skies of the Caucasus in search of the Golden Ram. Among them Orpheus keeps time for the rowers, drives away the demons of the road, and clears obstacles. Beside their leader Jason he plays something like the role of Beatrice beside Dante, or the Sibyl beside Aeneas. Let us follow him closely along the legendary route that peoples preserved almost religiously in their memory. The Argo is built of sacred wood, sails under the sacred protection of Athena — who will also watch over Odysseus — and sets out on a sacred mission. It can speak, because Athena has given it the gift of prophecy. Before beginning its voyage it visits Samothrace, a blessed place and home to the sanctuary of the deeply mysterious Cabiri.

Who are these Cabiri? Their name, *kabir*, is pure Arabic and means “great.” This proves two things at once: that Arabic vocabulary was fully present in Greek, and that the difference between the Aramaic of Homer’s age and modern Arabic can often be surprisingly slight. Note in passing that *kabir* survives in liturgical Hebrew *Kippur* — the same word pronounced differently — so that *Yom Kippur* would mean “the great day.” Greek and Latin writers are extremely cautious when speaking of these gods. They are Babylonian-Palestinian divinities of the highest importance.

Their father is Sadek, a Palestinian whose name in Arabic means “the one who tells the truth.” From the heights of heaven the Cabiri rule destinies; they are planetary and astrological powers, and also masters of navigation because they rule Night and Water — the lower element reflecting the bright markers of the night sky. Ships carried by the heaven below and lit by the heaven above are under their protection, and Orpheus is one of their priests, as are the Dioscuri.

The Cabiri have temples everywhere: Egypt, Mesopotamia, Phrygia, Palestine, the Greek coasts and islands. Aeneas and the Etruscans bring these guardian gods to Rome, where their symbols are the square and the cypress. The awe and fear they inspire are so great that people refer to them only indirectly, almost with a finger to the lips, not daring to speak their names. They simply call them “the Great Gods” — “great” being simply the meaning of the Arabic *kabir*. These are the sons of Sadek, a name in which he sees an unmistakably Arabic word that still appears in forms such as Sadok and Sadaka from the Atlantic to the Indus. Thucydides, for his part, mentions a Thracian king named Sadokos.

On the way to the Black Sea, the crew of the Argo crosses the Hellespont and stops in Phrygia to pay homage to Our Lady Cybele. They then sail on through many dangers to Colchis, north of the dark Caucasus. It is a strange land. Tradition says that it was settled by the soldiers of Sesostris, the famous Ramses II. A flying ram once landed there, and after the miraculous animal was sacrificed its Golden Fleece was displayed in the royal palace. When the Argonauts arrive, the king is Aeetes, brother of Pasiphae, wife of Minos of Crete and grandson of Agenor, king of Palestine. Jason demands the Golden Fleece, and Aeetes sets him the trials of the bull and the dragon. With the loving help of the king’s daughter Medea, Jason succeeds, then leaves the country carrying away both the Fleece and his accomplice Medea.

Then begins the endless return journey: along the Danube, Rhône, and Po; through the rivers and Etruscan shores of Campania; across the sea of the Sirens, those deadly singers from whom the Argo escapes through the spells of Orpheus; then to the island of the Phaeacians and onward to Libya by Lake Triton. This cultural geography marks out what we must call the East, set against a Germanic world shaped by a different mentality. Finally the travellers turn back toward Greece, and the journey is marked by a strange night off Crete. Darkness suddenly falls, swallowing the Argo and its crew. The sailors abandon hope of escaping this marine hell.

Then, answering the combined prayers of Orpheus and Medea, granddaughter of the Sun, Apollo himself appears, saves them, and dispels the darkness. They anchor near a small island of the Sporades which Orphic texts, in memory of this divine intervention, call Anaphe — “the island of Revelation,” or better, “of Unveiling.” By coincidence, it lies not far from Patmos, where the truth of the *Apocalypse* was “unveiled” to Saint John.

The Argo ends its voyage at Corinth. There too the love affair of Jason and Medea comes to an end. Furious at being abandoned, the sorceress kills her children, escapes to Athens, marries Aegeus, and eventually returns to Asia, where she ends her life. Orpheus meets an even more tragic fate. After returning from the Underworld, forever widowed of Eurydice, he begins to avoid all relations with women and founds sanctuaries of contemplation for his followers from which women are excluded. Should we see here the beginnings of monasticism? Those who lived in these sanctuaries abstained from meat and eggs. Perhaps they also took vows of celibacy. In any case, Orpheus is the only mythological hero to have left no descendants. He remained man in his solitude.

Back in his native Thrace, his behaviour provokes the anger of the women, who kill him, tear his body apart, and throw his head and lyre into the sea. Both, still singing Eurydice’s loss across the waves, drift from shore to shore until they reach Lesbos. Pious hands bury the head, while the lyre rises into the sky and becomes a constellation. The psalmist’s soul goes to the Elysian Fields, where dressed in white, an angel among angels, it never ceases to celebrate the joy of paradise. Ancestor of poets, Orpheus was said to have inspired Homer and Hesiod. For at least two thousand years, Orphic teaching continued to govern the Mediterranean imagination.

Prometheus the Titan is tied both to the Caucasus and to the myth of the Flood. He is an exact counterpart of the Mesopotamian hero Adapa, whose misfortune is told in the cuneiform texts of the library of Nineveh. Adapa is servant of Ea, god of the sea, who initiates him into knowledge until he dares to confront the god of heaven and claim immortality. Cast down from heaven, he spends the rest of his life working humbly on earth. Prometheus suffers an even worse fate, chained to the Caucasus through the anger of Zeus. Prometheus’s mother was named Asia, and that his son Deucalion, like Noah and the Mesopotamian Utnapishtim, saves humanity from the Flood by following his father’s advice.

Another figure, Icarus, the hero with waxen wings, has a Mesopotamian ancestor in Etana. Seeking the magical plant of creation that grew at the summit of heaven, Etana tried to reach it by fastening himself to the feathers of his friend the eagle. He had already reached the first heaven when Ishtar struck him with vertigo; Etana lost his grip and fell back to the earth of mortals. We know that Icarus, son of Daedalus, met much the same end. And who was Daedalus? A prince of the family of Cecrops, founder of Athens, who was himself son of the Egyptian Erechtheus, first ruler of Attica.

And what of Helen of Troy, wife of King Menelaus, sister of Clytemnestra and of Castor and Pollux, allies of the Cabiri? Her story does not end with the fall of Troy. She too has an Egyptian escape. On her way back to Sparta, slipping away from Menelaus’s watch, she takes refuge on the Nile and begins a long series of extraordinary adventures. She destroys serpents and falls in love with the boatman Pharos, the very man who, legend says, gave his name to Alexandria’s famous lighthouse. She is also said to have loved another sailor, Canopus, the hero who gave his name to a city in the Delta. Then she appears in Cyrenaica, along the Gulf of Sirte, in Tripolitania, on the Danube. Where did she not appear? And how much could be said about Hecuba, wife of Priam.

One curious parallel deserves mention. While pregnant with Paris, Hecuba dreamed that she gave birth to a torch. According to the *Golden Legend*, Saint Dominic’s mother had a similar prophetic dream before the birth of her famous son: “she dreamed that she carried in her womb a little dog holding in its mouth a burning torch with which it set the whole world on fire.” Once again we see that time and space refuse to divide themselves according to the arbitrary borders and partisan habits of modern thought. We might just as easily speak of the Danaids, granddaughters of Libya; Circe, sister of Pasiphae; Bellerophon, who killed the Chimera near Antioch and fought the Solymi at Assoun or Aswan; and countless others.

It was in the Aegean Sea, under the sign of Palestine, that much of the Greco-Roman pantheon took shape, with gods and heroes from Libya, Sicily, Egypt, Anatolia, the Arabian Peninsula, and Babylonia. Greece never stopped taking pride in this inheritance. And this inheritance is in fact the inheritance of all the religions, philosophies, and artistic traditions of the so-called Western world. In chapter 27 of the biblical book called Ezekiel we find a tribute to Palestine whose prestige plainly delights the author. Nowhere else in the biblical texts do we find such worldly enthusiasm:

> “O Tyre, you have said: ‘I am perfect in beauty. Your borders are in the heart of the sea; those who built you made your beauty perfect.’”

The passage goes on to celebrate the wealth and happiness of a country trading fruitfully with the islands of the Aegean, Greece, Thrace, Syria, the land of Israel, Arabia, India, Chaldea, Assyria — Haran, Eden, and Asshur — Ethiopia, and many others. “By the abundance of the goods you traded overseas, you satisfied many peoples; by your commerce and your accumulated wealth, you enriched the kings of the earth.” That is enough to show what Palestine represented in Greek eyes, since the author of the book of Ezekiel — *Hizqil* in Arabic — is of course Greek, as are all biblical texts.

Just as the aesthetic, decorative, clothing, and religious traditions of the East — circumcision, for example, practised in Egypt from remote antiquity — were preserved and passed down by churches of every confession, which were more careful than ordinary society to preserve their original marks, so popular tales and legends kept the memory of mythical times alive. We therefore look forward to a systematic collection of the oral traditions of the towns and countryside of the Mediterranean East, together with those of North Africa, Sicily, and the Iberian and Italian peninsulas. Some are striking in their simplicity: the story, for example, that the Virgin Mary used the money given by the Magi to pay for her seven-year stay in Egypt; or the tradition that places Plato and Virgil among the prophets who foretold Jesus, alongside Isaiah.

A serious study of the texts, frescoes, and sculpture of the Christian catacombs in Rome would surely reveal a major Arab influence. There we find the vine of Dionysus the Yemeni, Ishtar’s dove, the fish of Oannes, the boat of Isis, the sun of El or Horus. Mary is shown in the image of the grieving Demeter. Pan, the Greco-Palestinian divinity with a sheep on his shoulder, becomes the Good Shepherd. Orpheus is identified with Jesus. No less striking is the list of great Christian teachers who came from Arab families: Tertullian of Carthage, Saint Cyprian, and Saint Augustine the Numidian; Origen and Saint Athanasius, Egyptians; Saint Basil of Caesarea, Saint Ephrem, and Saint John Chrysostom, Palestinian-Syrians; and the famous Synesius of Cyrene, a Libyan. And these are only a few names gathered in passing.

In his history of the Goths, Cassiodorus presents an Attila steeped in Arab-Greek traditions. Gregorian chant arose from the meeting of the Dorian song of Greek tragedy — already shaped by Anatolian taste — and Palestinian hymns, whose collection Pope Gregory the Great ordered in the sixth century in the *Antiphonary*, intended to guide the Roman liturgy. The final and relatively recent form of Gregorian chant did indeed emerge through a long process of refinement in the churches of France, especially those of Compiègne, Metz, Senlis, and the Loire Valley. But the elements coordinated and reshaped there came from the ancient Arab choral traditions that Greece had once echoed.

Without disappearing into details of scholarship that ultimately raise more problems than they solve, it is worth remembering that the religious texts used by Jewish, Muslim, and Christian communities make up only a tiny part of the enormous body of mythical and ritual inscriptions that survives. They are certainly products of synthesis, but also of filtering: only the essence of the essence was retained. Eastern writings on the mysteries of the divine number in the thousands, in Greek and other languages — and Greek often served merely as a phonetic transcription of Palestinian, Hittite, Babylonian, or Egyptian texts.

Sibylline oracles, Orphic formulas, tablets of Mithras, magical spells, apocalyptic prophecies: what is remarkable about them all is their complete blending of traditions — biblical Genesis with Babylonian-Palestinian cosmology, Dionysian redemption with Christian resurrection, Moses with Hercules.

Around the third century CE, Alexandria gathered together the dense materials of this gnosis. They are not always easy to decipher, and in parable form they preserve ideas far older than what we call the revealed religions — as though the others had not been revealed. Among other things, they speak of the end of the world. Christians have Saint John’s vision of that end in the *Apocalypse*; Islam offers a broadly similar vision. Judaism has no properly apocalyptic reference of its own. Christianity and Islam are therefore close relatives on this point — so close that the Church of Rome, perhaps finding the *Apocalypse* too Eastern for European taste, keeps it at a certain distance and gives greater prominence to the Gospels and the letters of Saint Paul, while Eastern Christianity remains deeply attached to the Revelation of Saint John.

Contrary to the usual view, Islam’s apocalypse was not inspired by John’s. Both draw instead on a far older tradition whose traces survive in collections of gnosis. Once again, Greeks and Arabs — believers in Alexandria, Palestine, the Hijaz, Greece, and elsewhere — began from a common religious background and then divided between those who “saw” the end of the world and those who did not. The fragment of a Gnostic apocalypse quoted below, translated by Robert Brasillach in his anthology of Greek poetry, is usually dated to the third century CE, but contains phrases and images found in cuneiform, Egyptian, and Greek texts reaching back beyond the fifth century BCE.

> … When the sign appears above the nations  
> And children are born with white hair…  
> Woe to you who live to see that day…  
> Everywhere the stars will fall from heaven into the sea…  
> And every race of humankind will gnash its teeth…  
> Then the immortal ministers of the eternal God will appear:  
> Michael, Gabriel, Raphael, and Uriel.

The last three of these angels also appear in Milton’s *Paradise Lost* and in Haydn’s famous oratorio *The Creation*, where Raphael voices the warning:

> You turn away your face,  
> And everything trembles and grows still.  
> You take away their breath,  
> And they fall back into dust.

These meditations, invocations, and curses reach back to primordial times, when the Mediterranean East — in its sky, sea, and land — was saturated with a God present through saints, supernatural beings, and heroes, all more or less related to one another. “Everything is full of gods,” the Greek philosopher Thales observed: *panta plere theon*. They moved through the world as radio or television waves do today; they gave it life, ordered it, and consoled it. Such ages were by nature dynamic, swept along by currents in which intellectual values counted for nothing but intuitive powers were intensely creative. Humanity came to see in them the Golden Age for which it longed, and to believe that the further society moved from those times — from spiritual perfection — the more life became corrupted and dissolved into an apocalyptic chaos that was at once destructive and renewing.

In religious belief, ruin calls forth a return to the primordial age just as death calls forth life. The cosmic existence imagined by Mediterranean antiquity was therefore necessarily cyclical: every beginning implied an ending, and every ending a beginning. The idea of resurrection is contained within this as naturally as air within air. All change is governed by the mathematical law of Eternal Return, itself ruled by the movements of the stars. This is precisely the teaching of Orpheus and his followers, of Isis and Osiris, of the foundational myths of an East that might equally be called the City of Orpheus or the City of Isis.

Orpheus may in fact be the figure who best represents Eastern religious feeling in the West. Around him gathers everything from Isis to Dionysus that evokes Woman, Purity, Resurrection, the Last Judgment, Paradise, and Hell. This living current still carries our civilization and still inspires reflective pilgrimages back to the sources of our hope. The heart of Greek philosophy is the heart of Orpheus, just as it is the heart of Isis and the Word, of the Islamic Word or Jewish Prophecy. Everything connects; everything becomes intelligible if we are willing to look deeply enough into these intuitions.

Virgil’s poetry offers the best summary of the divine and mythical order of Mediterranean societies, and the clearest proof of the transfer of Eastern cults into the West. The *Aeneid* gives a detailed account of that transfer. The *Georgics* celebrate Mother Earth, drawing on the agricultural encyclopaedia of the Carthaginian Mago, whose Aramaic work was successively translated into Greek and Latin. Through the *Eclogues* blows the Greco-Arab tradition of Alexandria and Sicily. Virgil’s esoteric and Eastern character did not escape medieval readers, nor Victor Hugo, whose description is unmistakable:

> … In Virgil sometimes God is nearly an angel,  
> And at the summit of the verse a strange light gleams…  
> For without even knowing it, he is one of those souls  
> Touched by faint flames from the distant East.  
> He is one of those hearts that already, beneath the skies,  
> Gilded the dawning day of the mysterious Christ.

The Romantic poet was probably thinking of the famous Fourth Eclogue, which is indeed steeped in Palestinian religious feeling, as is the fourth book of the *Georgics* — why the same number four? — where the story of Aristaeus, son of the Libyan Cyrene, crosses with Orpheus’s journey to the Underworld. From its opening, the Fourth Eclogue stands under the sign of the Sicilian divinities. It foretells “the end of the age announced by the Sibyl of Cumae, the birth of new centuries that will henceforth be counted from zero” (*solvet soeculum in favilla, teste dave cum Sibilla*, echoed by the *Dies irae* of our liturgy). It then predicts “the return of the Virgin, of Saturn, and of the Golden Age, while from the heights of heaven a newborn child will descend.” Through him sin will be abolished and perpetual peace will reign over the world.

The poet turns to this child in prayer and hope: “For you, child, for your smile, the earth will pour out its gifts: creeping ivy mixed with baccar and acanthus; the young goats will return to the fold with udders swollen with milk, and the herds will no longer fear the great lions. Your cradle will bloom with flowers to caress you. Even the serpent will perish; the treacherous poisonous plant will perish; everywhere Assyrian incense will grow.” The poem follows the child as he grows among harvests and orchards. It foresees another voyage of the Argonauts, another Trojan War, all ending in the peace of human beings reconciled with earth, sea, and animals.

“Rise, then, toward the highest honours, for the time is near, beloved son of the gods, almighty offspring of the Father. Look at the universe tipping beneath the full weight of its curve — the continents, the moving seas, the depth of heaven. See how all nature trembles with joy at the coming age.” Virgil then wishes that he might live and breathe long enough to celebrate the miracles of this Child. He will sing them with such force that he will surpass Orpheus and Linus even if Calliope and Apollo come to their aid; Pan himself will have to admit defeat. “Begin, then, little child, to smile upon your mother…”

Every commentator has noticed the striking resemblance between the Fourth Eclogue and chapters 11 and 66 of the biblical book of Isaiah. What should we conclude? The conclusion is that many educated people, beyond the formal religion of the Roman state — though even that is less clear than we sometimes assume — were familiar with more mystical revelations belonging to a less openly acknowledged tradition. Virgil need not have copied Isaiah. Could he even have known the text? That is far from certain. The countless alterations, interpretations, revisions, and interpolations made to biblical texts over the course of their long history make firm conclusions difficult.

Virgil’s own religious culture, combined with the writings and liturgies flowing from the Arab, Greek, and Sicilian East, was more than enough to inspire a poem like the Fourth Eclogue. And such outpourings can hardly have seemed strange, since Virgil’s many readers in Italy and elsewhere in the first century BCE embraced them readily. It is worth remembering that Virgil came from Mantua, an Etruscan city closely linked to Lydian migrations and situated in that eastern part of Italy which, over the centuries, would take on a distinctly Arab face in Ravenna and Venice.

Even today, the strange Gonzaga palace at Mantua — with its architectural riddles and numerical geometry, and since the thirteenth century one of the Renaissance’s vastest allegorical monuments — evokes Gnostic symbols. Isabella, wife of Francesco d’Este, filled it with Eastern references. Surrounded by magicians and Jewish or Muslim interpreters, she was taught to read cards and consult the stars and saints. She too was preoccupied with resurrection. Among the symbols with which she filled the palace, we should notice a triangular one-branched candlestick recalling the old Easter service.

A similar stand beside the altar held fifteen candles, extinguished one by one as the service proceeded except for a single candle at the apex of the triangle. Still burning, this final candle was hidden behind the altar while the congregation sang the *Miserere*. It was then returned to the stand, and joy broke out at this sign of Christ’s resurrection.

A question naturally follows: did the Greeks know the sanctuary of Mecca? Its foundation disappears into the depths of time. According to tradition, the Kaaba — the Square House — was brought down from heaven by angels before Adam was born. After the Flood it was rebuilt by Abraham with the help of his son Ishmael and the angel Gabriel, who presented them on God’s behalf with the famous Black Stone to be set into the building. It must have been one of the sacred stones or *betyls* — *beit-El*, “dwelling of God” — like the black stone of Pessinus sacred to Cybele, or the stone of Emesa that we will encounter again in imperial Rome. The horns of the ram sacrificed by Abraham in place of Isaac were also said to have been hung there.

Damaged repeatedly by floods, the sanctuary was eventually restored, tradition says, by a Greek ship captain who happened to be both mason and carpenter and used timber from his own vessel as building material. That, at least, is what survives among the many traditions connected with the building and the sacred site it occupies near Mount Abu Qubays, where Adam was said to be buried. The Greeks could hardly have been ignorant of Mecca’s sanctuary, since before becoming — like many temples of the age — a kind of pantheon containing statues of Mary and Jesus, it had been a dwelling place of great Babylonian and Egyptian gods.

The word *Makai* does occur in classical Greek and, according to Strabo, seems to refer to a city in the Arabian Peninsula. We know no more than that. But, let us repeat, archaeological study of the peninsula has only begun, and the interpretation of the evidence — until now guided in a narrowly European direction — is fortunately beginning to become less one-sided.

At the end of this brief journey through Greco-Palestinian religious and mythical traditions, one conclusion seems obvious: Greco-Roman religion is saturated, to its core, with the Nile-Mesopotamian East. At the same time Judaism, Christianity, and Islam each gather up and summarize, in their own way, the collective religious life of peoples sharing the same language, customs, and rites. Islam, the latest of the three great religions to appear, had been prefigured for thousands of years, just as Jesus had been prefigured in the ancestors of Abraham.

Here we have to reverse our normal assumptions about criticism and history. The idea of prefiguration is so deeply rooted in the religious mind of the East because it expresses an intense desire to affirm, beneath accidental differences, the essential and timeless unity of humanity’s covenant with God. A few verses from the *Qur’an* say more on this point than pages of commentary:

> “When We gave Abraham the site of the temple of Mecca as a sanctuary, We instructed him not to allow idols there.” (*Qur’an*, Savary translation, Surah XXII, *The Pilgrimage*, 27).
>
> “We believe in the teaching of Moses, Jesus, and the prophets; we make no distinction between them, and we are Muslims.” (Surah II, *The Cow*, 130).
>
> “We gave Moses a book to guide the Israelites… We offered Jesus and his mother to the admiration of the universe.” (Surah XXIII, *The Believers*, 51–52).
>
> When God said to him, “Embrace Islam,” Abraham replied: “I have embraced it…” (Surah II, *The Cow*, 125).

As for the hope of resurrection — so dear to Eastern humanity and carried by it step by step to the furthest limits of the West — the *Qur’an* takes it up without changing either its words or its spirit: “The Hour will come, there can be no doubt of it, when God will restore to life the ashes that lie in the tombs.” (Surah XXII, *The Pilgrimage*, 7).

Saint Augustine, for his part, writes that “this truth which is called the Christian religion existed among the Ancients and has never ceased to exist since the beginning of the human race, until Christ himself came and the true religion which had already existed before began to be called Christian.”

# IV

## THE DIVINE LESSONS

> “Man is by nature deprived of reason.”  
> HERACLITUS.

Religion is philosophy. The two are one. Greek philosophy is an integral part of religion and therefore an integral part of Eastern thought. Its cradle was the Asian city of Miletus, where currents from Palestine, Anatolia, Babylonia, and Persia met. It has no interest in private inward speech and little use for literature. Its great popular virtue is that it never loses contact with heaven and earth. It is not metaphysics at all, but physics, magic, and wisdom.

There is no trace here of philosophy as a purely mental pursuit, no hollow intellectual exercise. Greek philosophy tends instead to suppress individual thought so that a person can adhere completely to reality, leaving no gap, speculation, or dead space between the self and the universe. Ideas, in the sense we give the word today, are foreign to Greek philosophy because they are foreign to existence itself. “I think, therefore I am” is the exact opposite of the ancient outlook. Its instinct would be closer to: “I think, therefore I am not,” because for the classical East the self is pure nothingness.

Looking for a passage that could sum up the spirit of Hellenic philosophy in a few lines, we chose this one: “Think above all of what can never end. Extinguish in yourself the desire for what passes away; entrust yourself to Providence; want only what it wants, as it wants it, and when it wants it. This is the only road to peace and the only firm ground for hope at the final hour.” The thought could just as easily be Islamic, Stoic, Platonic, or Christian. In fact it is Christian, taken from *The Imitation of Christ*, a Franciscan work of the Renaissance.

For the Greek, there is God, and then there is nothing. The only true space is cosmic; the only true time is eternal. Whatever is not perfect is insignificant. The task of philosophy is not to catalogue the moods of the individual, but to bring out what is solid and splendid in humanity. This explains its commanding style and its sovereign gaze. It also explains its deliberate lack of interest in psychology, sociology, and economics. Nothing could be further from our modern concerns. Philosophy was a method of salvation, not a classroom exercise.

Unfortunately, it later fell into the hands of scholars who reshaped it to suit themselves and left us with a dismembered image of it. The Greek philosopher was not a professor speaking from a lectern. His philosophy was religious wisdom, expressed and practised in the street, and it also learned from the street because it lived there every day. Plato and Aristotle were men among other men. They understood their society, and their society understood them. What matters most about them is less their books and meditations than the way they lived and the way they dealt with their fellow citizens.

We find this difficult to grasp because writing, books, academic careers, and intellectual circles have gradually divided Western societies into “uninformed classes” and “informed classes.” Each speaks a language the other does not understand, and they have almost stopped meeting at all. The people live apart while the doctrines of a mandarin caste rain down upon them.

Ancient culture was something entirely different. It was collective and belonged to everyone. Each person could take in at a glance the whole body of religion, art, and knowledge that belonged to the community. There was no music for initiates, no philosophy for specialists, no special vocabulary for scholars. What was aristocratic was also popular. No work of antiquity was performed for a closed circle.

Our intellectuals make a profession of educating the people. In earlier times it was the people who educated the intellectuals and opened their eyes. The greatest artist was still an artisan, entrusted with transmitting the images, ideas, and impressions he had received from the people, from whom he drew fulfilment and joy. Glory meant the approval and affection of one’s fellow citizens. Solitude, however brilliant, meant failure. If tradition has preserved the names of Empedocles, Plato, and Aristotle while forgetting the multitude of anonymous philosophers who filled classical antiquity, we should see this as an accident, not as a vote cast by public opinion.

There is scarcely a Greek philosopher who does not claim the East as his teacher, and hardly one who was not born there or travelled there for long periods, exploring its past and referring above all to Zoroaster. Plato does so, for example, in the myth of Er the Pamphylian at the end of the *Republic*. According to the apocryphal dialogue known as the *Magikos*, a Syrian Magus supposedly came all the way to Athens to foretell Socrates’ death. In the second century CE, the commentator Numenius described Plato as “Moses speaking Greek.” And in Xenophon’s *Apology of Socrates*, when the famous philosopher lists the heroes he hopes to meet in the afterlife, Orpheus comes first.

By the sixth century BCE, what we call Greek philosophy had taken shape outside the Greek peninsula, in Asia and in the Palestinian centres of southern Italy. Its towering figures were Pythagoras of Samos, Heraclitus of Ephesus, and Parmenides of Elea. Their heirs may disagree over which of the three deserves the greatest admiration, but one thing is certain: from the very beginning they raised Greek thought to its highest level, perhaps because they were the first to drink directly from the source.

We might ask why Hellenic philosophy appeared so late. For roughly a thousand years, perhaps longer, the Greeks had already been in close contact with the powerful spiritual systems of Egypt, Anatolia, and Babylonia. Why did the birth of Greek philosophy coincide with the rise, in the middle of the sixth century BCE, of the centralized states of Cyrus and Cambyses, who would rule the whole Arab world? There is no answer. We can say that the Greek language first had to develop enough to carry such thought, but that merely moves the problem rather than solving it.

It is reasonable, in any case, to suppose that a philosophy expressed in Aramaic existed among the Hellenes long before it began to speak Greek. Instead of “Greek philosophy,” it would therefore be more accurate to say “philosophy expressed in Greek.” That would save us from naïve claims like the following, which I quote exactly: “Clearly this was not a creation *ex nihilo*, since Greek thought found in Eastern thought a vast body of pre-existing material; … but there was nevertheless a creation, the appearance of a new principle: spirit. Greek thought is the birth of spirit. From the dark and profound soul as it existed in the East, spirit emerged in Greece: spirit as consciousness of itself, as certainty of its infinite nature, as consciousness of its independence from all external things, as certainty of its freedom. Freedom: this is what was born in Greece, and this is what the Greeks defended against the East.” (Charles Werner, *La Philosophie grecque*, Payot, 1962.)

In our universities, teaching rarely comes without some democratic, moralizing, or confessional mission attached to it. That Greek thought was only a lesson drawn from the East, a microcosm and reflection of Asia, is shown by the fact that Asia borrowed nothing from Hellenism and, on the contrary, gave it everything. Athens fertilized Rome, but not Alexandria, Babylon, or Mecca. Plato brought nothing to the Arab world, nor did Aristotle. The Greeks brought nothing to Judaism, Islam, or Christianity except a means of expression and transmission.

The East was operating on an entirely different scale from little Hellas. Plato, Pericles, and Alexander knew this perfectly well. It is we, not they, who turned Greece into the exporter of universal knowledge. Plato presented himself more modestly as a willing pupil. In the *Timaeus*, he places these paternal words in the mouth of an Egyptian priest addressing Solon: “You Greeks are only children.” We should take the true measure of things and not be more royalist than the king.

Perhaps the truth is simply this: through the Greeks, who made the secrets of the East accessible to a wider public, we know something of the teaching given in Egypt and Asia to priests, students, and the population at large — teaching of the kind still practised in the age of Jalal al-Din Rumi, or in our own medieval universities. Plato and others like him were above all arrangers and compilers. Their works were variations “in the manner of” older traditions. They were commentators of genius, comparable to Thomas Aquinas or al-Ghazali. Unfortunately, we have used their fame to block our view of the Eastern horizons for which they themselves were respectful spokesmen.

When we come to Pythagoras, we risk speculating without end because we know almost nothing about him. What we possess are scattered passages preserved as quotations by Empedocles, the doxographers, Philolaus, Plato, Aristotle, and Diogenes Laërtius. *Pythagoras* is a surname meaning “Pythian herald,” “spokesman for Python,” or, better, “advocate of Apollo.” This may explain the popular tradition that he was born of a virgin illuminated by the Sun.

An Asian, probably of Palestinian origin, Pythagoras was revered as a son of God, a priest of Orphism, and the head of a school. He is said to have spent twenty years with the masters of Thebes and Memphis. What exactly did he teach? Nothing that Eastern humanity had not already known for a very long time: that “God has neither body nor human head, but is a sacred and ineffable intelligence”; that the body dies but the soul is immortal; that the visible carries the invisible; and that the harmony of the universe is one and unchanging.

The mineral, vegetable, and animal realms interpenetrate and are carried along in the perpetual movement of the four elements: water, air, earth, and fire. Because that movement never stops, death and birth merge into one another, each defining the other. This last idea has too casually been interpreted as a picturesque belief in transmigration — a cat becoming a bird after death, or a rose turning into a fish. Pythagoras says nothing of the kind. He simply holds that when the body perishes it releases its eternal principle, which returns to the immensity of the cosmos from which it was never completely separated.

The soul’s ascent into the infinity of Paradise will be quicker and happier if, during earthly life, it has already “entered into Number.” In other words, before the body dies, the soul must drink from the secret of Death, without which there is no eternal life. For eternal life is a Number.

What Number? The Number of heaven, naturally: the one that governs the motion of the stars and the sun. Immutable, exact, and unique, it distributes the Numbers that order the movement of beings and things. It is the result of the divine equation that holds all the active forces of the universe in balance.

Pythagorean philosophy therefore appears at once as astronomical religion and mathematical science: a geometric figure animated by eternal laws. The soul itself must become geometric if it is to fit immediately into the figure of heaven. Once freed from the body, it will not be weighed or inspected for whiteness or blackness. It will be measured with square and compass before it is admitted to the serenity of exact spaces. Sin is a break in the design, a line that has gone astray.

Such a disembodied view of existence obviously leaves no room for psychology or for the so-called mental sciences in which our age takes such pleasure. In its purity, it closely matches the architectural, pyramidal vision the Egyptians had of the living order. Masters of Number and of geometry, both heavenly and earthly, they carried calculation so far that they turned it into a science of divination.

The Arabic proverb “the soul must drink death before death” expresses an idea that Pythagoras of Samos would not have rejected. Crowds, his disciple Empedocles of Acragas tells us in this passage from the *Purifications*, waited for him to speak “the word that saves”:

> … O my friends, I greet you. Here I am, forever freed  
> From death, an immortal god  
> Whom all revere as they should;  
> Ribbons crown me, and garlands  
> Have opened their flowers for me.  
> As soon as, escorted by my worshippers, I enter  
> The flourishing cities,  
> Men and women cover me with honours  
> And follow in my steps…  
> And those who for so long  
> Have been pierced by the knives of pain  
> Come to me seeking, against every illness,  
> The word that saves.

Greek philosophy never again freed itself from geometry or from the exaltation of Number. Modern science, in its own divination through theorem, may have pursued a similar path, but with one important difference. For Pythagoras, Number was simply the key to *enchantment* in the literal sense: entry beneath the vault of heaven. Modern science, by contrast, presents itself as a force of disenchantment. It treats Number as an end in itself and turns the temporary world of earth into a rational absolute — a contradiction in terms.

The spirit of Pythagoras and of the builders of the Pyramids may have left our engineers and educators, but it survives in art. Even when concealed beneath the apparent casualness of anarchic masters, art still turns to triangles, arcs, and the golden section. We know the reverence the Middle Ages had for the pentagon, a perfect figure of divine essence. Was it not the image of the Magi’s star, with five virtues distributed among its five points: “material, spiritual, intellectual, rational, and supersubstantial”?

The famous thirteenth-century mathematician Campanus of Novara, a commentator on Euclid, would inspire two guides of the Renaissance: Alberti and the Franciscan friar Luca Pacioli. In his celebrated *De divina proportione*, dedicated to Ludovico il Moro, Pacioli took up the Pythagorean ideas presented in Plato’s *Timaeus*. He called on art to build itself around the Golden Proportion, expressed through an equation involving √5. He described this proportion as occult, immutable, and irrational. Like the Holy Trinity, he said, it is unique and belongs to the *secretissima scientia*, the most secret science.

It is regrettable that this Pythagorean work, filled with Franciscan faith, has not yet been translated into French. It bears eloquent witness to a tradition that has run through our culture without interruption, a tradition in which divinity and knowledge have never been separated. Beyond Leonardo da Vinci’s combinations of hexahedra, tetrahedra, and projected cones, he must surely have seen, shining through them, the face of God. He too was a devotee of Number and of the Master of Samos.

If Apollo was the spiritual father of Pythagoras, then the younger Heraclitus of Ephesus seems, as his name suggests, to have been consecrated to Heracles. Both received the teaching of Zoroaster and of the Palestinian schools. The mystical lineage therefore remains unbroken.

The city in which Heraclitus was born was not only one of the most thoroughly Asian cities of Asia Minor; from early times it also had “kings of Ionia,” who still retained their title in the age of Christ. First vassals of the Palestinians of Tyre and Sidon, then of the Hittites and Babylonians, by Heraclitus’ time they owed allegiance to the Great King of Persia. They had the right to wear the purple cloak, carry the sceptre, and preside over the Eleusinian Mysteries.

More than a thousand years before Islam, the philosophy of Heraclitus is an expression of *maktub* — “it is written.” The key and unity of the universe lie in the Logos, the “Word.” This Word governs and permeates every thing and every creature with its strict law, leaving no room for freedom or chance: “There are, absolutely, laws of Fate” (fragment 159); “The sun cannot overstep its measure, or the Erinyes, servants of Justice, will find it out.”

It is pointless to claim that human beings possess some special privilege that allows them to understand their fate, still less alter it. “Man is by nature deprived of reason” (fragment 148). Thought and salvation are universal, not individual. Free will, personality, originality, individual thought — all of this is illusion, because strictly speaking “we are not.” Man by himself has neither knowledge nor merit. It is fire, and fire alone, “which advancing will judge and seize all things.” “Knowledge is not enclosed where man is; it is where God is.”

Few philosophers have expressed the vanity of man with so much sarcasm and contempt. Only Pascal can really be compared with Heraclitus, in his famous cry: “My God, how hollow and full of filth is the human heart!” The Ephesian never tires of invective: “The wisest man, compared with divinity, appears an ape.” “Human beliefs: children’s games.”

What remains for man, then, is submission: to entrust himself to God — in short, to sleep, since he cannot defy the inevitable. Put as sharply as possible, Heraclitus sees thought as something entirely foreign to existence. When someone opens his mouth and sets off down the path of intellectual argument, Heraclitus seems to assume he is dealing with a fool or a deceiver. “It is in their sleep that men work together as brothers in the becoming of the world.”

God must regulate and order the countless contradictions and oppositions of space and time, absorbing them into a nature that is itself both moving and contradictory. God is fire because he is perpetual consciousness. God is struggle, as in the Mesopotamian theology of Anu and Ea, where all creation is the result of universal conflict. God is irrational because he is absolute.

Hermaphroditic like Ishtar-Astarte, night and light like Osiris, the god of Heraclitus is the “unity of opposites”: “What is opposed, in composing itself eternally, establishes itself”; “Neither shadow nor light, neither evil nor good, differs in nature; their nature is one and the same.” “To live by death and die by life” is Heraclitus’ poetic formula, and one that Saint John of the Cross might have made his own.

Fear of the inexplicable has certainly impoverished human existence. Heraclitus wants to transform that fear into worship and teach people to love what cannot be undone, to see justice even in the harshest necessities — in short, to prefer the struggle to victory. For in any case, “man may escape sensible fire; he will never escape intelligible fire.”

According to Plutarch, Heraclitus was inspired by the myth of Isis and Osiris. In his thought, the moon and sun are indeed represented allegorically by boats, just as in Egypt. Zoroastrian fire worship, the theory of opposites dear to Egyptian and Mesopotamian thought, the Islamic effacement of the human being within the immanence of God, the search for a hidden harmony greater than visible harmony, scepticism used to justify faith — all of these meet and find fulfilment in Heraclitus of Ephesus.

It is unfortunate that his work has reached us only in fragments and second-hand quotations, obscured by abrupt breaks that sometimes let a cry from the depths of time break through: “The Sibyl, who with frenzied mouth utters painful words in their harsh nakedness, carries her voice across millennia through the power of the god who inspires her.”

Again and again the Sun returns to Heraclitus’ mind: source, master, and ruler. “The Sun, master and guardian of periodic revolutions, determines and directs, arouses and reveals transformations.” One could be listening to Pythagoras. “The sun is new every day, for it shares in Dionysian power.” One might think it a line from an Orphic poem.

Asia is unmistakably present here. Greece is present only in the writing.

Modern followers of Heraclitus, beginning with Hegel, have taken advantage of the fragmentary and uneven form of his surviving work to fish out a few forceful formulas that could be made to serve Marxism, socialism, or the most intellectual forms of dialectics. This is all the more irritating an abuse of trust because Heraclitus was first and foremost a believer — the exact opposite of an intellectual. His statements begin with tangible realities and are accompanied by an unusual refusal of purely cerebral argument, which for him is worth almost nothing. He is more a Muslim before Islam than an ancestor of Lenin.

The third of the great Presocratic philosophers, Parmenides, was not himself Asian, but his family belonged to the small city of Elea in Lucania, deep within a Greek-Palestinian region crossed by Etruscan influences. A contemporary of Heraclitus, he built his teaching on what sounds like a truism: “Being is; non-being is not.”

If Being exists absolutely and was never created, then just as generation is inconceivable, destruction must be inconceivable too. Immutable, complete, and inviolable, God is represented by Parmenides as a perfectly motionless allegorical sphere. Only immobility is lucid; movement is merely distraction.

Pythagoras defines universal reality as an algebraic power, Heraclitus as a will, and Parmenides sees it mechanically: the universe is made of vibrating wheels. His theory of finite Being has often been set against Heraclitus’ doctrine of becoming. But this is largely a matter of words, since for Heraclitus everything changes except change itself. Motion occurs only within a perfect coherence that can never move into incoherence.

For both thinkers, the principle behind all principles remains the Fire of Zoroaster. The sphere of Parmenides closely resembles the celestial figure of Pythagoras, itself inspired by the Orphic egg, the perfect and creative curve. In Parmenides too, extremes meet and contradictions cancel one another out. At the centre of his rings of fire, dynamic symbols of the universe, stands the “Sovereign Goddess,” Eros or Aphrodite, at once male and female, light and darkness, like Ishtar:

> But beneath the earth there is a road of dread,  
> Deep and muddy; better than any other it leads  
> To the enchanting realm of Aphrodite, rich in offerings.  
> (Fragment 30.)

Parmenides too had noticed that the morning star and the evening star are one and the same body. If we strip away the poetic clothing in which these three Presocratic thinkers dress their ideas, we can see clearly what they share: the sun, eternity, supreme abstraction, perpetual life, and a non-figurative conception of the universe in which substance itself is, so to speak, volatilized into the ineffable.

When they nevertheless resort to “figures” to express this transcendent truth, they do so as a musician uses sound to indicate silence, or as a poet lays words one upon another to suggest what cannot be said. If a philosophy is to reach the heart of the people and strike the understanding, it must be made tangible through some simple image — especially when, as here, the philosophers are physicists who are also musicians and poets.

The most serious explanation of Pythagoras, Heraclitus, and Parmenides should therefore be sought at the source of their ideas: in the Egyptian *Book of the Dead* and in the sacred texts of Palestine and Mesopotamia. Their real method is a lyrical synthesis, and for that very reason it escapes our hyper-intellectualized logic as surely as Surrealism escapes classical order.

Our language traps us. Our determination to explain everything leads us to overanalyse mystical and philosophical systems that stand very close to one another, hunting for differences whose relative distinctness often comes down to minor details. Ancient commentators were less fussy than we are. Hippolytus, bishop of Ostia in the third century, writes, for example: “Parmenides too, although he declares that the All is one, eternal, ungenerated, and spherical, nevertheless shares the mistaken beliefs of most philosophers of his time when he makes fire and earth the principles of the All: earth as matter, fire as cause and creative agent.”[^pierre-rossi-lci-001]

Plato’s dialogue *Parmenides*, which brings Socrates, Parmenides, and his disciple Zeno together in a literary fiction, ends in much the same spirit. After a brilliant stream of talk, full of irony about the existence of non-being and the non-existence of being, the speakers arrive at a general agreement: each concedes that his opponent is right… everything being in everything, and vice versa.

A smile, in fact, seems to run through Plato’s work from beginning to end. Is it possible that we take Plato more seriously than he took himself? There is no reason to be scandalized by the question. It deserves thought. What lies behind the theatrical façade of this astonishing sophist? There is something of Hamlet in his Socrates, and perhaps something of the charlatan too. After all, can charlatanism not have its geniuses?

The great currents that fertilized East and West were not called Platonism, Aristotelianism, or Stoicism. They were the myths of Isis and Baal, solar cults, Orphism, Palestinian-Anatolian apocalyptic occultism, Judaism, Christianity, and Islam — traditions infecting and transforming one another through extraordinary waves of influence. Platonism is a ship carried along by these powerful currents. It follows them; it does not control them. Above all, it creates nothing fundamentally new, because the deep work of creation had already been done over the preceding millennia.

Political, aesthetic, artisanal, spiritual, technical, and moral creations are formed in the silence and inward concentration of the great religions, not on the surface of philosophical speculation, however Platonic that speculation may be.

Plato’s life is perhaps more interesting than his doctrine, which can be seen as a synthesis of syntheses already carried out by the Presocratics. A follower of Heraclitus, Orphism, and Pythagoras, he went abroad to complete his education: to Egypt, where he stayed for a long time; to Libya, where at Cyrene he was received by the Pythagorean Theodorus; to Sicily, which would haunt him throughout his life; and to southern Italy. All of these were Arab countries, or regions deeply influenced by Palestinian politics and thought.

Sold into slavery during the war between Aegina and Athens, Plato was bought and freed by a Libyan. Back home, he founded the Academy and spent the end of his life preaching philosophy in the streets of Athens while also taking part in the political struggles that shook Greece badly at the beginning of the fourth century BCE.

His philosophical work, published comparatively late, is built for the most part around an imaginary or semi-imaginary figure called Socrates, an exact counterpart to great mythical heroes such as Heracles, Jason, Solon, and Tiresias. Plato’s work is, in this sense, the saga of Socrates just as the *Odyssey* is the saga of Odysseus. This tells us how epic, popular, and legendary the thought of the master of the Academy really is.

Philosophical discussion in Plato is always theatrical. It brings together protagonists of ideal stature whose nature remains abstract and symbolic. Like gods carrying numerical values, they take part in a choreography of obedient figures governed by Dionysian laws of the stage such as Aeschylus or Sophocles had imagined. Plato’s actors speak and mime their thought on the raised boards of poetry. They are performing a rite.

This has nothing in common with our philosophy lessons, conducted in sealed classrooms under the banner of agnostic rationalism. Here we are, on the contrary, in the middle of a religious service. Beside those who argue or meditate aloud stand Apollo, Dionysus, Demeter, Aphrodite, the “great gods,” Minos, and Orpheus.

The words spoken often carry two or three meanings at once. The doors of knowledge open only a crack onto forms that are suggested but never exposed in hard light. Thought proceeds through approximations, touches and retouches without end. Sometimes a sentence stops in mid-flight, as though afraid of revealing too much. Silence speaks. The voices belong to twilight.

This is Socratic irony. The Greek word *eironeia* means, etymologically, an “interweaving” or “coded combination” and derives from *eiro*, “to braid, knot, entangle.” The point is to intrigue the candidate for knowledge, not to illuminate everything for him. Think of the parables of the Gospel. Platonic dialogue is similarly enigmatic, so that only those who have received grace will understand. “Do not show heaven to someone who cannot see it,” says the old maxim.

Plato’s method remains religious. In his letters he himself explains how secrecy and long patience are necessary for any initiation. He even advises against fixing such teaching in writing, for fear that it will be misread. He writes to Dionysius of Syracuse: “Take care that one day you do not regret what you now allow to be unworthily disclosed. The greatest precaution would be not to write, but to learn by heart, because written words inevitably end up in the public domain. For this reason I myself have never written anything on such subjects. There is no written work by Plato, and there never will be. The works now said to be mine belong to Socrates in the days when he was beautiful and young. Farewell, and obey: when you have read and reread this letter, burn it.”

We can see, then, what Socrates is doing there: he preserves the author’s anonymity while giving the dialogue a legendary dimension. A portrait, Pascal said, carries both absence and presence. The chiaroscuro portrait of Socrates seems to fit the definition.

Plato makes so many contradictory claims and delights in smashing ideas against one another so unpredictably that we often wonder where he is trying to lead us. There is no real possibility of summarizing a body of work built from evasions and threaded with subterranean allusions to Egyptian or Babylonian initiation.

Still, a few articles of faith emerge, all familiar from Asian thought. First comes the claim that sensible reality is only the reflection of an Idea, and that the Idea is eternal, like the divinity from which it proceeds and like the soul, which is intimately bound to that divinity. It follows that the soul, made in the image of God, is immortal. Temporarily imprisoned in a mortal body, it longs for the death that will release it, just as the swan sings before dying at the approach of the God it is about to rejoin.

The second conclusion is that the soul naturally possesses true knowledge, which is knowledge of the Ideas. It therefore needs only contemplation and recollection to bring back into consciousness the knowledge that has belonged to it from eternity and that it could not lose without losing itself. The soul does not need to learn. It already knows. True knowledge lies in morality, not in the study of what Pascal would later call “external things.”

The body interferes with the soul’s understanding. The more the soul frees itself from the body’s grip, the higher it rises into the heaven of knowledge, until at last it merges into God.

God, and this is the third and final certainty, is the King of the universe, the author of harmony, the supreme end and sole reason of all things: beyond the reach of the understanding and impossible for mortal eyes to contemplate. In ending with the worship of the ineffable, Plato rejoins Heraclitus and Parmenides by way of Pythagoras, explicitly placing himself in the lineage of all three.

In the *Charmides*, Plato tells us that Socrates — that is, Plato himself — had once served alongside a Thracian physician who taught him about the correspondences between body and soul. We should remember that in the ancient texts of Gnosticism, Orpheus is indeed called the Physician of Souls, or the “Thracian Physician.”

Again and again Platonic dialogue returns to the sphere, beloved by both Pythagoras and Parmenides, and to a sphere containing a trinity: “Around the King of the universe all beings revolve; he is the end of all things and the cause of all beauty. Around the ‘Second’ are the second things, and around the ‘Third’ the third things.” (*Letter II*, section 312.)

Here we are squarely inside the Egyptian-Babylonian tradition that can be traced throughout the history of Eastern religions: trilogy, triad, trinity, triangle. Plato returns to it repeatedly. He divides the soul into three “levels”: bodily desire, moral courage, and ideal intelligence. In the same way, he divides universal reality into three: “*The first of goods is God; the second is the intelligence generated by him; the third is the soul of the world, the bond between the Father and the Son.*”

We should remember those final words. They would carry great weight in Christian scholastic teaching. More than five hundred years after Plato’s death, this famous trinity would be invoked again in the form given in the *Parmenides*: “*the Supreme One, the One-Many, and the One and Many*.” Plotinus would draw from it the conclusion (*Enneads* V, 1, 8, 490a) that Plato too already believed in the Christian theory of three natures: “The first belongs to the Ineffable, the second to Intelligence, the third to the Soul.”

Belief in a holy Trinity was not new in Plato’s time, and he had no difficulty explaining its esoteric meaning, if only through the Eleusinian Mysteries. Nor is it surprising that Christianity inherited the key to it. Since the Trinity is absent from both Judaism and Islam, we may conclude that each of these three religions, although ultimately drawing on a common source, remained attached to its own tradition, distinct from neighbouring currents.

Instead of following the natural direction of inquiry and explaining Plato through Egyptian-Palestinian religions and cosmogonies, scholars have generally worked backwards, using Plato to explain or justify Christianity and its origins. Yet one need only remember that every Egyptian temple is dedicated to a triad of three gods: the first is the male principle, the second female, and the third the product of the first two. Yet these three gods are one. The Father generates himself within the womb of the Mother and becomes both Father and Son. In this way the uncreated and eternal nature of Being is expressed.

More than Plato, his disciple Aristotle was repeatedly appropriated by the doctrinal authorities of the Roman Catholic Church, eager to support its dogmatic system with a coherent physics and a cosmology that could be made to match Scripture. Aristotle’s philosophy was reduced to an artificial and contrived system, emptied of its living substance, and turned into the sacred skeleton of scholasticism.

One of Blaise Pascal’s central aims was to attack intellectualism and restore the living water of faith to the Church. He wanted, as it were, to drive Aristotle out and replace him with Jesus. In the end, however, Pascal was defeated by Cartesianism and by academic philosophy. From then on, an Aristotle corrected and recast by the university — stripped of his Asian culture and isolated from the society that had formed him — stood like a plaster ornament over the entrance to the temple of received ideas. A false Aristotle.

One of the most prominent figures responsible for this falsification is Thomas Aquinas. The *Summa contra Gentiles* and *Summa Theologiae* reveal such a regression of the spirit that the irreversible corruption of the so-called intellectual classes can be dated from Aquinas’s age.

Yet the richest human values still survived and flowered in the sovereign art of the Romanesque style. This was possible only because of the people. Against the full force of destructive intellectualism, ordinary people, sustained by their primitive faith, managed to preserve what was essential in the Arab-Mediterranean tradition.

People instinctively distrust hyper-rational systems because in their eyes Hell is the world of mechanisms while Peace belongs to intuitive harmony. Has not popular wisdom proclaimed a thousand times the fearless superiority of the ignorant person over the scholar? In a healthy reaction against the pretensions of swollen rationalism — a prelude to spiritual death — people return all the more readily to nature the more their official thinkers try to free themselves from it.

Those thinkers wanted to keep only Aristotle’s rigorous method while discarding what was, in fact, the heart of both his work and his life: the worship of nature and of living creatures as they celebrate the divine. As a stern warning against excessive abstraction — if not Plato’s own, then certainly that of his followers — Aristotle brought humanity back to earth. He re-embodied philosophy in an exacting physics in which animal, vegetable, and mineral powers are presented as masters of the human being, not as obedient material for human speculation.

In Aristotle, mind and instinct remain closely joined in a natural and concrete balance. There can be no happiness, for him, in a society that rejects natural law or in which the simultaneous presence of heaven, earth, and sea, of human beings, animals, and trees, can no longer be felt.

With Aristotle, then, we return to the botanical and zoological paradise of Egyptian, Palestinian, and Greek religions: a world in which every animal is divine because it proceeds from God, and every stone is worthy of reverence because it is a sign and finger of the Creator. Human destiny lies in a marriage with nature, the only way to re-enter the order of creation. To live is to take pleasure in living among beings whose inner laws we understand.

We must therefore learn in order to know. But once knowledge has been acquired, we must meditate on it and move beyond it into philosophy — that is, into knowledge of the ultimate cause and final end. Science studies beings; philosophy seeks substance.

Beings consist of matter that has received a form. Yet matter itself escapes every definition and every act of perception. It exists only through the form of which it is an emanation and which constitutes its essence, its first and eternal substance. Here Aristotle stands very close to Plato and the theory of Ideas. The soul is the form of the body, and in its most rarefied part — the intellect — it is imperishable and divine.

Its joy is to join the whole universe in the contemplation of God, the eternal, unique, unmoving mover of the cosmos, whose perfection is a source of true jubilation. In its basic principles and overall vision, Aristotle’s philosophy comes very close to that of Heraclitus, who could almost provide its keynote. The main difference may be Aristotle’s happier tone: his *Physics*, *Metaphysics*, and *Nicomachean Ethics* become a hymn of glory to the Creator. Heraclitus remains more darkly contemplative, though most — perhaps all — of his work has been lost.

Where Aristotle truly differs from his masters and predecessors is in his brilliant attempt to reconstruct the stages, method, and hierarchy of the creation of the world. Beginning with the mineral, vegetable, and animal kingdoms, he rebuilds the universal family tree and moves step by step upward to the sovereign mover whose secret he seems to know. His work is therefore a cosmogony in reverse.

Aristotle moves by reasoning from creature to God. Hesiod, Orpheus, and the great Mesopotamian ancestors begin with God and then discover the creatures. The two approaches appear to be diametrically opposed. The first looks scientific and rational; the second, that of the Mesopotamians of the second millennium BCE, is openly irrational, driven by an act of faith — in other words, by intuition.

Yet Aristotle would not be seeking God if he had not already found him. His philosophy, a highly revealing hymn to joy, resembles the classic syllogisms in which the conclusion is already contained in the wording of the premise.

A pupil of the East through Plato, Aristotle returned eastward many times. In particular he went to Assos in Asia Minor, where he remained for more than three years, before settling on Lesbos, an Orphic island and a major centre of occult lyricism.

Why do scholars exhaust themselves searching Plato for the source of Aristotle, as though coming earlier automatically meant causing what came later? The influences on Aristotle were by no means all Platonic. He too must have gone back to the sources and absorbed the religious atmosphere shared by the peoples of the eastern Mediterranean. No Greek philosopher escaped it. Each responded according to his own temperament, but none altered the underlying features of the Egyptian-Palestinian culture that surrounded beings and things.

Why not investigate Aristotle’s intellectual sponsors in Asia rather than in Greece? We must keep putting this question to those who reduce history to the European navel and leave in darkness so many lands that carried its light to Europe.

Clearchus of Soli, among other commentators, tells us that during his stay in the Troad Aristotle held several philosophical discussions with Syrians. Book V of the *Metaphysics* dates from his time at Assos, and reveals an enthusiastic admiration for Zoroaster.

But Aristotle, like Plato, did not confine himself to philosophical preaching. He was politically shrewd and worked with Hermias, ruler of Atarneus, who in turn maintained excellent relations with Philip of Macedon. When the young Alexander later launched his Asian expedition, Aristotle would be there as adviser and guide.

After Hermias was executed on the orders of the Persian governor for conspiring against the internal security of the state, Aristotle — by then at the court of Pella as Alexander’s tutor — delivered his funeral oration and compared him with the heroes of the sacred Mysteries: Heracles, the Dioscuri, Ajax, and Achilles.

What role did Aristotle actually play while Alexander was away in Asia? We do not know. He remained in Athens, where he had founded the Peripatetic school at the Lyceum, then withdrew to Chalcis, where he died only a year after his pupil’s death in Babylon.

The double character of so-called Athenian philosophy — physical and moral, represented by the Academy and the Lyceum — became clear among Aristotle’s successors. Some stressed physics; others morality.

The Arab school of Libya, under Antisthenes and Aristippus of Cyrene, emphasized the role of the senses, pleasure, and pain, and defined happiness as a balance among the different parts of the organism. Democritus and the Ionian philosopher Epicurus of Samos belong to this sensualist current. In broad outline, both remain within the geometric, perfect, and eternal universe of Parmenides.

But where Parmenides stops, Epicurus pushes further into the elements that make up the universe. He sees primordial reality as a dust of indivisible atoms. These atoms whirl endlessly, joining and separating, carrying nature through a perpetual round of life and death, forever beginning again.

Love — Aphrodite — directs this endless play. She is queen of attraction, of atomic coupling and the breaking apart of bodies, of beginning and end. The Latin poet Lucretius, whose *De rerum natura* is a poetic exposition of Epicurean thought, celebrates with delight the all-powerful Venus as she conducts the marriages and separations of ethereal particles.

The moral outlook that follows from such a vision — for it is really a vision more than a philosophy in the strict sense — is perfectly serene and consoling. It asks the human being to surrender to the radiant rain of atoms.

It is impossible not to think of Akhenaten’s hymn, conceived in the sun-filled desert of Tell el-Amarna, or of the Qur’anic account of “whirlwinds” and “indivisible particles.” Returned to the very real world of a physics in which every cell of the body participates, the human being becomes plant, water, and earth, no longer different in any essential way from the creatures around him. He loses every special privilege and authority.

Absorbed into the creative currents that cross one another along countless slopes, weaving a dense fabric that flowers into animate and inanimate beings born from the same seed, he encounters God everywhere. His pleasure lies precisely in remaining exposed to the wind of these original and miraculous currents.

For, of course, everything is miracle in Epicurus. Every instant is both spring and winter, because at every instant a being forms and dissolves in the blood of the world.

More than an original philosophy, Epicurean theory is, quite clearly, a poetic commentary on the great Eastern syntheses represented by Plato and Aristotle, adding nothing essential to them. Long before Epicurus, beneath the outward appearance of polytheism, Egypt, Anatolia, and Assyria had spoken of emanations and interchangeable universal seeds from which the anatomy of every order and species was formed. At the sound of Orpheus’s lyre, stones came alive, waters divided, and the cells of the dead gathered themselves together to live again.

Alongside the Arab school of Libya that reinvented atomic physics stood another Arab school: that of the Palestinian Zeno of Citium, who around 315 BCE ran an import-export business in Cyprus. Some commentators describe him as Jewish, which is plausible. Zeno is generally regarded as the founder of Stoicism.

His spiritual successor was another Palestinian, Cleanthes, from the city of Assos — the very place where Aristotle had received the Zoroastrian revelation. Cleanthes was followed by the famous Chrysippus, a Cilician from Tarsus, the birthplace of Saint Paul.

Stoicism is simply a restoration of the philosophy of Heraclitus and of *maktub*, recast in a somewhat pedantic moral form. Nature, God, Fate, and Universal Reason are almost interchangeable names. The universe is a kind of solid body animated by fire, and the human being is set into it like a stone in a building.

Divine intelligence, or Providence, orders everything perfectly. Even evil is ultimately an element within the good. To our imperfect eyes it may be hateful, but to God, who sees every side of reality at once, evil is only the necessary reverse of good, just as night is nothing more than a day that does not yet know itself.

We must therefore learn to love fate even when it is at its harshest toward us, because that harshness too has its place in the natural order. The Stoic duty is to rise above personal interests and comfort and give thanks to Providence for all its decisions.

To accept one’s nature, to become what one is, to submit to the laws of the city and of the universe — since the city of human beings is only a projection of the City of God — to practise fraternity and charity, and to wait bravely for destiny, welcoming it gladly whatever form it takes: this is Stoicism.

“Everything suits me that suits you, O world! Nothing comes too early or too late for me if it comes at the right time for you. Everything your seasons bring is fruit to me, O Nature! Everything comes from you; everything is in you; everything returns to you.” (Marcus Aurelius IV, 23, quoted by Ch. Werner, *La Philosophie grecque*, Payot, 1962.)

The Islamic resonance of such words can be felt immediately. Heraclitus of Ephesus could have spoken in exactly this way. *Inshallah*: the whole Stoic faith is contained in this expression, which gathers hope, trust, and self-effacement into a few syllables.

Greek philosophy began in the East and would return to the East to reach its fulfilment, in Egypt, Syria, and Palestine. From the first century CE onward, the Syrians Libanius and Numenius of Apamea revived and developed Pythagoreanism and Platonism, while in Egypt four names shone in the firmament of universal philosophy: Philo the Jew, Ammonius Saccas, Plotinus, from Assiut on the middle Nile, and Porphyry.

And there were many others: Athenodorus of Cana, teacher of the emperor Augustus; Diogenes of Babylon; Apollodorus, the Iraqi from Seleucia on the Tigris; Sosos of Ascalon; and more.

Finally, both Old and New Testaments would be written and commented on in Greek, as though bearing witness to the coherence and continuity of a culture that, Arab in its earliest origins, became Arab-Aegean by the middle of the second millennium BCE and then spread into the West.

Christianity first and Islam later brought Egyptian-Mesopotamian traditions together with Greek forms in their respective syntheses. Judaism and the various Zoroastrian cults also made use of Greek.
