All of this makes our conclusion still clearer: everything essential in our culture was formed continuously and exclusively in the Greek-Aramaic lands. The West consumed and adapted that culture. It did not invent it.

# V

## ASTRONOMY AND THE ART OF LIVING

Religion, philosophy, science — everything is connected. Just as there is no such thing as philosophy for its own sake or art for art’s sake, there is no purely speculative science. In the East, science is always applied. It stays close to the tangible, solid world of nature and studies heaven and earth for practical ends. Even mathematics is treated as something almost physical, woven into the substance of the world. Numbers are active forces, “dynamic magnitudes.” A circle is an area; a straight line is a taut cord; division is a sharing out; the square of a number is literally a square space. The Pythagorean theorem, for example, can be expressed as one area equal to the sum of two others. The Socratic maxim “Let no one enter here who is not a geometer,” barring the uninitiated from philosophy, should be understood in a narrow and literal sense. The East rejects chaos and looks for order in nature as it does in the mind; a person without inner order is a heart without light. Science, then, is above all knowledge of the universal order. Scientific inquiry means discovering and classifying the signs of that order so that human life can be brought into harmony with it. Morality is the outcome of science, and wisdom is its foundation.

The Greeks themselves always acknowledged Egypt and Babylonia as their teachers in science. According to Josephus, “it was through Abraham that Chaldean science passed to the Egyptians, and from them to the Greeks.” Legend pushed the first Chaldean astronomical discoveries back 470,000 years before our era. Astronomy was, after all, the first science: the discipline that explained the close relationship between the heavens and an earth filled with living creatures. Astrology soon grew alongside it.

The legendary beginnings of astronomy lie far beyond anything we can measure, but the fragments recovered by archaeology show that Sargon the Elder, whose reign is dated to 3800 BCE, had already established an astronomical library. Babylon learned early about the precession of the equinoxes, the cycle of lunar eclipses, the positions of the fixed stars, the year of 365¼ days, and the division of the day into two periods of twelve hours each. Its system of numbers already reflected both curved and linear geometry. Alongside calculation by tens and twelves stood the base of sixty: the circle divided into 360 degrees, each degree into 60 minutes, and each minute into 60 seconds.

The Babylonians also arrived at a cycle of 43,200 years, which they regarded as one day in the life of the universe. Our year of 365 days represented one second of that cosmic day; sixty years made one minute of the universal cycle; 3,600 years made one hour, and so on. Thales of Miletus carried this knowledge into Greek science, which drew on it just as heavily as it drew on Egyptian astronomy, whose roots reached back into prehistory.

Like the Chaldean calendar, the pharaonic calendar was lunisolar, combining approximately the 365-day solar cycle with the moon’s phases of twenty-nine days. The Egyptians divided the year into three seasons, each made up of four thirty-day months. Spring began in April with the appearance of Sirius. Plutarch tells us that the Egyptians called the star Sothis, while the Greeks called it *Seirios aster*, “the blazing star.” Because the rising of Sirius shifts over time, 1,460 years are needed for its heliacal rising to return to the same point — that is, for its appearance to coincide again with the rising sun. The Romans called this the “dog” or “dog-star” cycle. It appears repeatedly in the chronology of heroic traditions.

It is worth noting that neither the Greeks nor the Romans were ever fully initiated into Egypto-Babylonian astronomy. For a long time, its most knowledgeable heirs were the Arabs of the Byzantine and Muslim empires. Greece never had observatories, and neither did Rome, while the Aramean lands from the Nile to the Indus were full of them. Every Mesopotamian, Hittite, or Persian city had its own.

Until Caesar’s reform, the Greco-Roman calendars were extremely poor. They were clumsy copies of Eastern astronomical tables, easily manipulated by political authorities, and often replaced in practice by rough local calendars that differed from one region or trade to another. Once Julius Caesar realized that this confusion over dates could seriously threaten the administration of an empire now open to the whole East, he had to impose a calendar that was both scientific and universal.

He entrusted the reform of the Roman calendar to Sosigenes, an Arab from Alexandria. In 46 BCE, in order to bring the calendar into line with an astronomical year of 365¼ days, Sosigenes ordered that from 45 BCE onward an extra 24 February should be inserted every four years, the *dies bissextus ante Kalendas*. Years of 366 days would henceforth be leap years. This so-called “Julian” calendar should therefore really be called the “Alexandrian” calendar.

The best evidence of the West’s ignorance of astronomy is the way Caesar’s successors mishandled the leap year until a further correction became necessary. On 24 February 1582, with the bull *Inter gravissimas*, Pope Gregory XIII — again with the help of Arab mathematicians — restored the order of the days and introduced the Gregorian calendar.

For all its precision, however, such a calendar remains theoretical. It gives us only an abstract idea of time. The ancient civilizations of the East used other, subtler methods to measure time as it was actually lived. Modern science has largely lost sight of them, though traces survive in popular customs and rituals. The Palestinians used a maritime calendar. The Yemenis and the peoples of the Arabian-Persian Gulf had a Pleiades calendar that spread among desert nomads as far as the Mauritanian coast. According to Louis Massignon, this was a remarkable calendar of rain and dew: the year began with the heliacal rising of the Pleiades and lasted about 364 days, or thirteen 28-day lunar cycles.

There were also secret calendars reserved for priests, built around pure numbers that were considered dangerous for humanity. Rome would derive from these its lists of lucky and unlucky days. We have no firm evidence that the Greeks or Romans observed a regular weekly rest day outside their festivals. The Assyro-Babylonians, by contrast, seem to have known the seven-day week, with one day set aside for complete rest.

They passed the sundial, the water clock, and the gnomon — the upright rod used to cast a measuring shadow — to their western neighbours, while keeping the astrolabe and perhaps the compass to themselves. Medieval Europeans, direct heirs of Greco-Roman science, were astonished to discover astronomical instruments and navigational devices among the Arabs that were completely unknown to them. They assumed these devices must have been recent Arab inventions, never considering that the Arabs might have preserved knowledge reaching back into a much more distant past.

The Greeks, in fact, acquired only part of the mathematical and astronomical knowledge that had been built up in the East over thousands of years. They had neither the schools nor the scholars nor the economic power needed to absorb a scientific system so far beyond the scale of their society and their knowledge. The vastness of the Eastern world fascinated them. They stood before it like schoolchildren before a master and were unable to transmit the whole of its legacy. The Arabs did.

The mathematics that so fascinated the Greeks, though they never fully mastered it, grew out of astronomy, land surveying, and the practical need for systems of weights and measures. We possess no Egyptian or Babylonian treatise on geometry or arithmetic from antiquity apart from a handful of papyri containing elementary school material. Yet the enormous achievements of the Pyramids and the temples of Karnak and Nineveh point to an extraordinary command of calculation and three-dimensional space.

There was also a persistent attempt to connect the straight line with the curve, the enclosed figure with the open sky, astronomical time with the layout of the temple, and permanence with the temporary. This brought into the mathematics of the Nile the idea of periodic and even transcendental functions: a mathematics involving several dimensions of change rather than simply the mechanics of solid objects.

In his chapter on the meaning of numbers,[^pierre-rossi-lci-002] Spengler, despite his unconventional view of the East, still remains too aesthetic in his analysis and gives the Greeks far too much credit. He draws arbitrary boundaries between Arab algebra, which he dates to the fourth century CE, Hindu trigonometry, and ancient mechanics. Yet the complex numbers and logarithmic relations that enter not only into the design of monuments but also into the numerical myths of the Egypto-Mesopotamian Arab tradition — long before the fourth century CE — point to a less simple conclusion.

Spengler sees the human mind historically, and therefore as something that evolves. He builds a whole series of territorial and chronological boundaries, only to prove remarkably good at jumping over them himself. He does not want the mental structure of an Egyptian or Assyrian to be the same as ours. But it is. The difference is one of form, not substance. Equations with several unknowns were as familiar to the builders of the Pyramids as they are to us. They were just as capable of including the irrational within a rational formula.

Many things may explain why advanced mathematical treatises have vanished from the archaeological record of Egypt and Mesopotamia. One possibility deserves particular attention because it touches on the ethics of science. The Egyptians and Babylonians regarded this mathematics as dangerous because their specialists understood its true power. They therefore kept it hidden. The astonishing architectural and technical achievements that still leave us in awe show how far they had gone in their understanding of functions and imaginary numbers.

On a more modest level, Egypt was also where the symbols used to write the numbers one through nine were adapted. The Palestinians adopted this system, and it eventually became our own under the name “Arabic numerals.” The Greeks, who used the twenty-four letters of their alphabet in sequence to write numbers, borrowed at least three numerical signs from the Palestinians: the *episemon* for six; the *koppa* for ninety, which is simply the Egypto-Palestinian *coph*; and the *san-pi* for nine hundred, which is exactly the Egypto-Palestinian *sin*.

Most of the units of measurement used in Athens, Sparta, or Syracuse likewise came from Babylonia or Palestine, which had long possessed an advanced system of weights and measures later found in Rome and the Roman provinces. Some units even kept their Arab names unchanged: *hamma*, a land measure of sixty feet; *mna* or mina, equivalent to 620 grams; *drakma*; *obolos*; and *talanton*, the talent.

Hellas acquired its units of measurement through Aegina and Euboea, but the Athenian Solon regulated their use after returning from Egypt. This must have been only an imperfect adaptation of Eastern practice, because under the Ptolemies, in the third century BCE, greater precision was considered necessary. New legislation extended the Egyptian system throughout Hellas. From there, largely through the activity of Greek merchants, it spread across the Mediterranean and into the hinterlands.

Greek mathematics should therefore not be exaggerated. The truth compels us to say that every Greek mathematician whose name has come down to us was, without exception, of Arab origin: Greek was the language of their science, but their families came from Egypt or Asia Minor. As we will see later, Alexandria was the great mathematical centre of antiquity, inheriting the accumulated experience of Thebes, Tyre, Sardis, and Babylon. Euclid and Diophantus were Egyptians.

The records of Athens, Corinth, the Peloponnese, and Boeotia have preserved the name of no mathematician and no scientist. It is curious that this absence has attracted so little attention, so absorbed have we been by Greek art, Greek philosophy, and the Greek ideal.

Zoology, botany, and mineralogy — subjects that delighted Aristotle and Pliny the Elder — were born in Mesopotamia, where the natural world had already been rigorously classified and catalogued by around 1000 BCE. The same applies to anatomy. Where did we get the entirely mistaken idea that the study and dissection of the human body were forbidden in the East? We find no trace of such a taboo. By contrast, we know in detail about the delicate procedures priest-physicians performed on bodies before embalming them. We also have anatomical illustrations and models of organs made in metal or stone.

Obstetrics, amputation, and trepanation — surgically opening the skull — necessarily required extensive knowledge of physiology. Hippocrates makes no secret of the instruction he received from Egypt; the surviving fragments of his work are there to be consulted. Arab medicine, which dominated Europe until the eighteenth century, did not suddenly begin with the caliphs, nor was it merely an adaptation of Greek medicine. It developed naturally, generation after generation, from medical practice and anatomical knowledge that had matured for centuries along the Nile and the Euphrates.

Why continue with the seemingly endless list of techniques mastered in ancient Arab civilization? Egypt, Palestine, Phrygia, and Mesopotamia excelled in at least five fields and reached a level of technical skill that has still not been surpassed: metallurgy, goldsmithing, glassmaking, stonecutting, and weaving.

Bronzework was a Palestinian speciality, as the excavations at Gezer show, and was already highly developed by the middle of the third millennium BCE. Iron was used long before the Greeks, having been introduced by the end of the second millennium. Mines in Sinai, Lebanon, the Jordanian regions, and the Caucasus were worked regularly. The cities of Thebes, Tyre, Sardis, Ilium, and Nineveh were famous for their arms workshops. Swords, daggers, shields, and breastplates found at Mycenae, Cyprus, and Crete are exact copies of Egypto-Asian weapons — unless they were imported directly.

Should we not, then, look to Assyria for the beginnings of the iron industry and tempered steel? Many have done so, seeing the famous steels of Damascus and later Toledo — so celebrated in the European Middle Ages — as products of ancient manufacturing methods reaching back to the smiths of Babylon. Mesopotamian sites have yielded iron tools and fittings in abundance. In his work on the “first civilizations,” Gustave Le Bon, quoting the Greek historian Diodorus Siculus, tells us that the stones supporting one of Babylon’s bridges across the Euphrates were joined with iron clamps and that the joints were sealed with molten lead.

And then there is jewellery. Assyrian goldsmithing was matched only by that of Egypt. Its tradition passed on to Damascus and then to the Visigothic craftsmen of Spain — not through what we mistakenly call the “Arab invasion,” but much earlier, through Palestinian travellers and merchants before the eighth century CE.

One of the most remarkable features of Egyptian and Assyrian metalwork was the use of pure, unalloyed gold and silver, somehow given great strength by methods that remain unknown to us. As late as the fourteenth century, European bankers, especially those of Genoa, were still sending raw gold to Damascus to be worked. The treasures of Tutankhamun, Queen Ahhotep, and Prince Khaemwaset inspire something close to reverence for the goldsmiths who made them.

At no point in their history did the Greeks or Romans reach this level of technique. The golden age of Hellenic ironwork, metallurgy, and jewellery belonged to the Aegean period — Mycenae, Knossos, and Cyprus — when these lands were still Egypto-Palestinian colonies. There was, to be sure, one great Greek art: bronze casting. It made possible the large equestrian figures whose proportions would later be perfected in Rome and Byzantium. Yet the most celebrated masters were gathered not in mainland Greece but on Asian Samos.

Italy would have to wait until the Renaissance before it again produced bronze casters of comparable skill, and it is hard to see where they could have found their models, advice, and techniques except in Asian documents translated into Greek or Latin.

Glassmaking, plain or cloisonné enamel, artificial stones, metallized or spun glass paste, the making of small flasks, bottles, and imitation pearls — all the arts of fire are as old as civilization itself in the East. Even in the twentieth century, Egyptian, Palestinian, Syrian, and Iraqi craftsmen were still offering tourists objects whose shapes, materials, and decoration recalled pieces archaeologists were uncovering in the tombs of Gezer, Ur, Samarra, Saqqara, Leptis Magna, and Volubilis. The continuity is astonishing. In Pompeii and in Greek cities such as Syracuse and Corinth, many glass objects and decorative elements show the workmanship, and often the maker’s mark, of Asian workshops.

The ceramics and glazed or enamelled bricks that came from Egyptian and Babylonian kilns were so beautiful that they approached the art of jewellery. The clays employed, and the sophisticated chemistry of artificial oxide mixtures made from copper, iron, tin, and cobalt, were too difficult for Greek craftsmen to reproduce. Only much later, in imperial Rome, did the art begin to develop there, alongside mosaic work, which ancient Egypt had already brought to a high level of refinement. Diodorus Siculus describes his amazement at the walls of Ecbatana, entirely covered in ceramics of different colours arranged in geometric patterns. Century after century, the Arab East preserved its love of gleaming glazed tile and ceramics, an art in which the Abbasid dynasty would later take particular pride.

The unquestioned mastery that modern Arabs display in embroidery, weaving, and tapestry likewise rests on a tradition thousands of years old. Here too, they have remained unsurpassed except perhaps by the Chinese. The Greeks and Romans themselves acknowledged their superiority. Classical literature repeatedly praises the fabrics of Palestine, the purple of Tyre, the embroidered linen of Egypt, and the fine cloth of Assyria, of which Mosul long held a world monopoly.

The fine linens, carpets, and brocades used by wealthy Greek and Roman townspeople were imported from the East, especially from Palestine. Workshops in Palestine and Syria specializing in brocade created and preserved the stoles, albs, chasubles, mitres, canopies, fans, and other luxuries worn by the Byzantine and Roman clergy. Under the Abbasid caliphs, state textile workshops grew on a large scale, and their fabrics later impressed the Crusaders.

Add to this the arts of perfume, furniture-making, harness-making, agriculture — especially orchards — and irrigation, and we begin to see, through the darkness of history and the clichés of a distorted academic tradition, how much light filled the Arab world before Athens had even been born. It is hard not to smile at Western Orientalists praising the Arabs for having “passed on” Greek science and technology to us after supposedly “translating” Greek religious and philosophical texts. It will take more than one generation to undo this learned misunderstanding.

Politics too is a science. Through their law codes — those of Hammurabi and Bocchoris — the Egyptians and Assyrians were the teachers of the Greco-Roman city and therefore of our own. Roman jurists did little more than translate and adapt the countless legal documents, contracts, and texts handed down to them from Egypt and Chaldea.

The Arab is a born jurist, with a highly developed instinct for argument and institutional order. In constitutional and criminal law, private and international law alike, the influence of the Egyptian code was dominant throughout the Mediterranean. That code reflected a civilization already at its height and, behind it, a prehistoric experience of remarkable continuity.

The very idea of the enduring, structured state — omnipresent, grounded in a sovereignty presented as divine, maintained by dynastic or hereditary law, and standing as an absolute entity above individual people — was born on the banks of the Nile. The Roman Empire owed Egypt, and Julius Caesar’s visit there, nothing less than its foundations and stability. The monarchy of so-called “divine right” inherited the strict paternalism of this model. Alexander likewise drew on Persian and Egyptian examples when he transformed Greek democracy into an imperial power of pharaonic type. Alexander was nothing other than a Greek pharaoh, as deeply imbued with Egyptian ideas of state and law as his teacher Aristotle.

One of the foundations of civil and criminal law is the principle that justice in Egypt belonged to the state. The state alone had the right to condemn, acquit, commute a sentence, or grant a pardon. At a time when many countries still lived under “an eye for an eye,” trial by ordeal, family vengeance, and summary punishment combined with a private right of revenge, the public settlement of disputes represented a major advance.

Egyptian law was formally written down, and every city possessed a copy. Judges were not free to rule according to personal sympathy, because the pharaoh was the guarantor of justice and complainants could appeal to him. Diodorus Siculus, who gives us a detailed account of civil and criminal law in ancient Egypt, describes elected juries, lawyers, and courts of appeal. Testimony, accusations, and verdicts were all formally recorded in writing.

Ancient Egypt produced enormous amounts of paperwork — enough to rival a modern administration. Paperwork is not a twentieth-century invention. Private contracts and agreements with the state were minutely regulated, and notarized documents sealed financial transactions, property deals, mortgages, and marriages.

Just as Justinian in the sixth century CE gathered together the customs and legal texts of the empire, the pharaoh Bocchoris of the Twenty-Fourth Dynasty revised the whole body of civil law. From that period onward, Greece had access to a coherent legal system from which it drew its civic and urban institutions. It is remarkable that school textbooks, still determined to present the Greek city as a creation out of nothing, springing miraculously from “Hellenic genius,” say nothing about these obvious legal origins.

Even more surprising is the way most historians speak of Greek domination over the East. The economic power of the Greek cities compared with that of the Egyptian and Babylonian empires was roughly that of the principality of Andorra compared with the United States.

Greece, as everyone knows, had poor soil. It could not feed even its relatively small population and depended for food on shipments of grain from Libya, Sicily, Egypt, and Scythia. It lacked timber for ships, flax for clothing, leather, and pasture for horses and cattle. It had no tin mines, no gold mines, no iron, and no coal. Copper came from Cyprus, an island that formed part of the Babylonian Empire.

Its main mining and industrial resources lay in Thrace, where a few veins of silver were worked, and above all in Attica. At the beginning of the fifth century BCE, an outcrop of silver-bearing lead had been found in the Laurion hills at the far southeastern tip of the country. In a typical year it brought the Athenian state about one hundred talents, or roughly three and a half tonnes of silver. This was the one true industrial centre of ancient Greece. Several thousand enslaved people worked there, and mining there continues to this day.

What weight could poor Greece carry beside the enormous agricultural economies of Egypt and Mesopotamia, or beside the industrial, maritime, and financial systems of the East, supported by millions of workers, engineers, and scholars and administered by states that had been centralized for centuries? What importance could Attic society have in international affairs when gold was so scarce there that in the sixth century BCE its purchasing power was so high that an ox cost the equivalent of ten new francs?

Wealth had little basis beyond a patch of poor land, and the tax system’s social classes are revealing. The first and wealthiest class consisted of landowners harvesting five hundred measures of grain, about 6,000 litres. The second comprised farmers wealthy enough to maintain a horse. The third consisted of those who owned a pair of oxen. The last was made up of people who hired out their labour.

All of this was insignificant beside the incalculable resources of the East. Gold, silver, and precious stones had been accumulated there for thousands of years as ingots, statues of gods, state treasures, and temple wealth, creating financial reserves beyond imagination. After the Macedonians plundered the palace at Ecbatana, enough precious metal still remained for the generals Antigonus and Seleucus to extract 4,000 talents,[^pierre-rossi-lci-003] or 140 tonnes. The mind reels.

What fabulous wealth, then, must have been stored in the temples of Luxor, Karnak, Memphis, Abydos, Edfu, Philae, Abu Simbel, Amarna, Alexandria, and countless others — or in the religious cities of Selinus, Ephesus, Miletus, Sardis, Tyre, Babylon, Nineveh, Susa, and Troy? The list is tedious, but the picture it creates is almost fantastical.

By comparison, Greece was like the poor cobbler of the fable going hat in hand to the financier. It adopted coinage late, and even then the money was Eastern. Coinage first appeared in Mesopotamia and Lydia in the form of electrum, an alloy of gold and silver. Croesus then issued the first gold coins, and Darius greatly expanded their use. For centuries, Greece, Sicily, Egypt, and Italy were supplied with staters and gold darics made in the East under the guarantee of the Great King of Persia.

In other words, only Asian currency was backed by substantial real wealth. The western cities lacked not only the necessary economic reserves but also the stability, permanence, and political and military power needed to inspire confidence. The great Eastern capitals, by contrast, had enjoyed hard, tangible credit since the dawn of civilization.

That a penniless and thinly populated Hellas should still be presented, simply by virtue of its supposed democracy, as the master of Mediterranean civilization and destiny makes us question not so much our Cartesian rationality as the paralysis of our common sense.

We have seen how Britain became a world power in the eighteenth century, able to intervene on every continent, once it had seized the treasures accumulated in India over countless generations. It then possessed a strong currency and could draw on the labour of the whole of Indian history. In the same way, Athens and later the Roman Empire, both modest in their beginnings, grew suddenly to the scale we know once they gained access to the resources of the Arab East.

By sea and in endless caravans, gold, precious stones, and ivory moved from depots in Ethiopia, Sudan, the Ivory Coast, Guinea, Senegal, and Niger northward toward Thebes, Tyre, and Byzantium. Asian gold travelled to Babylon, where bankers traded it and converted it into promissory notes, coin, ingots, and jewellery used as security for transactions. Only crumbs reached the Greek trading cities.

We can even say that Greece was quickly bought by the East, becoming little more than a province increasingly subject to Afro-Asian capital. Through the ruthless pressure of competition, that capital pushed the Greek cities into devouring one another. Banks play a decisive part in the social and political life of peoples today, and their role was no less important in the ancient world.

For this subject, the reader may consult three older but still interesting works: J. Duvernois, *Banques et banquiers dans l’Egypte ancienne* (1928); Laffon-Moutels, *Les Etapes du capitalisme, de Hammourabi à Rockfeller* (1938); and A. Colling, *Banques et banquiers, de Babylone à Wall-Street* (Plon, 1940).

Under such conditions, a war between the tiny Greek world and the immense Persian or Egyptian empire would seem almost inconceivable. Yet the classical history taught in school tells us, against all expectation, that Greece not only went to war with Asia but defeated it. A real fairy tale. Herodotus, Demosthenes, Thucydides, Arrian, and Plutarch see things very differently. In the end, it is to them — and to the few serious documents that survive — that we must turn if we want to understand the so-called Persian Wars and the fable of Alexander’s conquests.

# VI

## THE GREAT ARAMEAN KING

When Cyrus established himself in Babylon in 533 BCE, and his son Cambyses secured a kind of protectorate over Egypt from 525 onward — these dates are approximate, it should be said — the Achaemenid dynasty presented itself in these lands as heir to a culture shared across the whole East, and as the protector, not the enemy, of Aramean civilization.

Culture had long been moving from Asia into Greece by way of Palestine and the Aegean. The Achaemenid Great King was therefore also, in practice, king and protector of the Greeks on the European mainland. Ignore that fact, and we distort both the face of the East and the history of the West.

From 522 onward, then, the Aramean Darius ruled Asia and Egypt. Each country kept its own laws and princes, so the Great King was something like the president of a United States. Mainland Greece obeyed him readily and fell, in practical terms, within the reach of both his treasury and his diplomacy.

His army included many Greek contingents and commanders. The wealthy cities of Ionia belonged to the satrapies of Sardis and Daskylion and shared fully in the prosperity of the imperial system. When Darius launched an expedition into Scythian territory along the Danube in 512, Greeks and Arabs served side by side in his forces. Amyntas, king of Macedonia, swore allegiance to him, as did the cities of Thrace.

To reward Histiaeus, tyrant of Miletus, for his conduct in the campaign, Darius placed him in charge of the Thracian mines. Why, then, would Darius have attacked Greece? He had no serious rival in the sixth-century Mediterranean and ruled it as an unrivalled and uncontested master. And why would the impoverished Greeks, already paying tribute to the Great King, have chosen a hopeless war against Persian power?

Classical historians avoid the question. They prefer to present the conflict that followed — the so-called Persian Wars — as an ideological struggle between the democratic parties of the ancient world and tyrannical regimes. The weakness of that explanation is obvious.

Even a quick look at the events shows instead that the “Persian Wars” were local wars between Greek-Asian cities, sometimes encouraged by the Great King and eventually settled by him on terms that suited his interests.

The central figure was Aristagoras, prince of Miletus, who around 500 reached an agreement with his governor, the satrap of Lydia, to seize Aegean trading posts then held by tiny Greek-Palestinian republics. The venture failed. Greek troops from Miletus, reinforced by Lydian auxiliaries, were defeated by the Greek-Palestinian garrisons of Naxos and other islands.

Aristagoras then turned to Athens, which also wanted a protectorate over the Cyclades and Sporades, and proposed an alliance. Athens accepted. A small allied force assembled at Ephesus, but instead of sending it against the Cyclades, Aristagoras used it to attack the provincial capital of Sardis, which he occupied and made his base. Feeling that they had been diverted from their original purpose, the Athenians immediately went home.

The Great King moved quickly to restore order in Miletus. The Palestinians, alarmed at the danger to their trading posts, sent a flotilla that blockaded the rebel city, from which Aristagoras escaped to his estate in Thrace. Miletus, a Greek-Lydian city, was stormed with the help of Greeks from Samos, Lesbos, and Lade, and was partly destroyed.

None of this was unusual in an age when the central government directed grand strategy but left the provinces wide freedom to manage their own interests, treasuries, local forces, and even limited military operations. The sheer size of the empire, the slowness of couriers, and the limits of communication made decentralization unavoidable; in practice, it could amount almost to autonomy.

If Miletus went to war with Naxos, that did not mean that its sovereign, the Great King, had done so. In Darius’s larger policy, events in Greece could only be secondary. He had far greater concerns along the Indus, in Upper Egypt, and in Thrace and Scythia, where large and little-known peoples occupied broad, rich plains full of crops, horses, and strategically important minerals.

Those were the Great King’s real concerns. In Greece he was at home. He knew by heart the endless crises that shook these small cities, which he helped sustain and whose money systems he helped keep running. His court regularly received and supported leading political figures from Athens, Sparta, Corinth, Thebes, and elsewhere.

Two of them — the former Spartan king Demaratus and the former Athenian tyrant Hippias — asked for his help in regaining power. He agreed, and allowed a new expedition sent to Thrace in the summer of 492 to detach a small intervention force for that purpose. Difficulties prevented it from acting that year.

Only in the spring of 490 did a Canaanite flotilla carrying Greek-Lydian troops under the Athenian Hippias anchor off the beach at Marathon, waiting for Hippias’s supporters to carry out their coup. No coup came. The commander of the small landing force therefore ordered a withdrawal.

As the troops were re-embarking, Athenian hoplites under Miltiades charged and caused some disorder among the men and horses. That was the Battle of Marathon: a failure for Hippias, whose friends were ostracized; a shrug of the shoulders for the Great King.

Thrace remained the real concern. Xerxes had succeeded Darius in 486 and prepared a major expedition toward Macedonia, his ally, which had asked for military help against a coalition of Thracians and Scythians. The Greek-Aramean army that crossed the Hellespont in the spring of 480 over boat bridges built by Greek engineers was therefore very large, and a substantial fleet accompanied it.

The priests and the Pythia at Delphi had blessed the expedition. Most of the Greek cities on the mainland took part, with Ionian and Aeolian regiments in prominent positions. It could hardly have been a war against Greece.

While the main army moved west toward the Danube, Xerxes, at the request of Athenian and Spartan exiles, ordered several units to turn south and assist the Athenian political factions that had appealed to him. What followed is well known. Thessaly and Boeotia welcomed these troops. A small Spartan force was scattered at Thermopylae, and Athens was occupied without resistance.

The discouraged inhabitants of Attica even considered emigrating to southern Italy. The naval clash at Salamis between Palestinian and Athenian ships did little to alter the course of events. An occupation force remained in Thessaly and Boeotia under Mardonius, son-in-law of the late Darius, until the main Thracian expedition withdrew.

For the Aramean empire, the decisive game was being played in the north. Macedonia already held the key to the fate of Hellas. Closely tied to Babylon, steeped in Asian culture, and dotted with garrisons of the Great King, it stood firmly within the calculations of his bankers and economists.

Salamis, Plataea, and Athens were sideshows beside the great policy directed from Babylon toward the Danube and Bosporus, where the small city of Byzantium was already rising at the geopolitical crossroads of a new world.

There were never any true wars between the Great King and the Greek cities. The imbalance of strength was too great, and the Greeks were already dependent, almost colonized, and integrated into Aramean systems. With the interventions of Darius and Xerxes, even the fiction of Greek independence begins to collapse, to say nothing of a Greek unity that never existed at any point in history.

Pausanias of Sparta, who fought the Persians at Plataea, would later seek Babylon’s favour and offer his cooperation. The famous Themistocles would inform Xerxes of the movements of the Athenian fleet at Salamis, then appeal to Xerxes’s son Artaxerxes by reminding him of that debt. The king honoured it and appointed the Athenian governor of Magnesia.

“He had acquired at court,” Thucydides notes, “an authority such as no Greek before him had possessed.” And Herodotus, born at Halicarnassus in Aramean territory under Persian rule — what was he but an author in the service of the Great King, already called the *Basileus* with a capital B?

In truth, Greece never had any military strategy beyond the wars its cities fought against one another. Greek armies, absorbed in what were little more than district quarrels, did not alter the order of the Mediterranean world by an inch, much less that of Africa and Asia.

The great Greek conflict of the fifth century was not the Persian Wars but the Peloponnesian War — or rather the three Peloponnesian wars, which began as early as 459, only twenty years after Plataea, and ended in 404 after a series of uneasy truces.

Every side appealed to the *Basileus*. He subsidized one camp and then another, watched the blows from his palaces at Susa or Babylon, and placed agents in Sparta, Athens, Corinth, Thebes, Amphipolis, and Byzantium. Before long he had become the master of the peninsula’s internal politics, just as he already was in Macedonia.

Hardly any Greek citizen of consequence was not, in one way or another, a subject, client, or debtor of the *Basileus*. When Alexander later set out on his Asian expedition, he behaved as a subject of the King, much as Aristagoras of Miletus had done about a century and a half earlier. His action looked more like a coup within the empire than a foreign war. Alexander was as much at home in Asia as Artaxerxes was in Greece.

What, then, are we to make of the famous Athenian maritime empire created in the eastern Mediterranean after the “Persian Wars,” the alliance known as the Delian League?

First, the Palestinian navy was hardly troubled by it. Together with the Egyptian, Libyan, and Carthaginian fleets, it remained the dominant force in Mediterranean waters for more than another thousand years, before passing its skills, organization, and ports to its natural successors, the Byzantines and the caliphs.

Second, wherever the Persian government opposed Athenian expansion — in Thrace, Messenia, Egypt, and Cyprus — the Athenian fleet failed to capture the local markets.

Finally, Athens gained room for economic expansion in the Cyclades and Sporades and along the Asian, Byzantine, and Scythian shores of the Black Sea only through the favour of the Great King, whose official protection it received with the Callias agreement of 448. Palestinian, Babylonian, and Egyptian bankers then opened branches in Attica.

The man who turned Athens from a rural city into a commercial society was Pericles of the Alcmaeonid family, related to the noblest families of Greece, connected through his relatives to Lydian bankers, and a compliant instrument of the Achaemenid dynasty.

He made a country poorly suited to industry or large-scale agriculture into a meeting place for merchants and a centre of transactions. The countryside declined. Work was concentrated in commercial offices, mining, and shipyards.

Athens met the fate of any country colonized by a dominant economy, of which modern history offers many examples. It became the Algeria of the Great King. An artificial prosperity based on the sale and distribution of African and Asian goods gave Attica a brilliant surface appearance — the shine of the *Basileus*’s livery.

Life was expensive and hard, rewarding mainly the sons of wealthy families and a moneyed aristocracy that dressed in Eastern fashions. Athens became a small Arab trading post lost in the radiance of Sardis, Thebes, and Babylon. Its political constitution bore the mark of an economy dependent on the outside world.

Few societies offer a clearer example of such complete rule by wealth. And perhaps that is why Athens declined so quickly: its people had lost both responsibility for and interest in political and cultural life before they had time to benefit fully from the great Eastern civilization handed down through their ancestral religion and philosophy.

Such a fate was bound to provoke envy. Sparta wanted a share of it and therefore sought the same financial favour from the Aramean *Basileus*. The Peloponnesian War became a race for Arab money.

But the officials of the Great King supplied that money to the Greeks for calculated purposes. They were willing to pay for services, not waste funds on useless investment, because Greece offered little to invest in. Its value lay mainly in its people and in the fortunate position of its ports.

To earn Arab money, Sparta, isolated in its harsh mountains, had to gain control of port systems and banking centres on the Greek peninsula. Leaving Athens with a monopoly would have been fatal. From this need came the terrible Peloponnesian War, which was controlled and arbitrated from beginning to end by the very power whose favour was at stake: the Great King.

Open an ancient-history textbook and see how the war is taught to schoolchildren: “The opposition between Athens and Sparta was obvious: democracy against aristocracy, a military city against a city nourished on literature and the arts.” One can only hope that the scholar responsible for this little gem was driven more by politics than by sincere belief.

We have, after all, something unusually valuable for the ancient world: Thucydides’ account. And it is not ambiguous. In Book II, chapter II, he names the envoys Sparta sent through Thrace to the Great King “to obtain money and military aid.” The Athenians bribed Sadocus, son of the king, had the envoys handed over at the Hellespont, and executed them without trial.

Yet when they captured Artaphernes, an envoy of the Great King to Sparta, at the mouth of the Strymon, they sent him back to Ephesus in a “special trireme” with an honour guard, after confiscating letters in which the Persian king complained that he could make no sense of the Spartans’ contradictory messages: “he had received numbers of envoys, each saying something different.”

We then see the alliance between Sparta and the satrap Tissaphernes, governor of the coastal provinces of Asia Minor, grow steadily closer. Three treaties were signed with him despite the efforts and pressure of Alcibiades. The Persians allowed Spartan admirals to use the ports of Miletus, Chios, and Leros, and agreed to pay their crews.

Then they changed course and listened to the cynical advice of Alcibiades, who, after betraying his own country for Sparta, was now intriguing against Sparta as well. He told Tissaphernes that the cheapest policy was to let the Greeks wear one another down, while Persia contributed only modestly to the cost and ran no risk itself.

He also argued “that the King would do best to reach an understanding with the Athenians when dividing the Empire, since they had no wish to make conquests on the mainland and their aims and military resources fitted those of Persia in every respect. An agreement was therefore possible: Athens would restore its command of the sea and allow the King to impose his authority on the Greeks living within his territory.” (Thucydides, Book VIII, chap. II.)

Tissaphernes followed the advice and “doled out the subsidies to the Peloponnesians sparingly, preventing them from fighting at sea by claiming that the Phoenician ships were on their way and that they could then fight with greatly superior forces. In this way, because of him, the Peloponnesians’ position deteriorated.”[^pierre-rossi-lci-004]

In short, Athens and Sparta competed for the Great King’s handouts. They begged, sold out their allies, and were prepared to alter even the constitutions of their states if that pleased the Persian. Tissaphernes, with great skill, played one side against the other.

Even while working with the Athenians, he signed another agreement with Sparta. He promised a supporting Phoenician fleet of 147 ships. It did put to sea, but never reached its destination.

Eventually Darius II, exasperated, removed him and appointed his younger son Cyrus in his place as satrap of Lydia, Phrygia, and Cappadocia. Large sums were then paid to the Spartans, allowing them to pay their sailors and lure away Athenian soldiers.

Pressed by Darius II to bring the war to a quick end, and now backed by Phoenician ships and Cyrus’s staff — Cyrus personally took charge of naval operations — the Spartan commanders and their admiral Lysander assembled a fleet at Ephesus that swept the Athenians from the sea.

King Pausanias finally came to besiege Athens while Lysander blockaded the Piraeus in 405. The agony lasted six months. Sparta’s allies demanded that Athens be destroyed and its population sold at auction. The Great King insisted on moderation.

Spartan negotiators settled for demolishing the fortifications of the Piraeus and the Long Walls. Athens survived, but had to abandon all its overseas settlements and trading posts.

Sparta had triumphed, only to be absorbed in turn into Babylon’s financial and monetary power. It entered the service of the Great King, and its regiments went off to die at Cunaxa, in Phrygia, and in Paphlagonia. Sardis, capital of Lydia, became the real capital of Greece, and the satrap who governed there ordered truces and conflicts according to the King’s interests.

To the Great King, all of this had been a storm in a teacup. His attention remained fixed on Macedonia, Thrace, and Scythia. These regions were the natural continuation of Asia Minor, separated from it only by the narrow waterways of the Dardanelles and Bosporus.

They were also rich in gold, and the Great King held the monopoly on gold coinage. For a long time the rulers of Asia Minor had pursued a Danube policy whose traces survive in Orphic legend.

Under the Achaemenids, supported by unmatched economic power, constantly expanding maritime and commercial networks, an effective army, and capable agents, that Danube policy grew so strong that it would continue into the Ottoman Empire until the middle of the nineteenth century. This gives some idea of the depth of the ties between the Persian Empire and northern Greece and the Balkans.

These lands also contained wheat fields and broad fertile plains that could supply Asia Minor far better than the thin soils of the Greek peninsula. For the Great King, the Chersonese and Byzantium were certainly far more important strategically than Sparta or Athens.

Thrace and Macedonia controlled the great routes of Danube trade. That, together with their mineral wealth — the Strymon valley and the small Pangaean range, an offshoot of the Rhodopes, formed the El Dorado of the Balkans, while Mount Dysoron contained veins of silver — made Macedonia a favourite of the Great King from the beginning.

Why do textbook writers ignore geopolitical arguments like these, which would explain the success of Philip and Alexander of Macedon far better than the “drunkenness and brutality” attributed to Philip on the strength of Demosthenes?

Historians have fallen silent over the Macedonia of Perdiccas, Amyntas, and the shrewd queen-regent Eurydice, and over the dynasty’s origins in the Asian family of the Heraclids of Argos. One might think Macedonia sprang into existence with Philip, a barbarian who appeared from nowhere, when he was heir to a prehistoric tradition connected with the highest religious culture of the Aramean East.

How long will the “democratic” shadow of Pericles keep us from seeing the course of Greco-Persian history? If Philip of Macedon was able to extend his power over almost all of Greece, it was because the Great King not only approved but supplied the gold such an enterprise required.

The ports of the Cyclades, Euboea, Attica, and the Peloponnese mattered to his maritime and commercial policy just as those of Libya and the western Mediterranean did. Macedonia and Thrace, by contrast, belonged to his continental strategy.

Their populations were denser and larger. They were important markets and centres of buying and selling, unlike peninsular Greece, which was little more than the service sector of the Egyptian-Babylonian economy.

The Great King’s continental diplomacy was shaped by five rivers: the Indus, Tigris, Euphrates, Nile, and Danube, among the great waterways of the world. Once the Mediterranean port system had been secured after the Peloponnesian War, and control over the four African and Asian rivers was long established, the Aramean Great King had only to extend his influence over Europe’s great river, the Danube, whose history shows that it has carried a political destiny no less important than the Nile’s.

This does not require us to deny the personal ability of Philip or his son Alexander. But their task was mainly to follow geography and circumstance. Individuals count for little in history. Events and social forces built up over centuries take command and, when the moment comes, create the direction that history requires.

We should not therefore look to the personalities of Philip and Alexander for the causes of the political transformation that affected the eastern Mediterranean in the fourth century BCE. The countless attempts by professional historians to explain the period through the psychology of the Macedonian kings — their doubts, feelings, ambitions, even their love lives — belong more to fiction than to serious historical analysis.

Jérôme Carcopino’s learned and painstaking studies of his favourite “great men” of antiquity provide a model of the genre. They may be enjoyable to read, but the figures they present live largely in the realm of imagination. So it is with Philip and Alexander.

The events were greater than the men who thought they controlled them. Those events were continually guided by the Persian authorities of Lydia and Babylon. They saw Philip as an ideal ally for their Danube policy and therefore supported his plan to unify Greece, a development that served the interests of the Great King, then Artaxerxes III.

His reign, from 358 to 338, closely overlapped Philip’s, from 357 to 336. While Artaxerxes restored order across his vast empire and re-established his authority in Egypt, Philip established his own authority over the Greek peninsula in four stages.

A formal treaty was concluded around 350 between the two rulers — or rather between Artaxerxes as overlord and Philip as vassal, Macedonia having sworn allegiance to Babylon around 510, almost two centuries earlier.

Macedonia was a large country, ten times the size of Attica, with at least 500,000 inhabitants and an army trained in Persian and Thracian methods, including strong cavalry regiments. Its ruling class possessed a rich Greek-Aramean culture, substantial income from land and money, and prestige reaching well beyond Macedonia into the Greek cities, where it had committed supporters such as the celebrated and incorruptible Phocion of Athens.

The attraction of the gold staters of Mount Pangaeus won still more hearts. Demosthenes, writing with the violence of a polemicist and, we should remember, for purposes of military propaganda, poured abuse on Philip and his country. Fed on those speeches, we have listened far too readily.

Macedonia barbarian? What of the cultural tradition of neighbouring Thrace? Had close ties of friendship, family, and solidarity not linked Pella with the other Greek capitals since the sixth century? Scholars and poets, Arab or Athenian, including Euripides, had lived at this northern court.

King Archelaus was held up as a model: an urban planner, humanist, and mathematician who, by the end of the Peloponnesian War, had given his country the finest road and strategic network in Danubian Europe.

This was the country that Philip led easily to victory over the small Greek states trapped in their narrow concerns. There is no need to enter the maze of local disputes and political manoeuvring.

From the moment he took the throne in 357, Philip established himself on the Aegean, then took Thessaly and Byzantium, cut and controlled the grain route, moved into Boeotia, and spoke as a master. Just as during the Peloponnesian War, a diplomatic rush toward the Great King followed.

Athens sent an embassy asking for his favourable arbitration. The King ignored it and continued to support his Macedonian vassal. At Chaeronea in 338, Philip crushed his opponents, who were forced to accept his protectorate.

Soon afterward he assembled delegates from all Greece at Corinth, proclaimed a general peace and regional autonomy, and announced a League under his presidency and military command, with each city required to supply troops according to its strength. The division of Greece into tiny statelets had finally ended.

In twenty years, with the constant support of the Great King, who saw this as a way to strengthen his European policy, Philip created the centralized and coherent power that Persia had wanted around the Straits since the time of Darius the Great. From this point onward, control of the Straits would become a major issue in diplomatic history. Even today, it remains urgently relevant.

The stage was ready for Alexander, whose role, properly understood, was to integrate Greece institutionally into the Aramean world after centuries of cultural integration. Alexander symbolized the triumphant success of the Achaemenid project. It mattered little that he was not himself an Achaemenid: he took over and assumed the dynasty’s inheritance.

Here again, the almost fairy-tale way Alexander’s “conquest” is usually told is astonishing. We are asked to believe that with a mere handful of men — 35,000, we are told — Alexander “crushed the hordes of Darius III”; that Arbela was “truly the battle of Europe against Asia”; that “within a few years the Persian Empire was defeated, overthrown, occupied.”

Occupied by 35,000 men! Naivety? Bad faith? Hasty compilation? Simple laziness in checking an inherited story? How can anyone not be embarrassed by such enormous absurdities being printed and taught as unquestionable fact?

The legend of Alexander has bewitched us to the point of making us lose our sense. It closely resembles the legend of the Persian Wars and hides the same reality: the ambitions of the satraps of Lydia, openly or quietly rebelling against the central government and calling on outside allies — powerful Greek or Palestinian lords — to help them against it.

No succession or transfer of power in the Persian Empire ever passed peacefully, and no major governorship was free of ambitions to seize the throne. Palace plots and coups were routine.

At the end of the sixth century we saw the Greek tyrant of Miletus join with the satrap of Lydia to carve out an Aegean kingdom, calling on the Athenian fleet for support. After the death of Darius II, his younger son Cyrus rebelled against his elder brother, who had taken the throne as Artaxerxes II, and hired Greek mercenaries — the famous Ten Thousand who, after defeat at Cunaxa in 401, made their way back to the Black Sea coast under Xenophon.

We can also recall the satrap Mausolus trying to create a kingdom for himself in Caria with Spartan support, and the satrap Artabazus doing the same in Phrygia with Athenian mercenaries. Greek, Illyrian, Sicilian, Thracian, and Scythian troops served so often under Asian princes and governors that the examples are beyond counting.

Immediately after Chaeronea, Philip sent a force of ten thousand men under Parmenion into the Troad in 337. Artaxerxes III had just been assassinated by his minister Bagoas, who then placed an officer of the imperial guard on the throne in 336 under the name Darius III Codomannus.

We do not know what part the Macedonian force played during the interregnum or which side Philip supported. But Darius III’s accession to the throne strangely coincided with Philip’s assassination in 336 during celebrations for his daughter’s marriage.

There was another striking coincidence: Philip was killed just as Alexander and his mother Olympias returned to Pella, apparently coming back — or fleeing — from Asia Minor.

Without pretending to know details that cannot now be recovered, it seems clear that the Macedonian royal family was closely and militarily involved in the difficult succession to Artaxerxes III, who had treated Philip favourably and concluded with him the famous alliance of 350 that allowed him to dominate all Greece.

In Alexander’s eyes, then, the new Great King, Darius Codomannus, was simply a usurper who owed his throne to a double murder: that of Artaxerxes and that of his son and legitimate heir, the young Arses. Was he also responsible for the murder of Philip, Alexander’s brother? It is reasonable to think so.

In any case, relations with the usurper did not improve. The new king of Macedonia kept in the Troad the force his father had sent there. This clearly showed that Macedonia, until then a loyal vassal of Babylon, now refused to renew its allegiance and had gone into open rebellion.

Nor was it alone. The Persian-Babylonian legitimist party remained loyal to the memory of the dynasty of Darius the Great and regarded the newcomer as an adventurer. Much of the army agreed, as did most of the satraps of Asia Minor, Egypt, and Syria. Only Palestine and the Greek cities of Ionia had rallied to the usurper.

Alexander was therefore allied with almost the whole civil and military leadership of the Babylonian Empire, while the Greeks stood against him: not only the Ionians, but also Sparta, Athens, Thebes, and the other major cities, which received substantial subsidies from the usurper.

Demosthenes, for example, was accused of receiving 300 talents — a talent being worth about 5,000 gold francs — to “use in whatever way best served the interests of the Great King.” Thebes was the first to rise while Alexander was away campaigning on the Danube. In retaliation the city was razed and its population sold at auction.

Relations between the Persian legitimist faction backed by Macedonia and the government of Darius III backed by the Greeks had therefore reached extreme violence. To claim, as is usually done, that Alexander’s expedition was a war of Europe against Asia is nothing short of a farce.

Alexander in fact sided with the legitimist court of Babylon against the Greeks and acted as a loyal Persian subject. Far from benefiting what we call Greece, his victory buried it still deeper within Aramean power.

The contrast is telling: Alexander is still treated as a hero in the Arab world, while the specifically Greek tradition regards him with suspicion. In his successful struggle against Darius, he represented not Hellenism but Babylonian legitimacy. He was, in effect, a commander in a civil war, and the strongest part of his forces consisted less of Macedonian recruits than of Persian supporters.

In response to the provocations of Darius III, Alexander landed in the Troad in the spring of 334 and joined the small army his father had already sent there. He immediately found himself facing not Persian troops but Greek squadrons from Ionia commanded by Memnon of Rhodes, an outstanding strategist who was both admiral and general.

The encounter on the Granicus was little more than a token battle, since Memnon had advised giving ground before the Macedonian forces, now reinforced by Persian regiments in rebellion. The Greek cities of Miletus and Halicarnassus, by contrast, resisted fiercely. Taken and retaken, they eventually remained in the hands of Darius III’s army.

For nearly a year and a half, negotiations and secret conferences multiplied, until November 333, when Darius and Alexander met at Issus near the Gulf of Antioch. Defeated, Darius offered talks, but the Persian aristocracy rejected them.

The usurper was by then so isolated that both war and negotiation had become almost unnecessary: one only had to let his imposture collapse on its own. As soon as Alexander presented himself as defender of the true tradition and heir to Darius the Great, official bodies, priests, notables, and ordinary people welcomed him.

Palestine was the major exception. Tyre and Gaza had to be reduced after long sieges. Everywhere else there were processions, flowers, and receptions. In Egypt, popular joy greeted the new pharaoh, and the oracle of Amun addressed him as a son of God.

The rest is well known. Turning back toward the Tigris, Alexander defeated Darius’s army at Gaugamela, near the modern Iraqi city of Erbil, on 1 October 331. Darius fled and was later murdered by men close to him. Babylon and Susa surrendered at once. The magnificent royal residence at Persepolis was burned. Ecbatana fell after Pasargadae.

Alexander had now taken the place of the Great King. It had taken him only three years. To us this looks like a miracle. To the Aramean populations, it was nothing of the kind. They welcomed Alexander as a ruler of the same culture, religion, and stock as themselves, and as a natural leader recognized by their priests and notables.

The powerful imperial machinery built over centuries by Nile and Mesopotamian dynasties was barely disturbed by the fall of Darius Codomannus. After all, one man had disappeared, and that man represented no one but himself. The empire continued.

The scribe shrugged and returned to his stylus, the farmer to his hoe, the goldsmith to his thread of gold. Across the immense country, nothing essential changed and nothing stopped.

Alexander’s passage, as a hero blessed by the gods, was only a ripple on the surface of millennia of accumulated faith, knowledge, and scepticism. Like an ocean, the Arab world closed again over Alexander and over the Greek society that found its fulfilment within it.

This point matters: Alexander’s Asian campaign involved remarkably little actual warfare, and losses were small. Writers turned it into a “heroic epic,” and the princes who succeeded Alexander had every reason to help them do so. They benefited from giving his memory the same divine glow earlier rulers had given the memories of the Thutmoses, the Ramesses, and Cyrus the Great.

In reality, the campaign was barely military at all. It looked more like an election tour whose result had been settled in advance. In every respect Alexander behaved like an Aramean king, and his successors followed the same pattern.

The new dynasty continued toward the Greek peninsula, Italy, Carthaginian lands, and Iberia the protectorate policy long practised by the pharaohs and the Achaemenids. It continued to feed both the eastern Mediterranean and its hinterland with economic and cultural resources, while also supporting the Danube regions newly incorporated into the empire.

Alexander was so careful to respect Babylonian constitutional law and custom that he did not assume the royal title, insignia, and diadem until Darius was dead — that is, from 329 onward. Only then did he adopt the traditional court ceremony and the ritual *proskynesis*, the gesture of reverence in which subjects raised a kiss from their fingertips toward the king.

Coins struck in Asia and Macedonia then carried the inscription “Alexander King,” as if he wished to present himself openly as the natural heir of the Achaemenids. One of his first acts of submission to the established order was to place himself under the protection of Cyrus the Great and honour his tomb at Pasargadae.

Alexander did everything he could to make people forget that he came from elsewhere. This was all the easier because Greeks and the peoples of Africa and Asia were not sharply divided by ethnicity, culture, or religion. The nationalist antagonisms familiar to us today were unknown in that age. Alexander even sealed official documents with Darius’s own seal.

He made a point of visiting every province of the Aramean United States and, like his Achaemenid predecessors, of making the King’s physical presence felt everywhere. That is how we should understand his journeys through Bactria and Sogdiana in 329 and 328, Kabul in 327, and the Punjab in 326, all the way to the official frontiers recognized by Indo-Babylonian agreements.

Nowhere did Alexander cross the borders of the empire. The paradox is that, despite the flattering reputation history has given him, Alexander was not a conqueror at all. His career had nothing in common with that of Attila or Napoleon. He merely toured his own domain instead of taking what belonged to others.

When he returned to Susa in the spring of 324 after the exhausting march through Baluchistan, leaving his admiral Nearchus to return by way of the Persian Gulf, he did not consider the tour complete. Before his sudden death, he intended to finish it with a journey to Libya, Carthage, and Iberia.

This clearly shows that these lands too belonged to Babylon’s sphere of influence. Keep that list in mind. They would later form part of the historical Arab world because they had already belonged, under the Achaemenids, to the historical Aramean world.

Alexander’s strategic plans in Mesopotamia, Arabia, and the Persian Gulf have often been presented as dramatic innovations. He is credited with inventing the maritime route between the mouth of the Indus and the estuaries of the Tigris and Euphrates, and scholars have even imagined some obsession with an “outer sea.”

Yet the famous “Alexander plan” had been known and carried out since the time of Darius the Great, and probably long before him. Herodotus says so explicitly. Alexander used the same sailors, geographers, explorers, and merchants who had served the Achaemenids, for the same purposes and along the same routes.

By the end of the sixth century BCE, Darius had already annexed the upper Indus basin, and regular contact between Kabul and Babylon ran both by sea and overland.

Alexander was so little a stranger to imperial Asia that, once Persepolis had fallen, he sent the Greek contingents home and confirmed the Persian satraps and military commanders in their posts. He reorganized the army along Persian lines and with Persian troops.

Even the Macedonian regiments came under suspicion. After the revolt of the garrison at Opis — one rebellion among several — the King’s personal guard was recruited exclusively from Persians from 325 onward.

All of this is described in Arrian’s *Anabasis* and Plutarch’s *Lives*. Neither historian, writing respectively in the second and first centuries BCE, could of course have witnessed the exploits of the hero they described. Even so, they drew on reliable sources, including the memoirs written later by Alexander’s former general Ptolemy, by then king of Egypt.

We are therefore fairly well informed, and modern commentators have needed a great deal of prejudice to turn Alexander’s career into an attempt at Greek domination, an idea that was itself almost unthinkable in the world of the time.

The language of government remained Aramaic. It could be written directly in cuneiform, Phoenician, or Egyptian script, or represented phonetically with Greek letters. Greek and Aramaic had long been mixed, and their roots and structures now became even more closely intertwined — not because Alexander or his small band of Macedonian soldiers deliberately made it happen, but through the slow blending produced by constant contact.

A marriage of languages, and a marriage of peoples.

At Susa, ninety of Alexander’s companions married ninety noblewomen from the Persian aristocracy. The King himself married Princess Roxana according to Asian custom, with a ritual sharing of bread if not of wine.

The splendour of the weddings certainly impressed the population, but no one saw them as a political programme for physically joining Greece and Asia. The two had already formed one body for more than a thousand years, and marriages between people from opposite sides of the Hellespont had never aroused special curiosity.

This makes the following sentence from a school textbook all the stranger: “Alexander the Great revealed the East to the West. He also conceived the generous idea of blending Easterners and Westerners together and making Greeks and Barbarians into a single people.”

“Barbarians” — an unexpected word, and how revealing!

In any case, every surviving account points to a complete interweaving of religion and philosophy. At official ceremonies, Greek seers and Persian magi officiated side by side. In his fine book *Alexander and Asia*[^pierre-rossi-lci-005], Professor Altheim relates that at the funeral of Hephaestion, one of the King’s close companions, the sacred fires in the temples of Assyria and Babylonia were extinguished as a sign of mourning.

What exactly was Alexander’s religion? We do not know. It probably reflected the religious blending typical of the age. We see him honouring Dionysus and Heracles, the mythical ancestor of the Macedonian royal family, but also Orpheus, Cybele, El, Shamash, Jupiter, Isis, and Apis.

There was hardly a sacred community that did not receive Alexander’s gracious approval. His encounters with Jews are mentioned in the chronicles. At Tyre and Jerusalem he honoured the Palestinian god.

Yet it was in Egypt, on the edge of Libya, that he sought the highest consecration of all: that of Amun. The oracle of Amun recognized him and proclaimed him “Son of God,” giving him the same kind of sacred authority that the coronation at Reims would later confer on the kings of France.

Only a “Son of God” could properly claim to be head of state. By being integrated into the universal order, such a ruler became the guardian of natural law and therefore of the sovereignty needed to keep the human city aligned with the stars, the seasons, and the movements of the heavens.

The ancients might accept that an individual life was brief, but they saw the human city as an image of the city of God. It would have seemed wrong for such a city to be led by an ordinary man with no kinship to the society of the Immortals.

Power, in this world, was monarchical, and monarchy drew its legitimacy from divine right. That a Macedonian who had studied under Aristotle travelled to the Egyptian sanctuary of Amun to seek authorization to rule in Babylon is striking proof of the organic unity of the East.

Religion was one beneath its many outward forms; the state was one despite the decentralization of its communities; the Law was one despite the adjustments required by local conditions.

To succeed Darius III and wear the sovereign diadem, it was not enough for Alexander to be the candidate of the legitimist party. He also needed confirmation from above — and that “above” was Amun. Once Amun had spoken, all the religions obeyed.

Here is an extraordinary subject for historians of ancient religion and worship. If Egypt remained an all-powerful Vatican, if all gods looked toward Egypt as source and universal mother, then we are naturally led to accept that Egypt exercised an uncontested spiritual authority.

That in turn requires us to rethink the supposed originality of the religions usually called monotheistic. Fully absorbed into the wider harmony of Eastern mystical traditions, they too appealed to the God of Egypt.

More than a lesson in military strategy, Alexander’s experience therefore tells us something important about tolerance and about the real faith of the East. Looking beyond the boundaries of ritual, that faith rose to the highest level of thought, where human efforts meet and antagonisms disappear.

The ancients believed that rulers should govern from such a height. The King therefore had a duty to stand above all religions, to understand them, and to protect them all — in short, if not to be God, then at least to be a “Son of God.”

Alexander received that title, as Cyrus, Darius, and Ramesses had before him. How much ink this formula of reverence has caused to flow! Scholars have tried to see in it the sign of a new age, even the arrival of a pre-Christian Hellenism.

It was much simpler: a title that went with the crown. Nothing more — but nothing less.

As an Aramean king, Alexander belongs to Arab historical tradition at least as much as he belongs to Greek history. Most commentators seem to forget this. Schoolchildren make the same mistake when they imagine, for example, that Charlemagne was exclusively “French.”

The name Iskander, Iskandar, or simply Skandr is still given to Arab children. That too is interesting, because, contrary to what is usually thought, Alexander the Macedonian has nothing to do with it.

Iskandar is a very old Aramaic name from which the Greeks formed *Alexandros*, already the name of a Trojan prince — Paris, son of Priam. Once again, etymologists, most of whom in the West were trained as Hellenists, reversed the direction of borrowing and treated an Aramaic root as though it were derived from Greek.

Once again, they put the cart before the horse. If Arabs are called Iskander, they do not owe the name to Philip’s son; Philip’s son owed the name to them.

When Alexander died at the age of thirty-three in June 323, a year after returning from India, the East — and the East alone — determined the struggle over his succession. Queen Roxana was pregnant with the future Alexander II, who under dynastic law was expected to rule.

In the meantime, and still in accordance with the constitution then in force, a regency council was appointed. Philip Arrhidaeus, half-brother of the dead ruler, became provisional king, while the general Perdiccas became chief minister.

Suspicious of the political ambitions of Alexander’s generals, Perdiccas tried to preserve both the principle of divinely sanctioned hereditary monarchy and the unity of the empire. He was defeated and murdered.

The Persian Eumenes, formerly “minister of secrets” and keeper of the royal seal, took up the same cause. He too was executed on the generals’ orders.

Then the regent-king Philip Arrhidaeus was killed on the orders of his mother-in-law Olympias, mother of Alexander the Great, who was trying to protect her grandson — another Alexander. Olympias herself was then put to death.

Finally Roxana and her twelve-year-old son, at first held under house arrest, were executed in 310. Nothing remained of the royal family. It had been wiped out.

With it collapsed the political unity of the Aramean United States, firmly established by Darius from 522 BCE onward and resting on the two great pillars of Egypt and Babylonia. Alexander’s successors, unable to preserve imperial centralization, divided the inheritance and encouraged political divergence among the new states they created.

Yet the cultural, religious, economic, and strategic cohesion of the East remained so strong among the Aramean people that it survived every military and diplomatic upheaval. All that was needed was a sustained political project of sufficient scale for the lost unity to reappear.

The Roman Empire would restore it through Byzantium. The Arab caliphate and then the Ottoman Empire would preserve it in its Achaemenid integrity. The modern revival, under the name of Arabism or the “Arab nation,” now sees that unity as one of its dearest aims.

The theorists and strategists of Arabism are nothing other than the executors of the political testament of Darius and Alexander, carried by a popular tradition as old as the world itself. Instead of treating a reality rooted deep in the soil of the East superficially and according to the fashions of the moment, foreign ministries would do better to study Aramean history closely enough to understand its foundations.

But let us return to Alexander’s generals. After years of bloody fighting and intrigue so tangled that its threads disappear into a maze, the Battle of Ipsus in Phrygia produced an agreement dividing the empire into four states that were neighbours, partners, and rivals at once.

Macedonia and Greece became the hereditary possession of Cassander, son of Antipater. The Straits — a territory including Thrace and Asia Minor as far as the Taurus — went to Lysimachus. Assyria and Babylonia, from the Hindu Kush to the Aegean, went to Seleucus. Egypt, enlarged by southern Syria or Coele-Syria and most of the Arabian Peninsula, came under the rule of Ptolemy son of Lagus.

The Aramean world had therefore returned to something like the situation under Egypt’s Eighteenth Dynasty, when the East was divided between Egypt, Babylonia, and the Hittite kingdom, with the lands beyond the Hellespont little more than an appendage of those three great states.

History would repeat itself. Assyrians and Egyptians — now Seleucus and Ptolemy — would once again fight over Palestine and the Gulf of Aqaba. Armies would again move along the Orontes around Qadesh and Antioch. On the upper Euphrates, warriors coming from the Nile, the Persian Gulf, and Thrace would meet in battle.

Military reports would once more carry the names Gaza, Raphia, Carchemish, Byzantium, Salamis, Ephesus, and the rest. Nothing new under the sun, as Ecclesiastes might say — that faceless Babylonian who had seen it all.

Among the four rulers established in their four ancient capitals, one was more alert than the others: Ptolemy. He understood that true kings do not die, because a Son of God cannot share the ordinary fate of men. Even when the king dies, he lives on. He continues to “watch over the safety of the empire” and to lend authority to the faithful.

Ptolemy therefore managed to seize Alexander’s body, divert it from the road to Macedonia, take it home to Egypt, and bury it in the new city of Alexandria.

This was an event of enormous importance. For the first time, a Great King who had ruled both the Nile and Mesopotamia was buried in holy Egypt. Until then the Achaemenid kings had been buried east of the Tigris.

Both a real and a symbolic presence, Alexander’s body renewed Egypt’s imperial character and gave the Lagid dynasty an aura of special authority — especially since it had made Memphis its capital while waiting to settle permanently in Alexandria.

# VII

## PTOLEMIES AND SELEUCIDS: HEREDITARY RIVALS

Of the three dynasties that ruled by divine right — the Antigonids, Seleucids, and Ptolemies — the last two, both essentially Aramean, would dominate the history of the Mediterranean continuously into the seventh and eighth centuries CE. This remained true even during the period we call the “Roman Empire,” which, as we shall see, only became a truly important power once it presented itself, like Alexander before it, as heir to the Seleucid and Ptolemaic legacy.

Egypt retained its spiritual supremacy for a long time and remained the ideal model in matters of law. But it had the disadvantage of a population of only seven or eight million.
