Its role therefore gradually declined beside that of Syro-Babylonia, with some thirty million inhabitants and easier access to the wealth of Asia — to India, Southeast Asia, and China. Alexandria slowly gave way to Byzantium, later Constantinople. But the change would take six centuries.

When the Ptolemies first established themselves, Egypt still possessed all its mystical, economic, and strategic power. The Seleucids never managed to build anything quite as secure. Their northern and eastern frontiers were under constant threat. The king was a god. After the death of his predecessor, he established divine honours for him in a ceremony of “apotheosis,” a practice the Roman Empire would later turn into a constitutional ritual. The same idea existed in Babylonia, where a ruler might call himself *Epiphanes*: the visible god.

Across Egypt and Babylonia, in the kingdom of the Straits and in Greece, enormous sums were spent restoring the cults of Isis, Osiris, Cybele, Mithras, and Orpheus.

Tourists visiting the monuments of the Nile valley are sometimes surprised by the condition of the buildings, the freshness of the paintings, and the brilliance of the gold and ritual decoration. The reason is simple. The temples of ancient Egypt continued to be maintained and used for worship well into the Common Era. Their ruin and neglect came much later, especially with foreign invasions and Turkish rule, whose administrators had little interest in preserving an Aramean inheritance that was not their own.

Ephesus and Miletus were rebuilt, while new cities appeared in the kingdom of the Straits: Laodicea, Apamea, Pergamum. The Seleucids in particular were great city-builders. In Syria, their chosen heartland, between the upper Euphrates and the sea — precisely the country over which armies had fought for centuries — they founded magnificent Antioch and its port, Seleucia. Much farther east, among the oases of Merv in Turkestan, they created another Antioch. On the right bank of the Tigris, not far from the modern Iraqi town of Iskandiriya, they built Seleucia, a city whose population reached around 600,000.

We should not be surprised to find the same names appearing again and again. From Libya to the Hindu Kush there were Iskandiriyas, Ptolemaises, Seleucias, Antiochs, and Arsinoes, all named in honour of princes and princesses of the Macedonian dynasty. The religious and cultural unity of this new world was matched by a linguistic one. Greek existed alongside Aramaic, but Aramaic remained by far the language most commonly spoken by ordinary people. To call this a “Greek Empire” is therefore another distortion of reality.

Hellenism had never been anything more than the outward, written form of Aegean-Aramean civilization. It continued to provide the form of imperial life in the East, but not its substance. When Ptolemy III gave Alexandria its extraordinary splendour in the third century BCE and made it, in a real sense, the capital of the world, the city was Phoenician through its harbour, lighthouse, and maritime power. It was Palestinian-Babylonian through the religious traditions whose texts it gathered together. It was African through the Nile. It was naturally Egyptian in its architecture and cosmopolitan character.

What was Greek about it was a relatively small political and scholarly community.

Spengler’s observation[^pierre-rossi-lci-006] deserves attention:

> “What we know of Alexandrian mathematics presupposes a great movement in this field whose centre of gravity must have lain in the universities of Edessa, Gundeshapur, and Ctesiphon, with only certain features passing into the linguistic world of classical antiquity. Despite their Greek names, Zenodorus, who studied isoperimetric figures; Serenus, who studied the properties of a harmonic bundle of rays in space; Hypsicles, who introduced the Chaldean division of the circle; and above all Diophantus — the mathematicians of Alexandria were probably all Arameans, and their writings represent only a very small part of a literature written mainly in Syriac. This mathematics reached its fulfilment in Arab-Islamic science. After a long interval it was followed, as an entirely new creation on new ground, by Western mathematics — our own — which a strange optical illusion leads us to regard as mathematics itself.”

Alexandria, however, was not merely the capital of Aramean mathematical studies. Around the Museum, a true temple of knowledge and wide-ranging research founded by Ptolemy III, and around the Library established by Ptolemy I from an older pharaonic collection of between 400,000 and 700,000 written scrolls, gathered the intellectual elite of the civilization.

A few names will be enough.

There was Apollonius of Perga, the specialist in conic sections; Archimedes, the celebrated creator of theorems in geometry, physics, and mechanics; Eratosthenes and Dicaearchus, who measured the circumference of the earth; and Aristarchus of Samos, who long before Galileo demonstrated that the earth moved around the sun, as Chaldean astronomers had already taught. Studies of anatomy and medical dissection reached an astonishing level. Later, under Ptolemy VII, Asclepiades would distinguish himself in medicine, Hero in mechanics, Hipparchus in astronomy, and Didymus in philology.

Why, then, have so many modern commentators spoken of a “second-rate civilization”?

Its literature was extraordinarily rich, varied, and abundant. Theocritus, Bion, Lycophron — who mixed Arabic words into his Greek — and Callimachus, to mention only the best known, became masters for Virgil, Horace, Catullus, Propertius, and Ovid.

But there is no need to continue the list.

Callimachus came to Alexandria at the age of twenty and eventually became director of the Library. He was an Arab from Libya, more precisely from Cyrene. Through his father he descended from the ancient family of the Batta, while his mother’s name, Megatima — or rather Fatima — leaves no doubt about his origins. He was perhaps the finest representative of what came to be called Alexandrianism, whose influence on Petrarch and Ronsard is well established.

The lyric poetry of Theocritus, another Egyptian-Sicilian figure of this golden age, struck Bossuet as so Aramean in character that he compared his *Epithalamium for Helen* with the *Song of Songs*. There is nothing surprising in this since the biblical texts of the Old Testament were being written in Greek at roughly the same time as Theocritus. The geopolitical strategies of the Ptolemies and Seleucids are fascinating to follow. They were closely complementary, and they stretched both east-west and north-south.

Along the east-west axis lay the Pillars of Hercules, Carthage, Libya, Bactria, the Indus, and Mathura — regions that preserved Aramean traditions and language. Traces of this language can even be found in the royal administration of India’s Mauryan dynasty. The Red Sea and Persian Gulf provided the maritime routes of this east-west strategy. Its north-south corridors, moving from west to east, were equally remarkable.

One network of caravan routes came north from the Gulf of Guinea, converging on Libya or Carthage. From there goods travelled by sea to the mouth of the Rhône, then up toward the Rhine and onward to Britain and Scandinavia, eventually reaching Iceland. A second route followed the Nile and continued through the Greek islands and mainland before reaching Kiev. A third began in Yemen, crossed Arabia through Mecca, and eventually reached the Don valley by way of the Black Sea and Caspian.

A glance at a map is enough to show that both Ptolemies and Seleucids depended on the kingdom of the Straits — roughly modern Turkey — to use this last route. For a long time the region was a source of rivalry and conflict. Eventually, with Byzantium, it would become the keystone and dominant centre of the whole Seleucid-Ptolemaic system. This was, fundamentally, a continental strategy. Its maritime and port networks remained tied to rivers, coasts, and the Mediterranean because the limitations of ancient navigation made regular use of the Atlantic and Indian Oceans too difficult.

The Ptolemies had geography on their side. Their borders were secure, especially in the east where the Seleucids provided a barrier, so they needed relatively little major construction to expand their economic and diplomatic activity. Ptolemy II mainly had to link the Nile with the Red Sea and enlarge Alexandria. This placed the city in direct communication with the Indian world. He also built two Red Sea ports, Myos Hormos and Berenice.

To secure the trade route running north from Yemen toward Palestine and Sinai, he still needed the obedience of the small Sabaean kingdoms of Marib, El-Ula, Tayma, Yathrib, Mecca, and Umm al-Biyara — the settlement in the Nabataean lands where the fortress-city of Petra would later rise. According to Strabo, caravans took seventy days to travel from Aden to the Gulf of Aqaba. Ptolemy had little difficulty getting what he wanted, since the entire region had been under pharaonic protection since the earliest dynasties of Memphis.

Even today, road maps of the Arabian Peninsula still show El-Ula, Tayma, Petra, and nearby Ma'an as crossroads on the old caravan routes.

The Seleucids faced a much harder task.

At sea, they could use the old Babylonian port facilities in the Gulf of Bahrain, on the al-Hasa coast, especially at Gerrha near the modern silted-up port of Uqair. On the opposite side of the gulf, at the entrance to the Gulf of Oman, they enlarged the beautiful maritime city of Hormuz, capital of the province known as Carmania — modern Kerman. Here again we see the extraordinary ability of Aramaic place-names to survive the centuries. At the meeting point of the Tigris and Karun, on the site of modern Khorramshahr in Iran, they continued an Achaemenid project by opening a port.

Farther north, at a major road junction, they built the provincial capital of Antioch to control the hinterland. It later became Charax and corresponds to modern Shiraz. On land, however, toward Persis, Khorasan, and Bactria, the Seleucids encountered much greater difficulties. The Parthian peoples were stirring again. One of their leaders, Arsaces, proclaimed himself king in 247 BCE and founded a new Eastern dynasty, the Arsacids, which would rule until 226 CE — nearly five hundred years. From that point on, the history of the East, and therefore of the West, would have to reckon with this new power.

By the third century BCE, while Rome was still a young and uncertain state caught up in the Punic Wars, the growing weight of Central Asia was already beginning to upset the old Aramean order. At the same time, Thracians, Dacians, and Scythians looked greedily toward the cities of the Mediterranean and Mesopotamian East and dreamed of conquering them. Even before the Roman Empire had properly emerged, then, it was bound to be drawn into the orbit of an East larger, more threatening, more ambitious, and more warlike even than the East that had already absorbed Greece.

By the time Rome began to grow, the Nile-Babylonian world had expanded enormously. Its Greek-Aramaic bilingual culture extended from the Hindu Kush to the Danube and embraced more than fifty million people. Imagine the West and East of the third and second centuries BCE as the two sides of a balance. The western side is almost empty. The eastern side comes crashing down under the weight of its enormous mass.

Rome was not merely surrounded by the Aramean expansion. It was itself steeped in Arab-Asian traditions: Aeneas as its founding ancestor; Etruscan religion and attitudes; the Greek-Palestinian presence in Campania, Sicily, and Provence; and the deep Carthaginian influence extending from the coasts of Tunisia, Algeria, and Libya.

What weight could Rome possibly have beside all this?

Almost none.

No more than Athens had carried against Darius.

To understand our history, we need a map of Asia more than a map of Rome. Compared with the enormous events unfolding in Asia from the Aegean to the great plains of Indochina from the second century BCE onward, Roman history is a relatively minor episode. Its importance to us comes mainly from the fact that it affected our own later destiny. Measured against the movements that have shaken humanity, the speeches of Cicero and even the Gallic Wars are episodes.

In Sassanian tradition, for example, almost nothing of real importance happened in the world between the death of Alexander and the accession of Ardashir I in 227 CE. That alone tells us how little importance Rome could have outside the countries that later made its story their own.

We are going to repeat ourselves.

Before the so-called Persian Wars, we stressed the enormous imbalance between the Greek states and Persian power. The imbalance between young, rural Rome and the immensely wealthy cities of Asia was even greater. As far as we can tell, those cities had no equal anywhere in the world — not even in China or the Americas. Rome by itself could not therefore have been the power later tradition made it out to be. Culturally, as we have already argued, it was an Afro-Asian dependency. Economically, it was at least as poor in minerals and industry as Greece and could barely feed itself.

To whom, then, or to what, should we attribute the glory that later centuries attached to Rome?

How did a Roman Republic struggling along on poor land manage to seize the Mediterranean inheritance? The question cannot be avoided. Several misconceptions have to be cleared away first. We can begin by setting aside the grand but misleading explanations offered by works such as Montesquieu’s *The Greatness and Decline of the Romans*, which sometimes reads more like an entry for a prize in civic virtue than a work of political analysis.

The same applies to the academic histories of Jérôme Carcopino and his followers, who often explain history through the supposed inner feelings of individual conquerors — feelings reconstructed largely from the historian’s own passions. The reverence surrounding the Roman epic encourages us to accept the most misleading interpretations without question.

Faith in what might be called the Roman dogma lies behind the disastrous misunderstanding that produced an unnatural separation between Europe and Afro-Asian society. It created the unfortunate myth of “the West” and, with it, the danger of undermining the very foundations of our own culture. What happened to Rome was almost exactly what had happened to Alexander. The difference was that Alexander had been used by Babylonian diplomacy, whereas Rome was used by the Egyptian empire of the Ptolemies. The careers of Alexander and Caesar therefore run along parallel lines.

Roman policy, directed from the East and shaped by the needs of Egyptian strategy, was inevitably drawn toward the East. Its broad direction belonged to a much larger design in Eastern history. While Rome was still settling on its poor hills, the East was reorganizing a world already several thousand years old. Above all, the Ptolemies — who possessed the deified body of Alexander — wanted to reunify the Empire. That meant removing their hereditary enemies, the Seleucids, as well as the Antigonids who ruled Greece and Macedonia.

They relied on two main allies.

In the East they looked to Asia Minor, which controlled the upper Euphrates and inherited the old Mitanni-Hittite tradition. From there the Seleucid kingdom could be strangled at any moment. In the West they looked first to Sicily and then to Rome. Reviving battle plans that went back to Thutmose and Ramesses, the Ptolemies wanted to carry the war into Palestine and the upper Euphrates. At the same time they allied themselves with the Parthian Arsacids, who could attack the Seleucid Empire from the rear along the Tigris.

The Seleucids answered with alliances of their own.

They worked closely with the Macedonian dynasty, hoping to trap Asia Minor between two forces and absorb it. They also allied themselves with Carthage, which could threaten Egypt through Libya and cut Alexandria off from the western Mediterranean by bringing Sicily and Spain under the influence of Dido’s kingdom. What we are looking at, then, is a vast struggle between Aramean powers.

Given Rome’s Asian origins and its dependence on Sicily and the wealthy cities of southern Italy, Rome could not remain outside this game. The moment Alexander disappeared and his heirs began competing for his inheritance, Rome was pulled into the struggle. Roman wars and Roman diplomacy therefore cannot be understood within a merely local Italian setting. They were part of a vast strategic contest stretching from the Indus to the Ebro. Beside the great powers involved, Rome was still only a pawn in Ptolemaic hands.

The Ptolemies first pushed it into a counterattack against Pyrrhus of Macedon, then against Carthage, later against Macedonia itself, and finally against the Seleucids on Asian soil. We will pass over the years before 280 BCE, when Pyrrhus landed in Italy, because they are too legendary and obscure to support serious analysis. Even so, behind Rome’s wars against the Etruscans, Samnites, Latins, and Gauls we can already make out the influence of the Sicilian cities and Greek-Palestinian trading settlements, which were themselves Italian extensions of the great Aramean capitals of the East.

Rome was therefore already involved in Eastern conflicts before Pyrrhus ever confronted it. And Rome itself was not really what Pyrrhus was after. His aim was to take the Ptolemaic ports of Sicily and southern Italy. Once he was defeated, an enemy far more dangerous to Egypt took his place: Palestinian Carthage. Carthage too wanted Sicily, the Strait of Messina, the Strait of Otranto, and the Gulf of Taranto. At the same time it was intriguing against the Alexandrian court in Cyrene, while its Seleucid ally joined Macedonia against Egyptian possessions in Greece and Syria.

An urgent intervention against Carthage became necessary.

Rome provided it.

In the middle of peace, Roman troops suddenly occupied Messina. The First Punic War followed. During it, the Roman fleet was equipped, trained, and supported by the Egyptian navy. The war lasted eighteen years and ended in 241 BCE with Carthaginian forces driven from Sicily and the small kingdom of Syracuse — an ally of both Alexandria and Rome — strengthened. That same year Ptolemy brought a war against Seleucus to a successful conclusion and camped at Seleucia Pieria, at the gates of Antioch. For the Egyptian-Roman alliance, it had been an excellent outcome.

Carthage responded by seizing the rich mining and agricultural lands of Spain, along with the chain of ports running from the Pillars of Hercules to the mouth of the Ebro. These ports had served as bases for the Egyptian-Palestinian navy. The architect of this conquest was the famous Amalkher ben Barka, whom we have turned into Hamilcar Barca. Barka remains the Arabic name for Cyrenaica even today. After the death of Hiero, Carthage — still watching Syracuse closely — helped two of its supporters take power there.

Rome immediately intervened.

The Second Punic War lasted about seventeen years, from 218 to 201 BCE. On the Carthaginian side it was conducted with extraordinary skill by Hanna Baal ben Barka, Hamilcar’s son — the man we know as Hannibal. Before invading Italy through the Po valley, Hannibal made sure of strong military alliances with Philip of Macedon and with the Seleucid Antiochus. His alliance with Philip was confirmed and strengthened after the Battle of Cannae in Apulia. This leaves no doubt that the Second Punic War formed part of a carefully planned anti-Egyptian strategy.

Hannibal’s victories were overwhelming. The Carthaginian suffete, or chief magistrate, established himself at Capua and for nearly ten years ruled Italy like a sovereign.

Why, then, did he not take Rome?

The city had been stripped of troops, was hungry, and had partly been won over by Carthaginian gold. No serious local obstacle stood between Hannibal and an enemy capital that seemed close to surrender.

No one has ever given a satisfactory answer.

Why this strange restraint on Hannibal’s part?

The explanation may lie in the East.

In 217 BCE, a year before Cannae, Antiochus, Carthage’s ally, and Ptolemy, Rome’s protector, had fought the great Battle of Raphia south of Gaza. The Seleucid army was routed. A treaty was quickly concluded between the two rulers and remained in force for several years. Hanna Baal had lost his strongest and most important ally, and with him the substantial Asian subsidies his army required. It is striking that Carthaginian operations did in fact begin to drag on after Raphia. Carthage’s allies abandoned it in favour of the Eastern settlement Ptolemy had imposed after his victory.

Syracuse fell in 212.

In the Carthaginian hinterland, Egypt encouraged and financed the creation of the kingdom of Massin Aïssa — Massinissa — which began closing in around Carthage. The final blow came from Scipio, who landed in Africa and had little difficulty defeating at Zama a Hannibal already abandoned by his own side. In fact, Zama was a victory for Massinissa’s cavalry and elephants. Under the peace treaty of 201, Carthage had to recognize the new kingdom established on its doorstep under Ptolemaic protection. Rome, however, would now have to send its soldiers into the East.

When the treaty of 201 was signed, ambassadors from Egypt, Pergamum, and Rhodes came before the Roman Senate to warn it about the military ambitions of Philip of Macedonia — and Greece — who had once again joined Antiochus in an attempt to overturn the Eastern *status quo*.

Philip’s army was powerful, and so was his prestige.

At the ambassadors’ request, the consul Flamininus took command of Egyptian, Greek, Achaean, and Aetolian contingents, entered Greece, and defeated Philip at Cynoscephalae. Macedonia was pushed back to its earlier borders, and in 196 BCE the Greek states recovered their individual autonomy. The long decline of the Antigonid dynasty had begun, to the great satisfaction of the Ptolemies. Philip surrendered all his Asian possessions and was pushed back toward the Danube. He passed his frustrated ambitions on to his son Perseus.

Philip had scarcely abandoned his Asian territories when Antiochus moved in to occupy them. He then entered Coele-Syria and Palestine, took Cilicia, crossed the Straits, and threatened to annex Thrace. In 195 BCE he welcomed Hannibal to his court and gave him a command. The kings of Pergamum and Egypt urged the Romans to go and get themselves killed on their behalf.

The Romans obliged.

In 190 BCE Scipio Africanus, the victor of Zama, crossed the Hellespont with 30,000 men — exactly the same small force Alexander had once brought to these shores. The relatively minor Battle of Magnesia persuaded Antiochus to come to terms. Under the Treaty of Apamea, he handed Eumenes, king of Pergamum, Asia Minor north of the Taurus and evacuated Palestine and Syria. Hannibal, rather than fall into Roman hands, is said to have killed himself. Pliny tells us that his tomb could still be seen on the shore of the Sea of Marmara, outside Astacus.

Perseus, the last Antigonid, tried in vain to restore his family’s fortunes.

He was defeated at Pydna.

Macedonia became a Roman province, and its governor administered the Greek states, now reduced to little more than subordinate districts.

We have reached 146 BCE.

That same year saw the end of the Third Punic War, which had been provoked by Massinissa at Egypt’s instigation.

Carthage was destroyed.

One by one its possessions fell into Roman hands: Spain, Provence, the Rhône valley, Narbonese Gaul — in short, the coasts and great river routes of the western Mediterranean. After the death of Antiochus III and then his son Seleucus IV, Antiochus IV Epiphanes had made one more attempt to attack Egypt. In 169 BCE he had even advanced as far as the Nile Delta when a Roman embassy ordered him to withdraw. This shows just how thoroughly the Roman Senate had become an obedient instrument of Egyptian policy and strategy. It is doubtful that a Seleucid ruler of such power was genuinely frightened of Rome.

But Antiochus knew perfectly well whose spokesmen the Romans were and who stood behind their actions. One particularly revealing event would demonstrate just how deep and lasting the conflict between Antioch and Alexandria had become since Alexander’s death. When Eumenes, king of Pergamum, Ptolemaic vassal, and Roman ally, died in 160 BCE, he left the kingdom to his brother Attalus II, who ruled until 139. Eumenes’ son Attalus III then came to the throne but died without an heir in 133.

Pergamum was an obvious prize for Antioch.

But if it fell into Seleucid hands, Seleucid power would become so great that the independence of neighbouring states — and certainly of Egypt — would be threatened. Fearing precisely this, the Alexandrian court had advised Attalus III to leave his kingdom to the Roman people.

Pergamum therefore became a Roman province in 130 BCE.

That same year, the kingdom of Bactria, then under Seleucid protection, collapsed under attacks by armies from Turkestan. Rome was now stepping fully into both the sunlight and the shadow of the Near and Far East — sunlight and shadow which, in truth, had surrounded its cradle from the beginning. Two dates stand out at the end of this extraordinary period. The first is 146 BCE, when the Carthaginian Empire and the Greek Antigonid kingdom came to an end. The second is 130 BCE, when independent Pergamum disappeared.

Both were victories for the Ptolemies, whose position seemed more secure than ever as Seleucid decline accelerated. Rome and Alexandria now combined their efforts against Seleucid power. From outside, they used the Seleucids’ Parthian enemies to attack their eastern frontier. From within, they encouraged local movements for independence, rebellions, uprisings, and rival claimants to the throne. Look carefully at a map of the region in the middle of the second century BCE and one Seleucid territory appears especially open to direct Ptolemaic intervention: Palestine.

From the south, it was exposed to Egyptian influence because its territory merged into the Ptolemaic sphere. To the north stood Cyprus and the great mass of Asia Minor. Officially this region was governed by a Roman official. In practice, it was an autonomous kingdom under Egyptian influence, supported by ancient traditions, numerous immensely wealthy cities, a substantial army, and a large and active population. Palestine therefore became the main target of combined Egyptian-Roman intervention.

Meanwhile, in 141 BCE, the Parthians pushed into Babylonia, plundered Seleucia, and reduced the Seleucid dynasty to a state whose effective territory ended at the Euphrates. The weaker the Seleucids became in their eastern territories, the more determined they were to hold Palestine. For the same reason they placed enormous importance on their alliance with the Nabataeans, who controlled the overland trade between southern Arabia and Ethiopia while the sea remained under Egyptian control.

Through an alliance repeatedly renewed between Antioch and the Nabataean kings, the Seleucids could compensate in the west for what they were losing in the east. The Ptolemies, however, were firmly established at Gaza, the Mediterranean end of the caravan route. From there they kept the Nabataeans away from Mediterranean trade and confined them to the sands. Through alliances with the cities of Transjordan, and especially with Jewish leaders such as Hyrcanus, the Ptolemies also placed constant pressure on the Nabataeans. Caravans were raided and fortresses besieged.

A long and merciless struggle therefore developed between the Nabataeans and the Jewish communities of Jerusalem and other parts of Transjordan. In 168 BCE, for example, the Nabataean prince Arekas defeated Jason, the Jewish leader, who fled to Egypt. Our picture of these regions and their competing political currents is helped by the remarkable work of the Nabataean Iambulus. In the form of an imaginary travel story, something like *Sinbad the Sailor* or Marco Polo, he told of his adventures in the Fortunate Islands. The original text has been lost, but Diodorus Siculus and Lucian preserved extracts from it.

The Arabian Peninsula, whose shores faced the Indian Ocean and its two great extensions, the Red Sea and Persian Gulf, was therefore a major prize in the struggle between Alexandria and Antioch. It was only natural, in such a contest, for Egypt and Rome to support the independence of Seleucid cities and then bring those same cities under their own influence.

Jerusalem and its Jewish community provide one example.

It would be a serious mistake to look for hidden religious motives behind this policy.

We should repeat the point.

Between Amun, Baal, Yahweh, and the Cabirian gods worshipped at Rome, religious boundaries could be extraordinarily blurred. The different cults shared much the same atmosphere of sacred life. Ptolemies and Seleucids repeatedly “went up” to worship at the Jewish Temple in Jerusalem and elsewhere. Treaties between states were concluded in the names of everybody’s gods. The Greek historian Polybius has preserved the revealing oath used in the treaty of 215 BCE between the Aramean Hannibal and the Greek Philip V of Macedon.

Here, divinity clearly knew no borders:

> “In the presence of Zeus and Hera and Apollo; in the presence of the God of the Carthaginians, and Heracles and Iolaus; in the presence of Ares, Triton, and Poseidon; in the presence of the gods who fight beside us, and of the Sun and the Moon and the Earth; in the presence of the Rivers, the Lakes, and the Waters; in the presence of all the gods who rule over Carthage; in the presence of all the gods who rule over Macedonia and the rest of Greece; in the presence of the gods of all the peoples taking part in this expedition, and under their authority, the general Hannibal swears…”[^pierre-rossi-lci-007]

Josephus makes no secret of the confidential agreements linking Jerusalem with the Ptolemies, or of the military and financial support given by the Jewish high priests to enemies of the Seleucid dynasty such as Balas, Tryphon, and Zabinas, each of whom managed for a time to seize royal power in parts of the kingdom. In his *Antiquities of the Jews* (Book XIII, section 17), Josephus tells how the high priest Judas Maccabaeus sent Eupolemus and Jason as ambassadors to the Roman Senate to conclude an initial treaty which, he says, “was later renewed several times.”

Part of the treaty reads:

> “None of those subject to Roman authority shall make war upon the Jews or assist their enemies with grain, ships, or money. The Romans shall assist the Jews with all their power against those who attack them; and the Jews, in turn, shall assist the Romans in the same way if the Romans are attacked.”

The Egyptians and Romans pursued this policy so successfully that around 140 BCE they managed to separate the small principality of Jerusalem from the Seleucid kingdom. According to Josephus, a certain Aristobulus became the first crowned king of the Jews. His successor Alexander, however, proved so cruel and hated that the people rebelled and called on the Seleucids to come to their aid. Palestine had therefore become a fiercely contested battlefield between Alexandria and Antioch. The small local cities played secondary roles — as temporary allies, victims, clients, and vassals of one great power or another.

Because Christianity later focused enormous attention on this region, followed by a large body of writing shaped by that interest, the Jewish revolts alone have been singled out and magnified. Yet all the small cities — and there were countless such cities across Syria and Palestine — lived through much the same historical experience. They shifted from one protector to another and increasingly fought among themselves for the benefit of the neighbouring empires. Hyrcanus, prince of Jerusalem and ally of Rome, for example, besieged Samaria, a Seleucid client.

After a year of savage fighting, he “destroyed it completely and channelled torrents of water over it until it was left with no appearance of a city at all.” It is unfortunate that we do not possess the chronicles of Tyre, Shechem, Gezer, Beersheba, Jericho, Arad, Hebron, Gaza, and the many other cities of the region. If we did, we would find political histories almost identical to the difficult history of Jerusalem, with religion playing no larger a role than it did elsewhere.

The history of Palestine, governed by the ruthless double strategy of Ptolemies and Seleucids, should therefore be stripped once and for all of the divine halo that our habit of idealizing the past has painted around it. We should also abandon the mistaken belief that Jerusalem was the most important city in Palestine. Nowhere is it presented that way in Seleucid, Roman, or Ptolemaic chronicles. As late as the second century BCE, nothing marks it out as an object of exceptional reverence.

The oracles of Delphi and Phrygia, the oracles of the Libyan sands, and the Sibyls of Cumae and Ephesus still set the religious tone of the age. Gaza, Tiberias, Sebaste, Joppa, Adora, Larissa, Lydda, and Sidon were at least as important.

But where are the chronicles of those cities?

They were all holy cities, naturally, because every city in antiquity was holy by definition. And they were all capitals in their own way, because decentralized government made every major urban centre an administrative command in its own right.

# VIII

## ROME, AN EGYPTIAN COLONY

> “Rome was bought by the East.”

By the middle of the second century BCE, the stage was set for great historical changes. Alexandria had succeeded Babylon as the capital of a world in which Rome now occupied the place once held by Macedonia. Aramean political power and culture were spreading wider and sinking deeper into Western Europe, even as they began to lose ground in the East under pressure from peoples of the Far East. The Seleucids had already felt the first blows, a foretaste of the great Turkish and Mongol invasions to come. For the moment, Alexandria ruled the Mediterranean and Rome served it. In other words, Rome was becoming Arab.

We should remember what happened as soon as Rome brought Sicily and the Greek cities of southern Italy — Magna Graecia — into its administration. It was immediately flooded by a cosmopolitan world of banking and business financed from the great cities of Asia. This new system quickly destroyed the poor rural economy of the small pastoral and military town Rome had once been.

Contact with the great cities of southern Italy changed Rome itself. These cities were carefully planned and filled with monuments, temples, and philosophical and scientific schools of international fame. Roman language, civilization, and city planning were transformed at their core. The process went further once Rome entered the deeper East. It now had to deal with Asia Minor, Palestine, and the Seleucid principalities, and it was introduced to the imperial protocols and secrets of the Ptolemies. Rome grew larger in scale, but in doing so it was swallowed by the human tide of the East.

Republican Rome had once been, in Juvenal’s phrase, little more than a “lizard hole.” Its Senate house was a room twenty-five metres long and eighteen metres wide. There was no proper speaker’s platform and no heating, only wooden benches and a raised seat for the presiding officer. The capital barely had 50,000 inhabitants. The countryside was almost empty. According to Cato, rural estates were rarely larger than fifty or sixty hectares, and vineyards about twenty-five. Fields were worked with oxen or donkeys. Cato’s own advice shows how desperately short of labour Italy was.

There was simply no comparison with the rich estates of Egypt, Palestine, Syria, or Mesopotamia. Livy describes the Italian farmer ploughing almost naked under a burning sun, “clearing the stones and rocks of Samnium, drinking a little vinegar and water at the end of the field; three masters in the hut; a single slave.” It takes a great deal of imagination, or naivety, to believe that workers living under such conditions, supported by an economy of a few hectares of spelt, garlic, and lupins, somehow carried Rome to universal domination.

Yet the moment Rome became directly connected with the East, speculators began multiplying in the Forum. Money poured in. Interest rates reached and even exceeded fifty per cent. Cicero gives us a good picture both of financial speculation and of the speed with which Roman families made scandalous fortunes through partnerships with Eastern bankers. In *Pro Flacco*, his defence of a former governor of Syria, the hints are disturbing. They show how much Rome feared the hidden power of certain Eastern financiers.

More recent studies tell us that Aemilius Lepidus, who died in 152 BCE, instructed in his will that no more than one million asses — 285,000 francs — be spent on his funeral. The tribune Drusus owned 900,000 francs’ worth of silver plate. Pompey’s fortune amounted to about twenty million francs, while the actor Aesop possessed six million. In 62 BCE, on the eve of his departure for Gaul, Julius Caesar’s debts were close to seven million francs; Mark Antony’s would reach eleven million.

Under the Empire the bank balances only grew larger. Pallas, the famous freedman of Claudius, had 300 million sesterces — about sixty million francs — to his name. Nero’s associate Narcissus had 400 million sesterces, or eighty million francs.[^pierre-rossi-lci-008] The Atilian and Voconian laws tried in vain to hold back this enormous wave of speculation.

Where did all this money come from?

Certainly not from Roman raw materials, Roman industry, or Roman labour. It came from abroad, in payment for services, and it gave the patricians the purchasing power they needed to import finished goods from Spain, Bithynia, China, the Caucasus, Babylonia, Arabia, Sudan, and Egypt. All these regions had long been tied together by networks of Asian bankers and businessmen, for whom Roman families served little more than as agents. Rome was therefore bought by the East. Its sovereignty was more theoretical than real.

Roman society had been turned into a society of consumers, and eventually that was almost all it was. The flood of money, combined with runaway inflation, destroyed the working classes. There was no longer much room in Rome for craftsmen, labourers, small manufacturers, or local merchants. Agriculture itself was increasingly left to slaves. Wheat, wine, salt, hides, livestock, and even honey were now imported. What had happened to Athens had happened to Rome: the whole economy fell into foreign dependence.

Keeping this in mind will help us understand what Roman diplomacy really was, stripped of the peacock feathers with which it has so often been decorated. Soon there was little to distinguish Rome from Alexandria, Antioch, or Pergamum. Everywhere people spoke the same common language, a simplified Greek laid over Aramaic or Italic speech. They wore much the same clothes, reclined at meals, and practised the same cults. Alexandria set the tone of intellectual life.

Despite the resistance of old Cato, an anachronistic survivor from another age, the pastoral city of Romulus and Remus had adjusted itself to the world of Isis and Cybele. Rome’s wealthy districts and its successive forums were built in the image of the cities of Egypt and Asia Minor.

The Greeks had taken from Eastern architecture the great straight lines of earlier ages. Rome, arriving late, followed the newer fashion instead. From Aramean architecture it borrowed a taste for curves, multiplying vaults and round arches. The dome became a standard feature of public monuments. Builders even adopted the hemispherical vault so characteristic of Babylonia. These techniques gave Roman buildings a scale and freedom they had previously lacked. A traveller arriving in Rome from the Euphrates or the Nile would not have felt far from home.

Private houses followed the traditional Aramean plan: a large inner courtyard surrounded by a colonnade, opening onto the main reception room — the *oecus*, from the Greek *oikos* — and the bedrooms. The very idea of living around a courtyard could only have arisen among peoples accustomed to a nearly rainless sky. The native houses of northern Italy, by contrast, turned inward behind enclosed walls.

A twentieth-century Arab would probably have felt perfectly at home in a Roman house of the age of the Caesars, because its basic design, arrangement, and furnishings were Aramean. The presence in Rome of large numbers of Syrian architects, master builders, and decorators is in any case attested from an early date.

It would be wrong to imagine that the ordinary peoples of Italy shared in this luxury or in the magnificent incomes of the Equestrians and Patricians. They were shut out of both. As land values collapsed and manual labour lost its economic importance, these populations became useless to the new system. They also lost political responsibility, since voting depended on wealth and was reserved for citizens who had money to their name. Unemployed and politically powerless, they remained available for military service, where the authorities did not hesitate to keep them almost permanently.

So who governed Rome?

Foreigners.

A small, gilded class of notables, lavishly financed by Asian bankers whose interests it served, lived inside its own closed world. It did not so much govern the country as administer a few branches of government that it treated as private property. We would have little difficulty finding comparable examples in more recent history.

Serious social crises therefore shook the Roman world again and again without ever reaching its real centre, because that centre lay elsewhere, scattered among the corporations of the East. The agrarian reforms attempted by the Gracchi, crushed before they could begin, were so completely opposed to the established financial system that they were doomed in any case, even if their authors had not been murdered.

Rome remained subject to the policies of commercial and financial organizations of the kind we would now call multinational corporations. Palestinians, Egyptians, Phrygians, Greeks, Libyans, and Sicilians controlled these powerful networks. The visible actions of Roman policy, as they are presented in our schoolbooks, were often only the reflection of decisions taken elsewhere and for purposes quite different from those usually claimed. In any case, Rome lived, thought, and acted with its face turned toward the East, lit by the East and drawn toward it.

Some writers of the imperial age were scandalized by what they saw as a betrayal of ancestral values and loudly attacked the new fashion. “The dandy,” Martial writes, “is a man who hums Egyptian or Spanish songs under his breath.” Juvenal rages against the priests of Cybele in their Phrygian caps, now lording it over Rome. He complains about “Jews selling nonsense,” Armenian soothsayers, and Chaldean magicians. He pities “the poor devils who run off to consult the oracle at the Pyramids of the Circus (?)”; mocks organized tours to the East and pilgrimages to Egypt, Memphis, and the temples of Jupiter Ammon; and laughs at those who return carrying talismans or holy water.

The major conflicts that eventually produced the imperial system look, on closer inspection, much more like civil wars than foreign wars. Rome, remarkably enough, never won any great external wars, because until very late it hardly fought any. And when it finally did, it lost them. For a long period, history records no real national resistance in the East, Libya, or even Gaul to what has been misleadingly called the Roman conquest.

Take first the war against Jugurtha, heir of Massinissa and ruler of Numidia. Sallust’s account is revealing. Rome, we discover, was split into two camps, one paid by Jugurtha and the other by Prince Gauda and King Bocchus of Morocco. The conflict was in reality a local war, driven by Bocchus’s ambition to expand eastward at Jugurtha’s expense.

Sallust states quite plainly that during the campaign Marius and his lieutenant Sulla “took their orders from Bocchus.” Roman armies were therefore being used as mercenary forces. Their commanders, Sallust admits without embarrassment, fought to make money for electoral purposes. Foreign princes — Bocchus and Jugurtha in this case — were directly involved in Rome’s domestic political life.

The North African war ended, as we know, with Jugurtha’s defeat and the expansion of the Moorish kingdom. But it had another result, and this was the one that really interested Sallust: the prestige and money Marius gained carried him to the highest office in the state.

The final lines of the *Bellum Jugurthinum* say it clearly:

> “As soon as Rome learned that the Numidian war was over and Jugurtha had been captured, Marius, although absent, was made consul and the province of Gaul was assigned to him. Then, on the Kalends of January, he enjoyed the great honour of a consular triumph. The fortune and hope of the state now rested in him.”

African gold and silver had helped produce this good fortune.

But where did that good fortune lead?

To Asia: the supreme object of ambition, the final destination of every self-respecting Roman. To win Asia’s respect, to secure an appointment there, to gain access to the sources and splendours of civilization, to be received at the luxurious courts of the East, to share in human and divine knowledge — that was the dream. But credit is extended only to the rich, and Asia was open only to men already distinguished by fame and financial standing. These therefore had to be acquired by every available means before setting out for the Promised Land.

What did Marius do as soon as Bocchus’s money and his military victory had secured his position? He went to Asia. What did Sulla, his lieutenant and partner in success, do? He went to Asia. Why did Julius Caesar launch his expedition into Gaul? To make himself worthy of Asia. And so it continued. Not a single Roman consul, dictator, or emperor would escape this ritual act of allegiance, until Rome finally settled in Asia for good, like a river that had at last returned to its source.

The race toward the East became dramatic in the rivalry between Pompey and Caesar, still within the close alliance linking Rome and Egypt against the Seleucids. The ambitions of Mithridates VI Eupator, king of Pontus, alarmed the Ptolemies all the more because the Seleucids were preparing to support him. Mithridates had for a time seized Asia Minor and landed in Greece. The Ptolemies intervened.

Pompey fought alongside them without playing any decisive part. In 63 BCE Mithridates was forced to flee and take his own life, not because he had suffered a military defeat, but because his son Pharnaces had rebelled against him and taken control of the army.

Behind the grand phrases of Cicero’s *Pro Lege Manilia* and *Pro Murena*, both written in support of the Pompeian cause, we find a rather unimpressive adventure. The clearest result of Pompey’s expedition was that it brought him the money and prestige he needed to enter the First Triumvirate with Caesar and Crassus in 60 BCE. He was awarded the title *Asiaticus*. Caesar, however, was also thinking of Egypt. As early as 65 BCE, when he was still curule aedile, he had put himself forward for a special mission to the Ptolemaic court. He was passed over and watched Pompey leave for the East in his place.

Now Caesar wanted, as quickly as possible, to acquire the prestige and the sesterces that would make him a worthy partner in the eyes of Asia. So he went to Gaul in 59 BCE and completed its “conquest” in 51 — with a handful of men and by the usual Roman miracle. No one can seriously be expected to believe that Caesar played the role he later claimed for himself. A careful reading of the *Bellum Gallicum* suggests instead that he was little more than a mercenary in the service of the Aedui, the true masters of Gaul and the architects of its unification.

Part election pamphlet and part epic falsehood, perfectly suited to its age, the “Gallic War” allowed Caesar to return to Rome with the aura of a conqueror and a substantial fortune, ready at last for the great Eastern enterprise. Having secured Ptolemy’s goodwill, Caesar set off in pursuit of Pompey and his supporters and defeated them at Pharsalus in Thessaly. Pompey escaped by sea to Lesbos and then to the coast of Cilicia, from where he crossed into Egypt.

Unfortunately for him, Caesar’s envoys had arrived first. No sooner had Pompey landed than he was beheaded on the pharaoh’s orders by a detachment of soldiers that included Romans. Caesar, paying homage to his divine ancestors, made a pilgrimage to the ruins of Troy before taking the road in turn to the “kingdom of Pharos,” as the poet Lucan calls Egypt. He descended into Alexander’s tomb and bowed before his remains.

Received like a prince at the pharaonic court, he married Cleopatra, the king’s sister, and stayed in Egypt for almost ten months. There he was initiated into geography, astronomy, and the occult sciences, preparing himself for the role of king. The high priests gave him their blessing, and the Ptolemaic dynasty appeared willing to accept this hard-bitten warlord as a useful vassal, provided he committed himself to total war against the Seleucids.

So in 47 BCE Caesar and the Egyptian high command drew up in Alexandria a detailed campaign plan aimed at taking control of Alexander’s former empire from the Atlantic to the Indus. Once the agreement had been reached, Caesar could have returned directly to Rome. Instead, he first sent Cleopatra there, pregnant with his child, while he himself travelled through Libya, Tunisia, and Spain accompanied by the Egyptian fleet, destroying what remained of the Pompeian forces at Thapsus and Munda.

When he finally returned to Rome in 45 BCE, he had the bearing, authority, and ambitions of an Eastern prince. As pontifex maximus, consul, tribune, and holder of the *imperium*, he was elected dictator for life on 15 February 44 BCE. He proclaimed his divine ancestry and surrounded himself with a court protocol identical to that of Alexandria. Egyptian bankers opened unlimited credit to him. Suetonius claims that Ptolemy had given him 6,000 talents directly, each talent worth about 5,500 gold francs. For the first time in Roman history, the mints began striking gold currency, the *nummus aureus*.

Under these conditions, to speak of Roman independence — still more of Roman sovereignty over Egypt — is almost comic in its contradiction. Rumours spread that Caesar intended not only to take the title of king but to move the capital of the Roman Republic to the East. The idea seemed all the more plausible because he now had a son by Cleopatra and was living openly with her.

At the beginning of 44 BCE he prepared to honour his commitments and clear the Seleucids from the East. Caesar would attack from the north while the Egyptians attacked from the south. He had mobilized around 100,000 men. Italy and Greece, ruined by the banking economy of the East and suffering severe unemployment, had become inexhaustible reservoirs of soldiers. Asia Minor, Pontus, and Armenia had also been brought into the alliance and mobilized their own forces.

Caesar was to move down through the Danube region, Thrace, Anatolia, and the upper Tigris and join the Syrian-Egyptian regiments. The Seleucid Empire would have been finished — had Caesar not been murdered on 15 March 44 BCE by a conspiracy led by Brutus.

Jérôme Carcopino’s comment is illuminating:

> “With the same blow Brutus smothered the war and destroyed Caesar’s great design, just as in modern times Ravaillac’s knife cut short Henry IV’s plan to open hostilities against the German emperor in 1610.”[^pierre-rossi-lci-009]

Caesar’s murder had been preceded by a revolt in Syria, joined by former Pompeian officers serving the Seleucids. We may therefore ask whether the Seleucids themselves had anything to do with Brutus’s conspiracy. Wrapped in legend, Julius Caesar’s life has reached us in a form polished century after century by our obsession with “great men.” Much of its historical reality has disappeared. In truth, neither Caesar’s life nor his death could do much to alter the deeper events driven by the economy, geopolitics, and cultural development of the East.

Octavian, Caesar’s adopted son and heir, was carried by the same forces toward Egypt and toward Cleopatra, whom he had already met many times in Rome in his father’s apartments. The political struggle between Mark Antony, Caesar’s lieutenant, and Octavian, his heir, has been turned into a melodrama of good and evil. Antony is portrayed as a drunken soldier lost in luxury and lust, while Octavian is dressed in all the cardinal virtues expected of an emperor. To complete the picture, Cleopatra is cast in the dubious role of a glamorous prostitute.

All of this is bad literature.

In reality, the competition that had once set Pompey and Caesar against each other in the race for the East began again after Caesar’s death, this time between Octavian and Antony. There is little point in losing ourselves in the bloody details of the war of Mutina or the factional struggles involving Cicero, Lepidus, and so many others. These are episodes of small-scale history. The great game was being played in the East.

That was where the conspirators Brutus and Cassius fled. There they raised large armies in Antigonid and Seleucid territory — that is, in Greece and Macedonia. Such an operation required enormous sums of money, which could only have come from the kingdoms of Pergamum, Alexandria, and Antioch. But Pergamum and Alexandria supported Caesar’s followers — Cleopatra and her son were still in Rome — so it was Antioch that paid the wages and upkeep of the “Pompeian” army commanded by Brutus and Cassius. The two unfortunate men were killed by the Caesarians at Philippi in Macedonia in 42 BCE.

The victors, Antony and Octavian, then divided their roles. The Egyptians took Antony in, installed him at court, and for five years he acted as a kind of military steward. He travelled throughout the Ptolemaic East dressed in Eastern clothing and paid his devotions to Isis and Osiris.

Cleopatra, meanwhile, ruled.

Was Antony one of her lovers? Did she have children by him? Possibly. But such details from the queen’s private life explain nothing about the politics of the age. As Caesar’s designated heir, Octavian naturally had little reason to enjoy watching Antony grow rich in the East and perhaps prepare to put on the royal diadem. Egypt’s great dynasties had more than once seen an army commander or a mercenary officer of the guard rise all the way to the pharaonic throne.

Cleopatra, for her part, had been unhappy that Antony disobeyed her instructions during his missions in Palestine and Syria. Josephus says so plainly. Caught in intrigues involving the Arsacids, Seleucids, Armenians, and Palestinian cities, did Antony go so far as to plot against the queen? What is certain is that Octavian came to Egypt only after the Egyptians themselves called him there.

In the famous “inscription of Ancyra,” carved on the walls of a temple in ancient Galatia in Asia Minor, Octavian tells the story of his own life. He does not speak of a war against Egypt, which would have been the natural formula, but simply of the “war of Actium.” Even the circumstances of the Battle of Actium are highly ambiguous. The Egyptian fleet unquestionably controlled the Mediterranean, while the Roman fleet was of little importance. Was there even a real battle?

It hardly seems so. The chroniclers agree that the Egyptian ships abandoned Antony and sailed south. They also agree that Cleopatra was waiting for Octavian and refused to receive Antony when he returned wounded from this supposed battle. In Velleius Paterculus’s lively account, the fighting simply stops because there are no fighters left, and Octavian finds himself wondering for whom and with whom his soldiers are still fighting — *pro quo et cum quo pugnarent*.

When Caesar’s adopted son landed at Alexandria, he was welcomed, just as Alexander had been, not merely as a friend but as heir to the crown. He inherited his father’s Eastern plans and the consecration Caesar had received from the high priests and from the queen herself. He was therefore the obvious successor to the Ptolemies.

The fact that he was a foreigner did not trouble the high priests, the real guardians of the monarchy. For one thing, nationality mattered far less in that age than it does to us. The old French monarchy, much later, could perfectly well have passed to an Italian or Spanish prince. For another, Octavian was succeeding the Ptolemies, who were themselves of foreign origin.

More importantly, Egyptian banking, trade, and economic power stood so far above the politics of the moment that the identity of the pharaoh was more symbolic than tyrannical. Any Egyptian ruler was simply the spokesman of Egypt’s divinity, the protector and true sovereign of heaven and earth. A grain of dust in the hand of the Eternal, Octavian was therefore accepted as ruler of Egypt without any change in the life of the people. How did Cleopatra really die? Why did her fleet and army abandon her? We have no answers.

Classical historians speak of the “annexation” of Egypt to the Roman Empire. That is a mistake. Egypt was assigned personally to Octavian. Senators and Roman administrators were forbidden access to it. The country remained the “domain of God.” Octavian was worshipped there not as a Roman, but as an embodiment of the god. He wore the *pschent*, the double crown, and appeared enclosed in hieroglyphic formulas, honoured with the title “King of Upper and Lower Egypt, son of Ra, bearer of the Diadems.” The temples of Dendera, Philae, Aswan, and Thebes carry his cartouche and sacred emblems.

Octavian carried this imperial cult back to Rome.

The city of Aeneas became an image of Egypt not only in culture and religion but in its political constitution. The Roman Empire founded by Octavian was not the natural outcome of the internal development of the republic of Cicero and Cato. It was Egyptian in inspiration, pharaonic in nature, and unrelated to Rome’s old native rural customs.

From Octavian onward, monarchy by divine right was firmly established. The emperor was called Caesar, Son of God, Prince of the Senate, Augustus — that is, Sacred. He was commander of the armies, consul, and tribune of the people for life. Altars honoured him while he lived; after his death, a senatorial decree of apotheosis officially raised him to the rank of the gods. The old religion took on a mystical character. Popular superstitions were forbidden, while Asian and Egyptian priests, protected by the emperor, took part in priestly instruction.

Christianity would soon find in Rome a climate and a way of thinking ready to receive it, just as Rome had received Orpheus, Isis, Mithra, Baal, and Jehovah. When Jesus was born in Bethlehem, the conditions were already favourable for Rome to become a city open to his message. Palestine, an Egyptian possession, came under the authority of Augustus, and it was at his request that a general census of the population was carried out, following the old practice of the pharaonic scribes. The Gospels recall this census when establishing Jesus’s genealogy and the exceptional circumstances of his birth.

As a subject of Egypt under Augustus and then Tiberius, Jesus was not a Roman, but he nevertheless lived under the law of a Roman pharaoh. This helps explain the extraordinary place the City of the Caesars would later hold in the government of the Christian Church: Rome possessed the sovereignty of Egypt. Roman power was Roman only in name and Western only by geography.

This ambiguity is unmistakable, in our view, from the moment Caesar and his son Augustus enter the scene, but it can be detected much earlier. Virgil’s work, Asian in spirit and form, sheds light on the hidden paths of a politics whose effects still shape many features of modern society.

Roman historians, on the other hand, from Livy to Tacitus and Flavius Josephus, have done little to help us understand it. Their texts offer little more than an unqualified celebration of Rome, with every other people presented as defeated captives tied to the chariot of the *imperatores*: Lusitanians, Gauls, Dalmatians, Greeks, Libyans, Egyptians, Syrians, Cilicians, Ephesians, and on and on.

It is a great deal. Far too much.

How can we not regret the absence of a Roman Thucydides? How can we not be troubled by the absurdities, falsehoods, or ignorance of Livy, Suetonius, Tacitus, and so many others? Was this simply extreme nationalism? Probably not. The age was hardly suited to it. To project our relatively modern idea of “love of country” backward into antiquity would be to put the feelings of a later age into the mouths of the past.

Many possible explanations come to mind for the bias of the Latin commentators. Strangely, the bias always points in the same direction: toward a Rome that is universal, divine, blameless, and the seat of every virtue, when the evidence points elsewhere.

Perhaps the explanation lies in the fact that all surviving Greek and Latin manuscripts are relatively recent copies rather than originals. These copies were made by Christian monks and churchmen in monasteries and universities belonging to the Byzantine Empire, where from the time of Constantine onward Christianity was the state religion and “Romanity” an established dogma.

Orphists, followers of Osiris and Dionysus, Jews, and others did not enjoy the university privileges that the Byzantine state and its successors granted almost exclusively to the Christian Church. In the West this monopoly lasted for something close to two thousand years. In the East, the Muslim state, in its tolerance, saw no reason to subject the results of Byzantine historical scholarship to a new critical examination. In this way it helped preserve and circulate Greek and Latin documents whose authenticity was by no means guaranteed.

What remains in our hands, then, are texts arranged, corrected, and interpreted by secular or religious authorities with a direct interest in bending the truth toward their own orthodoxy. The amount of copying and compilation carried out in monasteries over the centuries is astonishing. But that achievement is also disturbing, precisely because this work took place at the source of nearly all the documents on which we depend. The emperor Julian, called the Apostate, strongly denounced Christianity’s control over the documents of antiquity. His anger changed nothing, and his reign was brief.

It follows that the only ancient documents we can regard as truly secure are monuments, inscriptions cut into stone, and cuneiform texts impressed into clay. These escaped, at least partly, the zeal of those who “adapted” history. Only partly, because many monuments were destroyed and entire libraries of cuneiform tablets disappeared, notably during the destruction of the famous Museum of Alexandria. If we wanted to be rigorously scientific and remove as much uncertainty as possible, we would build our historical arguments only on documents that were actually inscribed.

Take the manuscripts of Caesar’s *Gallic War*. There are no fewer than fifty in the hands of the Church: thirty-three in the Vatican Library and seventeen in Florence. The two oldest date only from the ninth century. They are full of interpolations, mixtures of different material, geographical errors that are hard to explain, and strange formulas that sound like translations. We even know the names of at least two men who corrected the Caesarian text. They lived in the sixth century CE and were churchmen: J. C. Constantinus and F. L. Firminus Lupicinus, nephew of Ennodius, bishop of Pavia.

Did Caesar himself dictate the *Bellum Gallicum* or write it in his own hand? We simply do not know. Even in Caesar’s own time, his friend Pollio thought the account suspicious. Cicero’s correspondence expresses similar reservations. Suetonius speaks of scandal. If the original account, which we no longer possess, was already suspect and perhaps not even written by Caesar, how much more doubtful is the version that has reached us through the ignorance, adaptation, invention, or betrayal of copyists and grammarians? And the *Bellum Gallicum* is actually one of the less disputed texts.

There are manuscripts of the same work that contradict each other so badly through their notes and variants that without complete reconstruction they are impossible to understand. Any study of antiquity that does not leave a very large place for scepticism is not merely mistaken but dangerous, because it sends thought down arbitrary paths.

With Caesar Augustus, then, Rome’s integration into the East was complete. The borders of what we call the Roman Empire now coincided with those of the Ptolemaic Empire. The Seleucids had been reduced to the Syrian provinces. The Parthians under the Arsacid dynasty stood on the Euphrates and extended their power all the way to the Indus. It was they who would eventually draw Mediterranean politics farther and farther east, giving Asia a lasting victory.

The historian Pompeius Trogus, himself an Asian, wrote his chronicles in Caesar’s time. In Books 41 and 42 of his *Universal History*, later summarized by Justin in the second century CE, he explains how the Parthians had become a “world” power. Their capital, Ctesiphon, built on the left bank of the Tigris opposite Seleucia, was famous for its wealth.

As long as the Seleucids remained powerful, the Arsacid Parthians could fight them without allowing the conflict to turn into hatred. They also maintained cordial relations with the populations described as “Greek.” The Arsacids went so far as to call themselves “Philhellenes,” using Greek and striking coins in the Greek style. Everything changed once Romano-Egyptian power established itself in Palestine and the Seleucids were reduced to the small territory of northern Syria.

The Arsacids had pushed back the Seleucids in order to establish their own sovereignty, not to watch it challenged once again by newcomers. They therefore began a war against Egypt and its Roman allies that would never truly end. They fought all along the Euphrates, but also in southern Palestine, where they tried to outflank the Syrian positions and threaten the commercial and strategic routes linking Egypt, Gaza, and the road to Aden.

The war began under Tiberius and continued through Caligula, Claudius, and Nero. In the end it helped bring down the Caesarian dynasty and carry to power the commander-in-chief in Palestine: Vespasian, founder of the Flavian family. Nero had sent him there not “merely to punish the revolt of the Jews, but to keep the rest of the East obedient.”[^pierre-rossi-lci-010] Fighting beside him was his son Titus, who had come from Alexandria with troops, funds, and instructions from the Egyptian government. So many errors have been spread about this supposed “Jewish War” that some clarification is necessary.

It was a Parthian war directed by the authorities in Ctesiphon, whose aim was to free Syria from hated Roman domination. They had managed to win the support of several wealthy cities in southern Palestine. Some were indeed Jewish, like Jerusalem; others were Samaritan or “native,” to use Josephus’s curious expression, like Tarichaea. The Idumaean Arabs of southern Jordan, for example, made up “the great majority of the forces of Simon and John, the Jewish captains.” Arsacid officers commanded regiments fighting the Romans: Monobazus, Senneba, Niger, Peraite, and Tlass.

Titus would later accuse the Jewish community of “acting in agreement with the people of the Euphrates.” And the Palestinians allied with the Arsacids were not fighting only on land. Ships from the port of Joppa attacked the merchant fleets of Egypt and Syria. The kings of Commagene and Armenia, also won over by the Arsacids, threatened the Romans from the rear.

The siege of Jerusalem, conducted by Titus after his father left for Rome, was therefore part of a strategic struggle extending across all the territories east of Sinai. It was a dangerous situation for the Roman command. According to Josephus, troops had to be pulled away from the Euphrates front and sent as reinforcements to Ashkelon, Joppa, Jerusalem, Tarichaea, and elsewhere. Antioch itself almost fell to the Arsacids. The disaster would have been irreparable.

That is enough to explain both the ferocity of the fighting and the relief that greeted Titus’s capture of Jerusalem in September 70 CE, especially in Egypt. It was to Egypt that Titus sent most of his prisoners. Egypt also welcomed the emperor Vespasian in triumph when he came there in person. Egyptian resentment toward the Jewish community was all the more bitter because relations between the two had been excellent for centuries. Reprisals came quickly, but they did not strike the whole community. Far from it.

Only people of modest means had joined the Arsacids. The educated and wealthy classes, financially linked to the leading circles of Antioch and Alexandria, had remained loyal. Flavius Josephus himself is an example. Many Palestinians of every religion had enlisted in the Roman regiments. Egyptian anger therefore fell mainly on the poorer population and on a few leaders accused of treason. In Alexandria, the synagogue that had stood for 343 years under the Ptolemies was closed by order of Vespasian. In Cyrene, Jews and “Greeks” fought in the streets and burned whole districts.

But neither the reprisals, nor the fall of Jerusalem, Ashkelon, and Tarichaea, nor the presence of a large military police force brought peace to Palestine. Arsacid agents continued to move through the region, and the enormous scale of Sino-Indian trade made access to Mediterranean ports increasingly essential for Ctesiphon. War broke out again with renewed force under the Antonine dynasty, with Armenia and Palestine once more serving as the main centres of conflict.

The emperor Trajan, after treacherously arranging the murder of the king of Armenia, entered Mesopotamia, seized Ctesiphon, and followed the Tigris down to the Gulf. But his lines of communication were cut. After a three-year campaign he was forced into a hurried retreat and died from exhaustion at Selinus in Cilicia in 117 CE.

The same drama returned under his successor Hadrian. Syria and Jerusalem revolted. Cassius Dio, the historian of this period, describes the priests Eleazar and Simon taking control of Jerusalem, which Hadrian had renamed Aelia Capitolina by decree. The Romans retook the city in 134, but even then they failed to turn it away from the Arsacid cause. This is clear evidence of how insecure the power of Alexandria and Rome had become in the region, and how strongly the Asian world was pressing in.

Marcus Aurelius in turn went to war against Vologases IV, who had invaded Armenia and raised Syria in revolt, bringing half the country under his control through the daring of officers from the Sassanid tribe. By now the Romans and Egyptians were discouraged. They considered themselves unable to hold territories so close to the enemy and populated by people so unwilling to make their task easier.

A new policy began to take shape: political and military authority would increasingly be entrusted to Syria itself. It was almost as though Rome regretted destroying Seleucid power, whose presence had once provided a protective barrier. The need for a Syrian policy independent of Egypt was becoming obvious. Civil and military responsibility was therefore given to a Syrian governor, Avidius Cassius. After driving back the Parthians, Cassius launched his troops against Ctesiphon and Seleucia. His ambitions were clearly large.

Some claimed that his mistress was the wife of Marcus Aurelius himself, an Arab woman concerned with restoring Seleucid greatness. Unfortunately for her, Cassius was murdered. But the idea survived him. Under the growing weight of Central Asia and the Parthians, Syria and the countries east of Sinai were gaining ever greater political importance. With Septimius Severus, an Arab dynasty reached the highest office of the Empire in 193 CE.

The emperor himself was a Libyan from Leptis Magna. His wife, Julia Domna, was the daughter of Bassi, priest of the sun at Emesa, the ancient city on whose site modern Homs stands. There was a temple of the sun there. Its greatest treasure was a black stone, similar to the stone in the sanctuary at Mecca and belonging to the same Arabian cult of the Black Light. A sacred well symbolized the Water of Life.

Later, a Christian church and then a mosque would continue the sacred tradition on the same site. On ancient Syrian coins the Black Stone, triangular in shape, appears together with the eagle, another symbol of the high Light of the heavens. Handing power to the priestly dynasty of Emesa, which ruled from 193 to 235 through Septimius Severus, his two sons, and his two great-nephews, was clearly an attempt to move the centre of imperial initiation from Egypt to Syria. It was another sign that the centre of gravity of the new world was shifting still farther east.

One detail is especially revealing: the princes of Emesa rarely lived in Rome. They divided their time between Libya, Syria, and Egypt. They also seem to have maintained peace with their Arsacid neighbours while giving their government a strongly universal and Arab character. Under them Egyptians entered the Senate. Caracalla, through his famous edict of 217, granted Roman citizenship to every free man in the Empire, finally removing the legal boundaries between East and West, ethnic groups, and religions.

His cousin Bassi El Gabal — Elagabalus — the high priest of the sun, brought the Black Stone to Rome and placed it in a temple built for it on the Palatine. His successor Alexander Severus, raised in Antioch by the great Christian theologian Origen, worshipped a holy Trinity made up of Abraham, Orpheus, and Jesus. His reign, from 222 to 235, was the swan song of the Egyptian-European political system and the announcement of Babylonian Asia’s return to the stage of history. Septimius Severus and the last Arsacids had already sensed that return.

The Arab emperors did not gradually turn away from Egypt and place the Roman Empire under the sign of the Arabian sun of Emesa out of sentiment. They did so because the Arsacids, carried forward by a Zoroastrian and Median revival, had restored the ancient Elamite religion and the language of the Persians, while Sanskrit was emerging on the borders of India. The Zoroastrian prophet Mani once again called people to worship a single god, Ahura, without temple or image, against the demon Angra Mainyu, or Ahriman.

Then came the decisive event of 226. Ardashir, son of Sassan, overthrew the Arsacid dynasty, which he regarded as still too attached to the Romano-Egyptian West. He seized Ctesiphon and brought 470 years of Parthian history to an end. He immediately declared himself the enemy of the Greeks and Romans and claimed the inheritance of the great Achaemenids Cyrus and Darius. The religious rapprochement attempted by the descendants of Septimius Severus did not satisfy him. He regarded them as usurpers, went to war against Alexander Severus, and eventually had him assassinated.

The classical Roman Empire now entered its death agony.

The Sassanids’ determination to destroy it took an apocalyptic form in old texts adapted to the new circumstances, such as the famous *Apocalypse of Hystaspes*, which announced the resurrection of the East and the death of Rome. Every material and spiritual force was thrown into this “battle of destiny.” For the first time, a strict state religion was established at Ctesiphon.

The prophet Mani began his passionate preaching in 241, on the very day Shapur I, Ardashir’s successor, was crowned. Claiming to have written the sacred books of the one God with his own hand, Mani appealed to three predecessors: Buddha, Zarathustra, and Jesus. Abraham, Orpheus, Isis, and Moses disappeared from the line. By modifying and adding vowels to Aramaic, and using Greek as a phonetic element, he set out to reshape religious thought through a new alphabet.

Condemned for heresy, Mani was beheaded, flayed, and hung at the gate of Ctesiphon. Yet he nevertheless became the first pontiff of the Sassanid Church, which completed the organization of the sacred book of the *Avesta* in the Zend alphabet and prepared to wage holy war against Jews, Christians, and the schools of Isis, Orpheus, and others that used either Greek or Aramaic. The Jews responded by creating a sacred language derived from Aramaic. Christianity remained with Greek, collected and organized the Gospel tradition, and approved the Greek text of the Septuagint as the sole version of the Old Testament.

These parallel efforts by Jews and Christians to consolidate their religious languages would not be completed until around the fifth century. The Sassanid cultural revolution of the third century was therefore an essential event in both universal and Arab history. It restored Asia to itself, strengthened the spiritual powers on both sides, and in a sense broke the bridge that Alexander and Caracalla had built between East and West.

The gods of Egypt now stopped at the Euphrates, while Buddhist preaching entered Mesopotamia. The name of Buddha’s mother, Maya, travelled as far as Egypt and came to be identified with Isis. In this third century, Buddhist monks preached at Ctesiphon under the missionary king Ashoka. On coins, Prince Peroz, brother of Shapur I, is shown worshipping Buddha. The ancestors of the famous Barmakid family of ministers, who would later govern Baghdad, had been Buddhist monks in Khorasan. The family then passed to Zoroastrianism and finally embraced Islam.

Nestorian Christians, specifically Asian Christians persecuted by Greco-Western Christianity, also found refuge among the Sassanids. From there they would set out to evangelize the Arabian Peninsula and the distant Mongol tribes. It is not difficult to see in Sassanid reform the first faint dawn of Islam. The excavations carried out by André Parrot at Dura-Europos, a key post in Syrian-Mesopotamian relations, reveal the scale of this political and cultural change.

Founded by the Seleucids under the protection of the dynastic gods Apollo, Zeus, and Artemis, the city took on an Arab-Assyrian character under the Parthians. Shamash, Hadad, Bel, and the goddess Nanaia appeared there. Then came Mithra, Yahweh, and Jesus, and finally the Palmyrene-Zoroastrian sun. Syria was turning eastward, toward Ctesiphon, now a brilliant and thoroughly Persian capital. Architecture and painting, freed from Greco-Egyptian mannerism, recovered the solemn, solitary, immense, and rigid character of ancient Assyrian figures. Byzantium would draw heavily on this style.

Plasterwork, stucco, ceramics, and chased bronze all revived. Their influence travelled far, even into China, where artists imitated them. The famous horses of Tang art seem almost to have galloped in from the banks of the Tigris.
