Archaeological work carried out by the Soviet government in the Yenisei region confirms this Sassanid influence, as do the treasures of Bulgaria and southern Russia. Even the art of rock relief, with its symmetries and layered cavalcades, was renewed by the artists of Ctesiphon.

The publication of Mani’s gospel brought confusion not only to established religions but to philosophy as well. Gnostic writings multiplied, along with interpretations, notes, and commentaries on the Old and New Testaments. At the same time Plotinus and his pupils — Amelius, Alexander of Assiut, and above all Porphyry — developed an entire metaphysical system directed against gnosis, the Manichaeans, Jews, and Christians.

They subjected all of them to a broad critique: exposing certain works attributed to Zoroaster as apocryphal, attacking the supposed genealogy of Christ, pointing out contradictions between the Evangelists and between the Apostles, and directing particular criticism at Saint Paul. Through the Neoplatonists, Egypt tried to regain control of the spiritual currents of the age. In vain. Egypt itself was now being carried along by the Asian tide.

The cities of southern Palestine, especially Jerusalem, which had “betrayed” the Egyptian-Roman cause and embraced that of the Arsacids, now found themselves logically on the Sassanid side. The Jewish communities made no secret of it. Inscriptions from the synagogue at Dura-Europos show that even before Shapur’s army conquered the region, Jewish priests were in communication with Ctesiphon. The same was true of the cities of the Arabian Peninsula. The Roman Empire was struggling with problems it could no longer solve.

In the East it was in serious trouble. In the West it could no longer stop the Germans and Dacians. Invaders crossed the Rhine and Danube constantly. Emperor Decius had been killed by the Goths in 251. Valerian had been unable to resist Shapur, who invaded Syria, captured Antioch, and carried the emperor away as a prisoner to Ctesiphon, where, after three years of captivity, he had him flayed. Once again a Syrian came to the rescue: Odaenathus, prince of Palmyra, defeated the Sassanids and saved the country.

But his wife, the famous Zenobia, sought an alliance with Ctesiphon. After Aurelian defeated her, she was shown a revealing degree of mercy.

The Empire was exhausted.

Diocletian deserves credit for restoring its administration, but not its unity. By then it was becoming obvious that the western provinces were little more than a vulnerable and relatively unimportant appendage. Between 284 and 305, Diocletian created an absolute, collegial monarchy of Eastern type, matching the absolute power of the Sassanids. Like them, he strengthened the state religion around the solar cult of Emesa. With the Edict of Nicomedia in 303, he is said to have begun a new period of persecution against Christians.

These persecutions are difficult to explain. They began under Nero and did not end until Constantine, lasting for more than two hundred years. Antiquity offers no other clear example of rulers attacking religious communities so persistently. Moreover, a number of Roman emperors had made no secret of their sympathy for Christianity. Cities were punished, not individuals, and no city was exclusively Christian. The very idea of punishing a person merely for an opinion was unknown to the age.

The Sassanids certainly regarded Christians as religious enemies, but they had little more affection for Jews. Yet they too persecuted Christians and not Jews, and even then they left Nestorian Christians untouched.

The mystery remains.

It has been claimed that Jews denounced Christians to the authorities. The accusation is particularly weak given how low Jewish political influence had fallen since Vespasian. Much is also made of a work called *Against the Christians*, supposedly written in the second century by a certain Celsus. Its charges — that Christians were “people without homeland or traditions,” held “secret meetings,” followed a “doctrine of barbarian origin,” and practised “magic” — are insubstantial and could hardly have shocked anyone in Rome.

The pamphlet also attacks Jews and Christians together. Filled with crude mistakes and unsupported claims, it reads more like a school exercise in rhetoric than a serious study. Everything suggests that the work is apocryphal, and it is hardly reasonable to rely on it. Even the supposed refutation by Origen, which some critics use to authenticate Celsus, is itself open to doubt. In any case, *Against the Christians* passed almost unnoticed, and the manuscript did not appear until the fourteenth century.

The often quoted correspondence between Pliny, governor of Bithynia, and the emperor Trajan does not help much either. On the subject of Christians it is administrative and largely uninformative. The fact remains that no government of classical antiquity normally presented itself as the intolerant champion of one religious system against another. Nor should we forget that under the Severan dynasty there were emperors who were Christian or close to Christianity, such as Philip the Arab, and bishops who were associated with political power.

If we rely only on doctrinal explanations, Diocletian’s exceptionally brutal reversal is therefore very hard to understand. Whatever the explanation, Diocletian failed to create a lasting state religion, and his collegial reform, the Tetrarchy, finished breaking the Roman Empire into pieces.

What did survive was a strict court protocol borrowed from Alexandria and the Sassanids, though even this was nothing new. The Antonines had already introduced the ceremonial homage of the ruler’s rising, formal presentation at court, the official body of ladies-in-waiting around the empress, the privilege of sending the imperial kiss from the fingertips, and ritual prostration. Diocletian added still greater splendour. Subjects approached the imperial throne on their knees. The emperor’s clothes were sewn with gold. His portrait on coins carried a halo.

He was addressed as “Majesty.”

# IX

## BYZANTIUM AND THE HOLY WARS

> “The ultimate achievement of celestial geometry.”

It fell to Constantine, son of Constantius Chlorus, Diocletian’s colleague, to do what circumstances had long been demanding: move the capital of the Empire east, where the emperors themselves were now spending most of their time. On 11 May 330, on the site of ancient Byzantium and one of the finest positions on the Bosporus, Constantine inaugurated what official documents called the “eternal city” and the “new Rome.” It took his name: Constantinople.

From these shores, where the great geopolitical currents of the ancient world converged, one can look back across history with unusual clarity while also seeing the future of the Mediterranean begin to take shape. The Danube routes, the Euphrates corridor, and the road of the Nile all directed the wealth of Europe, the Sino-Indian world, and Africa toward the same Arab-European meeting point: Constantinople.

Look long enough at the city’s position and the logic is obvious. The Black Sea, Crimea, the Caucasus, and the lands of southern Russia fall naturally within its imperial orbit. Until the cutting of the Suez Canal, the geographical and economic conditions governing the politics of the Old World would remain much the same as they had been under Constantine.

The movement of the commercial capital from Alexandria to Constantinople in the fourth century marked the growing importance of the Far East relative to Africa. It also showed that the Black and Caspian Seas, outlets for the provinces of Central Asia, were becoming the stronghold of a major civilization. The emperor in Constantinople was therefore, in a sense, a Danubian, Scythian, Russian, Arab, and Mongol prince all at once.

A crossroads of trade routes, Constantinople was also a crossroads of war. The Byzantine economy was tightly interwoven with that of the Sassanids, whose powerful organization controlled the routes of the East from Samarkand and Bukhara all the way to Ceylon. In a remarkable sixth-century book, the Alexandrian merchant Cosmas Indicopleustes mapped Sassanid commercial settlements in considerable detail.

Constantinople and Ctesiphon might fight one another, but they also needed one another. Each sought alliances with Goths, Egyptians, Arab cities, and Ethiopian cities, arming them heavily and in the process preparing the conditions of its own eventual ruin. Even the army of Shapur I, which had inflicted a humiliating defeat on the emperor Valerian, contained large numbers of Huns and Arabs from the peninsula.

The fourth century, under Constantine and then Constantius, Julian, and Theodosius the Great, was spent trying to preserve some appearance of unity in an empire geographically divided into four prefectures, to secure its frontiers, and, perhaps above all, to create a state religion capable of standing against the Sassanid Church. That was no easy task in lands where churches and doctrines were countless, endlessly varied, blended with one another, and divided by the finest shades of belief.

Constantine nevertheless tried. His mother, the empress Helena, was Christian and had travelled through Palestine searching for physical traces of the life of Christ. Constantine himself was no fervent disciple. He remained a catechumen throughout his life and was baptized only *in articulo mortis*, at the point of death. In the end, the *suprema divinitas* — the “supreme divinity” of philosophical paganism — probably did not seem very far removed from Christian teaching to him.

But he believed Christianity could serve as the religion of synthesis his political project required. In the Edict of Milan of 313 he therefore proclaimed freedom of Christian worship without formally outlawing the other religions, though he quietly closed a number of “pagan” temples.

The question remained: which Christianity?

Arius, a priest of Alexandria, stressed the human nature of Christ. Christ, he argued, was not God but a creature of God, and calling Mary *Theotokos* — Mother of God — was therefore a fraud. Another Alexandrian, Athanasius, taught the opposite: the absolute divinity of Christ, a thoroughly mystical position that lay outside rational categories. The difficulty, politically, was that many of Arius’s arguments resembled the teachings of Mani and the Sassanid Church — the enemy church. Arius therefore had to be condemned.

To achieve this, Constantine personally presided over the Council of Nicaea in Bithynia in 325 and delivered its opening address. Arius was ordered to recant. The bishops instead adopted the profession of faith associated with Athanasius, later sanctified by the Church:

> “We believe in one Lord, Jesus Christ, Son of God, the only-begotten Son of the Father. God from God, Light from Light, true God from true God, begotten, not made, of one substance with the Father.”

The Council also issued twenty canons, or disciplinary rules. Canon 6 recognized that the bishop of Alexandria possessed in Egypt powers and privileges comparable to those of the bishop of Rome in Italy. More strikingly, Canon 7 refused to grant the bishop of Aelia — the official name of Jerusalem — anything more than honorary precedence, while the bishops of Antioch and several other provinces retained unspecified “ancient rights.”

The Council was an enormous event.

For the first time west of the Euphrates, the state was trying to impose a religion on the population. This was a serious break with a Mediterranean tradition whose ecumenism and cosmopolitanism stretched back thousands of years. It is true that Constantine was only responding to the Sassanid authorities, the first to take this path. But the very idea of a council was revolutionary and strange. That priests — men of God — should gather in order to condemn other priests, or another form of God, seemed intolerable to much of the Arab East, where many saw the whole procedure as an invention of governments.

We are so accustomed to ideological and religious conflict that it is difficult for us to imagine the shock that followed the decisions of Nicaea. In the East they were understood as a double declaration of war: first against the Sassanids, then against Arab tradition itself. The year 325 therefore opens a new period of deep religious unrest. These conflicts were also national and popular reactions against an imperial power increasingly regarded as foreign.

Many followers of Arius left for Ctesiphon, where they were warmly received. Antioch obeyed the Council of Nicaea only reluctantly. The danger grew that Asia Minor might fall into the Sassanid orbit. To contain this separatist movement, Theodosius the Great called the Council of Constantinople in 381 and made a point of paying conspicuous tribute to Egypt. Arianism was condemned a second time. At the same time, accepting the arguments of the Alexandrian theologians, the Council proclaimed that the Spirit proceeded from the Father and the Son.

The doctrine of the Trinity had taken shape.

Alexandria’s prestige rose so high that its patriarch came to be seen as an heir of the pharaohs, worthy of a pontifical throne in the East with authority equal to that of the pope, the bishop of Rome. At the same time, the Arab world was being split more sharply in two along the Euphrates.

Between the imperial armies and the rulers of Ctesiphon, the battles grew longer and the truces shorter. And while the Empire fought abroad, it was being torn apart from within. The Council of Constantinople had intensified rebellion and encouraged the separatist mood in Antioch, whose bloody uprising Theodosius the Great had struggled to suppress.

The European provinces were meanwhile breaking apart under pressure from Germanic peoples. When Theodosius died in 395, his two sons divided the realm between them. Arcadius received Constantinople. The unfortunate Honorius was confined between Rome and Ravenna and was little more than an emperor in name.

From the fifth century onward, the great invasions overwhelmed the Roman territories. The Vandals settled in Spain and gave their name to Andalusia. From there they crossed into Africa with Constantinople’s permission and founded a kingdom under Genseric. The Goths occupied Italy, Gaul, and Illyria. Attila’s invasions, after he became king in 445, shattered what remained of Rome’s foundations. In 476 even the title of Western emperor disappeared. Only one empire remained: the empire of Constantinople, which would survive, through every upheaval, until 1453.

The West fragmented into small rival kingdoms ruled by Germanic tribal chiefs. The attempt to regroup them under Theodoric (455–526), brother-in-law of Clovis the Frank, led nowhere lasting. The East remained the key to the world. It possessed immense urban strength, major industries, river and port systems designed for control of the seas, and a durable economy served by millions of people organized into corporations whose traditions went back thousands of years.

Alexandria, Berytus — modern Beirut — Trebizond, Chalcedon, Corinth, Antioch, Laodicea, Thessalonica, Pergamum: city after city kept an illustrious tradition alive. The invasions that triumphed in the West broke against Byzantine defences in the East. The imperial army successfully held its ground against the Goths in Phrygia, the Huns advancing into Mesopotamia and Syria, and the Sassanids. In 413 Theodosius II built the great walls of Constantinople, parts of which still survive: six kilometres of fortifications, ninety-six towers, and ten gates — one of the finest monuments of Arab military architecture.

The deeper the Roman West sank into shadow, the more majestically the Arab East seemed to rise. But the trouble was already inside the system. Religious conflict became more violent than ever. Nestorius, the Syrian patriarch of Constantinople, was influenced by Ctesiphon and adopted the Christological theories of Arius. He argued that Jesus was only a created being. Against him stood Cyril, patriarch of Alexandria, a supporter of Monophysitism, a doctrine inherited from Athanasius.

With Rome’s support, the patriarchate of Alexandria imposed its mystical position at the two Councils of Ephesus, in 431 and 449, and secured the condemnation of the clergy of Antioch and Constantinople. The Egyptian triumph seemed so complete that, for a moment, people could imagine the Roman papacy being eclipsed — or even transferred to the banks of the Nile.

Then came something unprecedented. In 450 the ceremony of imperial coronation was instituted, and the emperor Marcian received his crown from the hands of the patriarch. Church and state, now solemnly joined, translated temporal power into divine law and made the subjects of the Empire followers of a religion devoted both to God and to Caesar. For the pope in Rome, this created an obvious danger: he could become little more than a servant of Constantinople and Alexandria.

At the Council of Chalcedon in 451, Pope Leo the Great therefore manoeuvred to have Monophysitism condemned, stripping Alexandria of much of the immense moral prestige it had gained. The Arab East felt this as a deep wound. Its peoples saw themselves as under spiritual assault from a West whose religious guardianship they could not accept. They believed themselves to be the true holders of a faith for which the West was merely a convert, while the East was its birthplace, home, and guardian.

Twice, then, Rome and the Empire had struck at the sacred spirit and mystery of the East: first by condemning the Syrian faith symbolized by Arius, then by publicly disgracing the Egyptian faith represented by the patriarchal tradition of Saint Athanasius and Saint Cyril. The resentment ran deep. Antioch and Alexandria, until then rivals, not only reconciled at Rome’s expense but also turned toward the third great Eastern church: the Zoroastrian Sassanid Church of Ctesiphon.

By the middle of the fifth century, then, we can already see the beginnings of a more or less open religious and popular coalition between the Egyptian and Babylonian worlds. It would later become part of the cement of Islam: the revenge of Aramean society on Latin dogmatism. With the reign of Justinian, who ruled from 518 until his death in 565, the attack on the European West — now treated as a disruptive outsider — became broader and more deliberate.

The empress Theodora was herself an avowed opponent of Latin influence. Throughout the long reign she supported her husband’s attempt to restore something like Seleucid power. The problem was that Justinian wanted to subordinate both Egypt and the Sassanids. He was certainly willing to unify the Arab world, but only under his own rule and under the decrees of the Council of Chalcedon. He underestimated popular traditions and the very nature of an East that was tolerant and deeply resistant to rigid divisions.

In 425 he founded the University of Constantinople, where the fifteen chairs of Greek now outnumbered the thirteen chairs of Latin. In the same year, the University of Alexandria was stripped of its privileges. In 529 the University of Athens was closed. The sanctuaries of Amun and Isis in Libya and at Philae were cleared by the police. The Monophysites remained a larger problem. They had the support of the patriarch of Egypt but had been condemned by the Council of Chalcedon.

Determined to be master in his own house, Justinian unleashed violent persecution against them: public executions, exile, armed attacks. “Heretics” were barred from public office.

But the preaching continued.

Popular resistance to Justinian’s union of imperial and religious power was extremely strong. Theodora secretly supported Egyptian Monophysitism and received priests wanted by the police, hiding them in the palace. With her protection, Jacob Baradaeus secretly organized the Jacobite Church, which still survives today.

Meanwhile the deserts from the Nile to Armenia filled with hermits and monasteries. The mystical intensity of the sixth century carried Christianity down a path largely unknown to the Church of the Apostles: solitary contemplation. Orphism, Buddhism, and Zoroastrian serenity all contributed strongly to this new religious attitude. A first monastery had already been founded around 330 at Tabennisi in Upper Egypt — the “Palm Grove of Isis” — by a monk from Qena named Pachomius. His sister founded the first convent for women.

In Jerusalem, the famous hermit Saint Sabbas set down the monastic rule in the *Typikon*. In 534 the Rule of Saint Benedict was written. The Empire was full of monasteries. As many as sixty-seven could be found in the province of Constantinople alone. All of them became centres of resistance to Justinian: cells of popular teaching into which Arab and Far Eastern spiritual traditions flowed together.

Alongside the official, conformist churches governed by the palace and the upper patrician classes, there seemed to arise something like revolutionary parties built around a religion of the heart — light on dogma, rich in feeling, half wrapped in legend and shadow. Monks and senior clergy watched one another suspiciously, just as town watched country and rich watched poor. Revolt was always close to the surface. Behind every hermit lurked a potential rebel.

Against Rome, against the pope, against imperial power, against episcopal dogmatism, the Aramean people plotted or fought in the name of Christ — but also out of loyalty to a religious world older than the Gospel, steeped in Osirian, Jewish, and antediluvian faith, and already disposed toward Islam. The endless wars against the Sassanid Persians kept this popular unrest alive. Fields were destroyed, peasants fled, taxpayers sank into poverty, and Ctesiphon’s gold bought loyalties. Again and again the urban poor were driven into open revolt against the Empire.

During the winter of 532–533, Justinian was besieged in his own palace and survived only because General Belisarius trapped and massacred more than twenty thousand rioters in the Hippodrome of Constantinople. And yet, on the central question, Justinian still had much in common with the peoples of Asia. He shared their contempt for Rome and for a West that had by then become Germanic. His armies reconquered the Vandal kingdom in Tunisia, Sicily, Naples, and Rome, and broke up the territories held by Theodoric’s heirs.

He eventually signed a “perpetual peace” with Khosrow, the Sassanid king, agreeing to pay him tribute in return for his trading posts at the foot of the Caucasus. Then he turned against the pope of Rome. At the Fifth Ecumenical Council of Constantinople in 553, Justinian humiliated Pope Vigilius and forced him to reverse his own position. The unfortunate pope was denounced throughout the West, where part of the episcopate went into schism.

The West had already been politically ruined by Justinian’s transfer of the capital from Rome to Ravenna. Now it lost its spiritual solidarity as well. Vigilius’s two successors, Pelagius and John III, elected under instructions from imperial officials, became little more than obedient servants of the palace. After Theodora’s death, however, Justinian moved closer to the Monophysites, the monks, and even the Arians. He now tried to reconcile the whole East within a single church more in keeping with its traditions.

He renewed relations with Egypt, multiplied conferences and theological discussions, and worked constantly toward religious unity. To achieve it, he had already abandoned the orthodoxy established by earlier councils and was no longer afraid to make terms with “heresy.” In 565, the year of his death, he issued an edict taking the side of the dissident churches. But by then imperial authority had discredited itself through too much manoeuvring between rival camps. Justinian’s goodwill was met only with suspicion or anger.

The reign ended in confusion.

For all that, the emperor had restored the culture and art of the Arab East to their full splendour. His great legislative achievement, the Justinianic Code, was an immense compilation divided into twelve books in imitation of the Roman Twelve Tables. It largely gave formal recognition to customs that had existed in the East since remote antiquity. The *Corpus Juris Civilis* would become a guide for later legal systems. Drawing on Egyptian, Babylonian, and Palestinian law and on their Roman derivative, it would support the societies of the caliphs as well as the great monarchies of the West.

It recognized that all human beings were naturally equal and free; that inheritance law should make no distinction between the sexes; that a woman should be protected against divorce through an *ante nuptias* gift; and that the family was the fundamental unit of society. At the same time it affirmed, without ambiguity, the absolute power of the state, giving the state its own legal definition and identifying it with the person of the monarch. In this way, the pharaonic idea of power was transmitted to the states that followed.

If the Napoleonic Code would later be borrowed by the capitals of the modern East, then the debt was simply being repaid: the Napoleonic Code was itself a condensed form of ancient pharaonic and Justinianic law. The revival of Arab letters under Justinian is an important chapter in the wider history of civilization. Greek was used alongside Aramaic, which among educated writers had developed into what was called “Syriac,” but in everyday popular speech was simply Arabic.

Nearly all the great names of Justinianic literature were Palestinian or Syrian. Procopius, the greatest historian of the age, came from Caesarea. John Malalas, author of a universal history reaching back to the origins of the world, was a monk of Antioch. John of Ephesus wrote an *Ecclesiastical History* in Syriac. A whole flowering of poets, hymn writers, and songwriters anticipated the brilliant Umayyad age and treated the same themes in Greek or Syriac.

Leontius, the century’s greatest theologian, may have been from Byzantium, but the majority of writers came from Gaza, Edessa, Nisibis, and Berytus. From Berytus, for example, came distinguished professors of law such as Dorotheus and Anatolius. Other names can be added almost at random: Paul the Silentiary, Romanos, Corippus, John Lydus, and many more.

The scale of Syria’s contribution to world culture in this period is poorly understood today. No less forgotten is the fact that Constantinople, having replaced Alexandria as a great centre for the transmission of thought and art, projected its influence deep into Europe, Asia, and Africa. This influence had old roots. Since the beginning of the twentieth century, Louis Bréhier and Paul Scheffer-Boichorst, writing in the *Byzantinische Zeitschrift*, had drawn attention to the impact of Syrian thought on the West.

At least seven Arab popes governed the early Church: Saint Anicetus (155–166), Saint John V (685–686), Saint Sergius I (687–701), Saint Sisinnius (708), Saint Constantine (708–715), Saint Gregory III (731–741), and Leo III. There was hardly an oasis or city in the Arabian Peninsula untouched by this remarkable culture. Its architectural summary and highest expression was surely Hagia Sophia.

With all the tradition embodied in it, Hagia Sophia casts a revealing light across the history of art. It helps explain the inner harmonies of Romanesque architecture; it helps explain Venice and Tuscany; it gives meaning to the forms of Saint Peter’s in Rome, the extraordinary cathedral of Altamura in Apulia, and the churches of Le Puy, La Chaise-Dieu, Fontevraud, and many others. Through Hagia Sophia, in all its majesty and hypnotic power, we can grasp the secret of Arab architecture as it flowered in the West and transformed it.

The central theme of Hagia Sophia — and of Arab art in general — is the simultaneous presence of heaven and earth, brought together in a ceremony in which the building itself takes the place of prayer. There is love and pride in everything humanity can make splendid; a preference for large architectural plans and smooth, rounded surfaces in the Egyptian manner; a passion for order; an intimate knowledge of geometry. No convolutions, no frivolity, no waking dreams. “No architecture has known how to call heaven down to itself as Arab architecture has,” the Egyptian architect Hassan Fathy observed.

Justinian regarded Hagia Sophia as the work of his life and as an expression of Syro-Asian greatness. He entrusted it to two architects, Anthemius of Tralles and Isidore of Miletus, both pupils of the Syrian tradition. They created something miraculous. Completed in five years, Hagia Sophia was inaugurated in December 537. When the great dome collapsed in 558, it was rebuilt and completed in 563. For more than fourteen centuries it has seemed to defy gravity, a triumph still unsurpassed. In *De Aedificiis*, Procopius calls it “the work of divinity itself.”

Consider the sight for a moment. The great dome, thirty-one metres across, floats fifty metres above the floor. Four arches carry it, braced by two half-domes, which in turn rest on three semicircular niches. The interior decoration must have been dazzling: mosaics, gold, silk, carpets, and enamel flashing like jewellery. Long, bare, deserted walls outside; splendour within. An image of the soul.

Byzantine art already anticipates Islamic meditation because it is Arab art. It understands that the most splendid souls are desert souls: souls that have emptied the world around them so that the breath of God can move freely through the space. The origins of the dome, symbol of the vault of heaven, and the techniques used to support it through round arches and squinches can be traced through Babylonian and Nile architecture, the ruins of Marib in Yemen, Ctesiphon, the Pantheon in Rome, and the synagogues of Damascus.

The Syrians brought the dome to perfection. On a truly monumental scale in Europe, it would not appear again until Brunelleschi in Florence. The most celestial form of the dome is the one placed over an octagonal base — a square whose corners have been cut away — using squinches, small corbelled sections of vault that carry the transition from one shape to the other. This is the ultimate achievement of a technique inspired by celestial geometry.

Its masterpiece is the *Qubbat al-Sakhra*, the Dome of the Rock, often incorrectly called the Mosque of Omar, built in Jerusalem by the Umayyad caliph Abd al-Malik in 691 around the stone associated with Abraham’s sacrifice. But the model for this Muslim sanctuary was itself a Byzantine building: the Ghassanid cathedral of Bosra.

From the granaries of the Ramesseum, dating to Egypt’s Nineteenth Dynasty, to the Sassanid arch at Ctesiphon; through Byzantine fortresses, Hagia Sophia, and the houses of Carthage; and onward to the Moorish palaces of Granada and Toledo, the mosque of Kairouan, the Norman palaces of Palermo, and the cathedrals of Venice and Burgundy, we find a single art. The dome reigns, and the octagon triumphs. This is characteristically Arab art, and Hagia Sophia expresses it perfectly.

Sicily carried this art into Tuscany. Ravenna and Venice introduced it into Lombardy and Piedmont. The Crusades carried it directly into France and the rest of Europe. Translated into painting, the same tradition produced the great works of the early Sienese painters, Giotto, and Piero della Francesca: colour gathered into firm, architectonic volumes wrapped in stillness and silence; calm stretches of sky resting above faces freed from psychological expression and bodies freed from anatomical ugliness.

A kind of frozen purity, suggesting a crystalline world beyond this one, links a painting by Piero della Francesca to the epic mosaics of Ravenna, where halos crown the figures with eternity and pupil-less eyes search the invisible. Hagia Sophia and Byzantine art come from very far away. They were born from thousands of years of effort and meditation whose inheritance Egypt and Asia carried and passed on. There is no Muslim art or Christian art, any more than there is Jewish, Nestorian, Protestant, Orthodox, or heretical art.

Hagia Sophia, Saint Mark’s in Venice, and Saint Peter’s in Rome could just as easily be mosques or synagogues. The bell towers of Pisa and Torcello are simply minarets under another name.

Where is the difference?

All these monuments share a style and a purpose. They are Arab, and they are designed to carry human beings beyond themselves. The point is precisely that Islam did not transform the inner harmony of the East. It entered that world, respected it, and gave it new faith and new strength. We should be careful not to lose ourselves in rationalist legends that endlessly divide human thought and draw borders where none ever existed.

The history of an art is not the history of religious wars. Nor does it simply follow chronicles or applied sciences. We cannot make mosque architecture begin with Islam, or non-figurative art begin with the *Qur’an*. Art is created by the natural movement of people and things, by the cohesion of societies, and by their sense of solidarity across the centuries. Islam did not alter the mentality of the East. It preserved and strengthened it. The physical and intellectual structures of Arab society did not suddenly change.

The clearest proof is the Dome of the Rock itself: the architects and masons who built it were Christians. The new faith therefore took possession of the Arab world’s essential aesthetic language and carried it to the fullest expression of its truth. The permanent common foundation of Aramean civilization, stretching from the Nile to the Indus, remained intact under Isis, just as it would remain intact under the law of Jesus or Muhammad.

Because academic scholarship failed to recognize this continuity, it created artificial classifications that have done more harm than good. A little more modesty in our scholarly ambitions might have produced a less partisan understanding of Romanesque and Gothic art. Because the faith inside a church appears different from the faith inside a mosque, we have insisted that their architecture must also be different. But it is not. We have to accept that the Arab artist inspired our churches just as he inspired synagogues, mosques, and Orphic or Zoroastrian sanctuaries.

Art has no denominational homeland, because a religion is not a homeland. Neither is a political party. As far as we know, there is no such thing as Marxist art or Fascist art either. Unless we deliberately cultivate paradox, the opposite is difficult to defend. Simple common sense therefore leads us to reject the very category of “Muslim art” and to recognize instead the historical reality: Arab art. The East carries Islam as the sea carries a wave. The millions of particles that raise that wave come from depths in which all the waters have mixed.

Hagia Sophia, church and mosque, is a jewel of Arab art. For the East it is a culmination; for the West, a dawn. From the banks of the Euphrates and Tigris to the banks of the Arno and Seine, one artistic current has flowed without interruption. To complete the picture of Byzantine achievement in the fine arts, we would also have to discuss its workshops in gold and silk; its ceramics, leather, glass, jewellery, chased metal, and spices. But that would only repeat the lists already associated with the Babylonian and Seleucid empires, or anticipate those in which the empire of the caliphs would later take pride.

One fact is enough to show Byzantium’s prestige: in the sixth century, coins struck in its mints were sought after in financial centres across the known world. The Justinianic dynasty ended amid murderous wars on the Danube and Euphrates, complicated by invasions and military rebellions supported by Slavs or encouraged by the Persians. One such rebellion, led by the officer Phocas, ended in the massacre of the imperial family and eventually brought Heraclius, exarch of Carthage, to power. He was a great blond soldier with a nervous temperament.

He would reign from 610 to 641 and witness the collapse of the great empire built by Constantine and Justinian. When Heraclius came to the throne, the young Muhammad was growing up among his family in Mecca, while two grave crises were gathering over the Empire: an unforgiving war with Persia and a ruinous conflict between religions. The armies of Heraclius and Khosrow marched and counter-marched across the region, devastating the land as they tried to destroy one another. The Persians seized Armenia, Damascus, Jerusalem, and Egypt, helped by populations in revolt against the emperor’s papal pretensions.

Heraclius, accompanied by Patriarch Sergius, struck back through the Black Sea region, entered Sassanid territory, ravaged the countryside, destroyed numerous cities, and reached the walls of Ctesiphon. The Sassanid army was not intimidated. In a daring countermove, it raced toward Constantinople and laid siege to the capital in 626. The fighting was extraordinarily savage: beneath the walls, in the harbour, through the suburbs, and even in the residential districts of the city. The urban poor helped the invaders.

Constantinople was saved only by the extraordinary energy of Patriarch Sergius. A statue of the Virgin was carried along the ramparts through praying processions, and from the crisis grew a legend of the Mother of God as protector and saviour of the city. Sergius composed a hymn in her honour, and it has passed from century to century into modern Orthodox liturgy.

Constantinople had looked death in the face.

The Sassanids were also on the edge of disaster. They made peace and returned to Heraclius the fragment of the True Cross they had held since the sack of Jerusalem. When the emperor returned to his city in 629, he received an unforgettable welcome, remembered by the chroniclers in dazzling terms. That same year, Egypt was watching another event, less spectacular but far more consequential: the prophet Muhammad entered Mecca. He had been born, it is said, around 580 into the Meccan aristocracy, and he possessed a profound faith in the voices of angels.

We will say no more about that. Scholarship cannot explain such a thing; it can only report it. The birth of a religion reaches depths into which analysis cannot descend. The illumination Muhammad received on the edge of the desert was powerful enough to compel him to preach and call his people to God.

For three centuries, fierce disputes over the exact nature of Christ and the unity of God had torn consciences apart. These arguments had never been confined to Asia Minor and Egypt. They were discussed everywhere — in Mecca, Petra, and Yemen — and with even greater passion because religious debate was entangled with political resentment between the peoples of the Arab East and the ambitions of the Germanic West. The Meccans, scandalized by Muhammad’s preaching, considered putting him to death. Before the authorities could act, Muhammad left Mecca in 622 and took refuge in Yathrib.

In memory of this departure, the Hijra, Yathrib would later be called *Madinat al-Nabi* — the City of the Prophet — or simply Medina. This exile coincided with Heraclius’s successful campaign against the Persians of Khosrow. Some have tried to make Muhammad a disciple of the Jews or the Nestorians. That misses the larger picture. Jews and Nestorians themselves were only elements within the endlessly complex currents of Eastern religious life.

# X

## THE PEACE OF ISLAM

> “Before Abraham was, I am.”
>
> *(Gospel according to Saint John, 8:58.)*
>
> “Before the Qur’an came to us, we were already Muslims.”
>
> *(Qur’an, Surah 28, verse 53.)*

We should not look for the roots of Muhammad’s central ideas in the teaching of some particular monk or rabbi. He found his path in a vast, tangled mystical forest alive with voices, echoes, and resonances thousands of years old: one path, a divine path, a royal path. Like everyone else in the Empire, he was consumed by one great question: the unity of God and the unity of the people.

The theologians of Rome, Alexandria, Antioch, and Constantinople had spent generations arguing over it, reasoning, analysing, and defining. Muhammad refused to follow their method. In a simple and decisive act of faith, he stepped beyond the limits of the dispute and went straight to what he saw as the indivisible reality: God is one. In doing so, he took a side in the long conflict between the Arab churches and the theologians of Rome and Byzantium. This won him the support of many ordinary Christians, among whom Qur’anic teaching would for a long time blend with a particular understanding of Christ.

There was surely, in the Prophet’s position, a desire to bring the Byzantine debates to an end — the disputes that had exhausted Arius and Athanasius, Nestorius and Cyril, Theodora and Saint Sabas. Muhammad was a man of his time and his society, and he dealt with the problems of that time and society. He was also a man of action and decision. He wanted to give the religious crisis a radical solution through a teaching addressed to everyone in the Empire who had been troubled by theological uncertainty.

Because he spoke to everyone, everyone listened to him — especially bishops and churchmen who had taken part in the various councils. Many saw him as a kind of Christian. Traditional commentators, working from an exotic picture of the Arab world and an unforgivable ignorance of its history, have unfortunately tried to turn Muhammad into a dreamer and his preaching into a kind of rural theosophy. The facts tell a different story.

Muhammad’s voice was heard from the beginning in drawing rooms and churches, among educated and informed circles, in ministries and imperial offices, as well as in the countryside. Many of the finest minds of the age embraced his message enthusiastically because they heard in it a call of great metaphysical power. The proof is the extraordinary speed with which the cities of the East converted to it, as though they were embracing a familiar doctrine that gathered the fundamental beliefs of Eastern culture into one simple synthesis.

How are we supposed to believe that Islam conquered, by military force alone and with a handful of horsemen, a territory stretching from the Indus to the Garonne? Is it really credible that powerful capitals that had made Goths and Huns, Persians and Scythians tremble suddenly fell to their knees at the first neigh of a horse from the Hijaz? No one should be expected to believe such a story. The truth needs to be put right. Our pupils and students deserve better than invented history.

When Muhammad returned to Mecca in 629, the influence of his person and his ideas had already reached the Empire’s vital centres. He had recently sent ambassadors to Khosrow, to Heraclius, to the king of Abyssinia, and to the patriarch of Alexandria. It was in Egypt that the reception was warmest — and most significant. Egypt was about to give Muhammad the means and political support needed for his success. The reasons take us back to a strategy we have already seen many times. Egypt had guided Thutmose and Darius, and later the Ptolemies and Seleucids.

To present Islam as a sudden, improvised adventure; to make Muhammad into a figure somehow lost in a world that was not his own; to detach his life and work from the full history of the East — this would be to reduce the hard history of humanity to a fairy tale. The strength and success of his mission place him, on the contrary, at the very centre of the concerns that gripped seventh-century society, often to the point of anguish. He is its dominant figure. And if he succeeded, it was because he was expected.

His spontaneous inspiration met the hope of the Aramean peoples that they might be strengthened against violation, domination, and contamination. The success of Islam was therefore the irresistible meeting of a call and a hope. At that time Egypt was both the only power in the East that remained intact and the homeland of Aramean resistance to the Byzantine-Roman drive for domination.

Constantinople had certainly moved a long way from Rome and had become far more Eastern in character. Yet its major interests in the West still forced it to work with the Roman Church, especially in defending the doctrine of the Trinity. After coming into sharp conflict with Egypt, Constantinople had pushed it out of imperial decision-making while deliberately turning its own economy northward and toward the Far East. But there it ran into the Sassanids. The long and bitter struggle left both the Byzantine and Sassanid worlds exhausted and unstable.

At the same time, from the beginning of the seventh century, political upheavals had disturbed the stability of China and India and badly damaged both Sassanid and Byzantine trade. From 605 onward, China was shaken by widespread revolt, accompanied by military interventions by the Yang dynasty in Persia, India, Indochina, and Korea. These campaigns ended in disaster. Turkish forces then invaded Chinese provinces. The reaction helped bring the warlike Tang dynasty to power, and the Tang eventually drove them back.

India, meanwhile, had to face the same Toul-Kiné Turks. The Vardhana dynasty, established in the Punjab after defeating the invaders, strengthened Buddhism and closed its frontiers to the Sassanids, who were allied with the Turks. The later break-up of the Vardhana dynasty plunged south-western Asia into chaos without improving matters for Ctesiphon. It is difficult to understand the rapid diplomatic collapse of Byzantium and Ctesiphon — that is, the victory of Muhammad and the caliphs — without taking account of the troubled history of Central Asia in the seventh century.

Egypt, by contrast, had stayed out of the wars around the Euphrates, the Black Sea, and the Caspian. Trade through Iran and the Persian Gulf had been disrupted, but the Red Sea routes remained relatively prosperous. This benefited Egypt and the merchant cities running from Aden to Petra. Alexandria had seen its temples closed by order of Constantinople, its university ruined, and its religious ambitions smothered by the combined actions of Rome and Constantinople. It now felt that the time for revenge had come.

It regarded Heraclius much as the Ptolemies had once regarded the Seleucids. Alexandria had come close to securing the papacy; it had produced martyrs and distinguished patriarchs; it had founded the great monastic orders of Sinai; and it had not forgotten that the empire of the Caesars had grown in the shadow of the Ptolemies and with their financial support.

It therefore embraced Muhammad’s cause instinctively.

Alexandria placed its credit, economic strength, and cultural influence at his disposal. Above all, it rallied around his name the countless followers of the Monophysite and Nestorian churches — deeply “nationalist” and Arab churches that had repeatedly suffered from the cruelty or clumsiness of Constantinople. What brought Islam its great success was a genuine popular uprising, not the technique of set-piece battles. At the head of that uprising stood Egypt, whose firms and the trading houses of Mecca had long been linked as branches and business partners.

If Muhammad’s spiritual victory came from a divine sign, his worldly victory came through the intervention of Egypt. The timing is striking. Muhammad’s preaching was contemporary with the Monothelite experiment. This policy, led by the Byzantine patriarch Sergius and Honorius, bishop of Rome, developed between 616 and 641 — almost exactly the same period as Muhammad’s public teaching, from around 620 until his death in 632.

Sergius wanted to break Egypt’s practical hold over the population through Monophysitism. As early as 616, he tried to make a major concession to the popular Monophysite tradition by adopting the idea of a “single will,” intended to modify the doctrine of God in three persons. The Armenian Church agreed, followed by the Church of Antioch, and then Rome. To bring the Alexandrian clergy into line as well, Constantinople installed a new patriarch there, Cyrus, whose skill initially succeeded. In 638, a council in Constantinople approved the text Sergius had written on the “single will,” known as the *Ecthesis*.

But the appearance of unity concealed deep and irreconcilable resistance. Sophronius, patriarch of Jerusalem and Palestine, refused to sign and became fiercely hostile. The Egyptian Monophysites, briefly attracted by the compromise, decided that Sergius had not gone far enough and demanded the repeal of the decrees of the Council of Chalcedon. Cyrus responded with harsh police measures that went as far as persecution. After the death of Pope Honorius, his successor Severinus rejected the *Ecthesis*. John IV went further and had it condemned by a Roman synod.

The crisis remained acute until about 645.

This timeline matches almost exactly the time Islam needed to win over the East. In 630 Muhammad entered Mecca for the second time, now as the undisputed victor, and proclaimed at the Kaaba the faith in the one God. In 635 the bishop of Damascus handed the keys of the city to Caliph Umar. In 636, at the Battle of the Yarmouk, Syrian troops rebelled against the Byzantine general Theodore the Sacellarius. They killed him, demanded the emperor’s removal, and joined Umar’s envoys.

In 638 Sophronius, bishop of Jerusalem, welcomed the caliph as a friend and ally. Antioch did the same. In 640 the Armenian Church reached an agreement with Islam, bringing the province of Mosul with it. At the end of 641, Cyrus, patriarch of Alexandria, gave the kiss of peace to the Muslim general Amr and opened the road into Libya. By 647 the authority of Caliph Uthman reached almost to Carthage.

Islam’s advance toward the Indus was no less dramatic. A single battle was enough to break the Sassanid Empire. Defeated at Qadisiyya, a small town about thirty kilometres from Kufa in Iraq, the ruler of Ctesiphon fled and was eventually killed in 651 in the mountains of Khorasan.

But who actually defeated the Persians at Qadisiyya? The small Muslim army?

Not at all.

The Persians faced a mixed force that included Christian Syrians, equally Christian Ghassanids, Byzantine soldiers who had broken with the Empire, and contingents of tribal cavalry. Before the battle, men in the same camp followed different rites: some attended Mass, while others listened to Qur’anic prayer. The populations of Mesopotamia and Iran, moreover, welcomed as brothers the entirely peaceful “invasion” of their southern and western neighbours.

Who, then, was defeated at Qadisiyya?

Only the Sassanid dynasty.

Everywhere, popular celebration greeted liberation. Messengers sent from Alexandria, Antioch, Jerusalem, and Mecca rushed from place to place carrying the new message. Aramean solidarity worked everywhere against an exhausted Byzantine enemy. “Heretical” solidarity mattered just as much, because North Africa and Spain contained large Arian, Montanist, and Monophysite populations already in rebellion against the Empire.

Byzantium’s economic decline was also driving away many of its former allies. The new power established in Mesopotamia and along the Nile attracted them more strongly because it had restored relations with the Far East and signed agreements with India and China that the Sassanids had been unable to renew. In 662 Kabul was thus brought back into the Nile-Babylonian empire. None of this required organized “Arab” armies to march bodily across the continent.

Yet our history teachers stretch their imaginations to the limit and show us enormous squadrons of Allah’s horsemen sweeping from the Indus to the Garonne, crossing mountain ranges, deserts, and chains of fortresses as though by magic.

If Carthage surrendered in 698; if the rich and powerful Visigothic kingdom of Spain passed within seven years into allegiance to the caliphate; if Narbonese Gaul became “Arab” in 720; if Sind and the Punjab sought Umayyad protection in 711 — it was because these regions were moving toward a newly restored source of wealth and security: the Nile-Mesopotamian empire rebuilt under the banner of the one God, meaning Islam together with “heretical” Christianity.

From then on, a great east-west system, running roughly parallel to the equator, carried regular convoys of raw materials and manufactured goods from the China Sea to the coast of Portugal and back again. Alexandria and Damascus resumed their traditional role as bankers to the world, until Baghdad rose as an even greater capital. Between 673 and 677 the caliphs laid siege to Constantinople year after year in an effort to finish off the Empire. But in 739 the Umayyad caliph Hisham, after invading Asia Minor and reaching the Bosporus, was crushed at the Battle of Akroinon with the help of troops from Europe.

From that point onward, Byzantines and caliphs would live on relatively good terms and within a shared cultural world until the Turkish victory. If, in the seventh and eighth centuries, the empire of the caliphs stretched from the Pyrenees to India and absorbed, to varying degrees, Aquitaine, Spain, Sicily, southern Italy, and the Balkans, it was not because the “Arabs” had supposedly conquered all these lands *manu militari*. It was because these regions had traditionally belonged to the economic and cultural sphere of the old Aramean empires.

The Arameans, whom later ages would call Arabs, felt at home there whether those territories were ruled by the pharaohs, the Ptolemies, Darius, Byzantium, or the caliphs. The populations themselves saw neither their language nor their civilization fundamentally change over the centuries. Arab they were; Arab they remained. No rupture, no divorce. Thus, in eleventh-century Cairo, we find the sister of the bishop of Jerusalem marrying the Fatimid sultan al-Aziz. Foreign occupations had done little to alter the essentials.

Saint Augustine tells us that Aramaic was in common use in North Africa in his own time. The same was true in Spain, and if the language of the Iberian Peninsula remains so rich in Arabic words, it cannot be because of a few ghostly horsemen under Tariq ibn Ziyad. When those horsemen landed beyond the Pillars of Hercules, they found people whose speech was already much like their own, as was the speech of Moors, Numidians, Carthaginians, and Libyans.

Saint Augustine himself spoke Aramaic as his native language. Augustine tells us that when the peasants in his diocese of Hippo — today Bône, or Annaba — were asked what they were, they answered in Punic that they were Canaanites: “*interrogati rustici nostri quid sint punice respondent CHANANI*.”[^pierre-rossi-lci-011]

There was therefore never an Arab conquest or Arab domination in the usual sense. What happened was that the peoples of the eastern and southern Mediterranean, now grouped under the name “Arabs,” openly recovered a political sovereignty that had been exercised from the age of Alexander to the seventh century by dynasties foreign to their own soil. Do early medieval manuscripts not use the names Arabs or Saracens for Mediterranean peoples who were neither Germanic nor followers of Rome’s religion?

*The Song of Roland* describes the fighting at Roncevaux as a battle against Saracens. Yet those “Saracens” were Basques. More curiously still, Christians in Africa and Spain, when distinguishing themselves from Roman Catholics, called themselves *Punici christiani* — that is, “Christians of Palestine.” In the age of Charlemagne, and even after him, there were therefore “Arab Christians” and “Roman Christians.” This throws a sharp light on some of history’s darkest corners and challenges the foundations of what is taught in our schools.

The prophet Muhammad and the caliphs therefore gave the East back to itself. They restored it to the one Divinity in a form so convincing that all the religions and metaphysical traditions of the East could recognize themselves within it and merge into it. They also restored the Aramaic language, since the Qur’an brought the ancient speech of the Nile-Mesopotamian peoples to its highest perfection of sound, meaning, and syntax. Arabic is in fact the first fully organized language of Mediterranean humanity, predating the language of Homer and giving it its rules.

Since the Prophet’s call awakened it to modern life, Arabic has risen out of the depths of the ages, carrying with it their monumental echoes, and has become the language of hundreds of millions of people. Through Arabic, we Europeans can read our own Scriptures and our own history anew. We will see them far more clearly. Knowing Arabic will help us not only to move beyond the narrow horizon of Athens and Rome and rediscover the immense spaces of an East that is still alive, but also to take a full part in the future society now emerging from the uncertainties around us.

Of that we can be certain.

In the same way, the Arab world, if it is to recover the thread linking it to the West, must turn again to Greek culture, because Greek is the unique bridge between East and West. The day Arab universities restore the study of Greek to a place of honour, and Europe discovers the treasure of Arab culture, the two sides of the arch will meet again in a Mediterranean reconciliation that will be more than architectural. Such a reconciliation would restore the continuity of civilization broken in the middle of the eighth century by the divorce between Rome and the East.

The bishops of Rome saw themselves as the sole heirs of the Caesars, while ignoring the true character of those emperors, who had often been more Eastern than European. In the name of the apostle Peter, they tried to bring the East under their authority. They had just suffered a serious defeat. Through their own inflexibility, they had succeeded in bringing Eastern Christianity, Islam, and Judaism together into a single national force — three religions Arab in language and tradition. They did not accept defeat. Instead they began a long campaign of reconquest through doctrine, politics, and war.

To do this they needed the support of a secular power, and they chose the Germanic Carolingian family. The year 754 was therefore a decisive and ominous date in European history. Pepin the Short and Pope Stephen II agreed to establish a Papal State under Carolingian protection. The Byzantine cities of Italy passed under Romano-Germanic rule, while those that remained within Constantinople’s orbit were now treated as enemies. By this act of separation, the Latin Church — though it could not deny its Aramean origins — placed its future in the hands of the Germanic peoples.

Arabic and Greek were pushed aside in favour of Latin. Catholic clerics made it their mission to glorify the role of the Roman Empire, pushing Palestine, Babylon, Egypt, and indeed all of Asia to the margins of history and shrinking our field of vision to Europe alone. Out of this emerged the Holy Roman Empire: a strange collection of contradictory words that was already a provocation in itself.

The doctrinal alliance between the princes of Europe and the Roman papacy was soon joined by an economic, political, and military alliance. Its consequences are familiar: the condemnation of everything classed as Arab — Jews, various “heretics,” Cathars, Spaniards, Provençals, Sicilians labelled Saracens for the purposes of the conflict — the Crusades, the sack of Byzantium, colonial expeditions, missionary campaigns, attempts to set Eastern Christians against one another or unite them against Islam, and efforts to persuade some Jewish communities to side with a Germanized Rome against the Aramean East, despite the paradox involved.

This list goes a long way toward explaining the kind of relationship that, for twelve centuries, set the Eastern world against a Europe that had deliberately broken away from it in 754. Seen from this angle, the true nature of the Zionist expedition into the East becomes clear: it is simply one more Crusade, driven by the same strategic urge for domination as the campaigns of Godfrey of Bouillon or the planners of the Fourth Crusade, in which one Christian power looted the Christian state of Byzantium… Year after year, meanwhile, the East had strengthened its solidarity in the face of Europe.

Islam had united rather than divided it, fulfilling the hopes that Justinian and Heraclius had pursued before it. The Isaurian emperors who ruled Byzantium between 717 and 842 became iconoclasts, like the Prophet, and broke with Rome. In 867 Patriarch Photios, a distinguished scholar and man of letters, declared himself independent of the papal see, opening the schism that the crisis of 1054 would finally confirm.

By the eleventh century, Roman Catholicism had therefore taken leave of the East, the birthplace of its own spirit, and confined itself to the West. The East, gathered around Islam and the Christianity of the patriarchs, stood on the other side, with the Russian world beside it. So it was that in October 1187 one of the first rulers to congratulate Saladin on the recovery of Jerusalem was the emperor in Constantinople. There is much that could be said about the reasons why the Eastern capitals became so deeply separated from the Latin city.

It would be wrong and one-sided to blame the Western papacy alone for the division. Unfortunately, we do not possess the documents needed for a decisive judgment about responsibility, and still less the economic and political evidence that would reveal the forces working behind the scenes to create and intensify religious conflict. Our ignorance in this area is almost complete. The centuries have destroyed the fragile evidence.

Throughout its history, the Roman Church has regretted its quarrel with the East and continued, despite everything, to feel a certain nostalgia for it. Nor can we be sure that one of the motives that drove the Frankish peoples toward the Crusades — alongside the obvious strategic aims — was not a quieter desire to restore trust with the Holy Land. Christian thought had been shaken at its deepest foundations by the break with the East.

The panic surrounding the year 1000 can be explained by this enormous rupture. It gave rise to countless expressions of terror: apocalyptic obsessions, religious trances, visions, and outbreaks of mystical frenzy that continued for at least three centuries and found striking expression in the art of the period.

A true madness.

For generations, people had seen nothing like the abyss that now seemed to open beneath their feet. The stigmata of Francis of Assisi are only one bloody sign among many of the extreme pain of the medieval soul after its divorce from the East. By 904 the Holy See had become the private possession of a Tuscan family, the Theophylacts, who established in Rome a genuine “pornocracy”[^pierre-rossi-lci-012] under Theodora and her two daughters, Theodora the Younger and Marozia.

By the time the Western papacy came under the protection of Otto I of Saxony, founder of the Holy Roman Empire in 962, it had ceased to count as a spiritual power. It would take three centuries to recover — the same three centuries during which the Great Fear surrounding the year 1000 endured. And this revival came from the mystical vitality that still survived within the Church, a vitality it drew from its Arab sources. In its search for consolation, the whole of our Middle Ages lived with its eyes turned toward the East, which gradually took on a legendary and Manichaean character in the Western imagination.

The peoples of the East — Jews, Christians, Muslims, and others — were often irritated and puzzled by Europe’s attitude toward them. Yet they never quite came to regard Mediterranean Europeans as foreigners. They saw them instead as brothers through history and culture. And it is true that wars, humiliations, and conflicts between states never fully broke those bonds. A life-giving current from the East never stopped producing, on Western soil, a rich flowering of art and creative thought. We have remained Arabs in our faith as much as in our scepticism.

In Monteverdi’s *Orfeo*, where the solar divinity still hovers; in Dante’s infernal forest, where the panther prowls; and even in contemporary science, ruled by the atom and the logic of hypotheses, the continuous murmur of our Eastern sources can still be heard beneath the surface.

We only have to listen.

*King, I Wait for You in Babylon* was the title of a work once exhibited at the Museum of Modern Art in Paris, where André Malraux’s pen and Salvador Dalí’s algebraic forms came together in fourteen drypoint prints to evoke an allegorical history of the Near East. Its deserts are strewn with jewellery, and the breath that moves through them on the scale of the heavens seems powerful enough to wake the dead. To our bloodless and imprecise intelligence, to the mechanical ideas that inhabit us, to our dying common sense, the East truly offers the hard climb back toward resurrection.

Yes, life is waiting for us in Babylon…

Paris, 19 February 1975.
